- **`Servers` Round 1 added two more seams, and one of them is a spec sentence that describes a warning reaching further than it does.** *(From Round 1 closing, 2026-08-19. Both recorded rather than repaired; no closed document and no spec was edited.)*

  **The machine password's lifetime class, and two closed documents disagree.** `State, identity and recovery` §11.1 puts *"the machine password held for a connection"* in the **period-held** class, whose lifetime is the connected period ending however it ends. `Security and licence` §2 item 3b makes it **connection-held** — it ends with that connection ending, and a connection replaced under a standing period takes it with it. **`Servers` follows §2 item 3b and says so**, at S12 and Section 16 item 17, because that is the document that owns the six held things. **What is owed:** one clause in §11.1 naming the machine password as connection-held rather than period-held, or a ruling the other way. **Breaks nothing built** — the Subject states which it follows and why, so no builder is left to choose. ~~**Due: at the stage close, with the other architecture seams.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close; marked 2026-09-02.** `State, identity and recovery` §11.1 now reads *"The machine password held for a connection is connection-held, not period-held, and it left the period-held row above the moment this was written."* **`Servers` was right and §11.1 moved to it**, which is the way this entry expected it to go.

  **The disconnected-removal warning reaches less than the spec's sentence describes.** `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md` describes a removal warning for a disconnected server that holds what Smoozy last knew; `Servers` W19 establishes that the record it is composed from is period-held, so **the row is reachable only in the stretch between runs of attempts** — a server the user disconnected by hand this run has no such record and gets no warning at all. **The promise itself is kept**: the sentence that owns it is `Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md`'s, and it says a removal with no record warns about nothing whatever the reason. What went imprecise is the consequence sentence in the `Servers` spec. **It is not a Rule 7 stop and the round asked for no ruling** — the consolidator tested it against all three of `Method`'s shapes and it failed each. **Due:** the Creator, whenever they want that sentence tidied. It blocks nothing.

- **`Servers` was written owing three seams to three closed documents, and one of them is a rule written absolute that two values cannot obey.** *(From the document's filing, 2026-08-19. All three recorded rather than repaired; no closed document was edited.)*

  **The carrying rule. `State, identity and recovery` §2.3 requires every durably remembered machine-derived value to carry the installation identity it was read under, and it is written absolute.** Two values in the server row cannot: the **remembered host key**, because it is read before any connection presenting Smoozy's credential and is recorded on machines where no installation exists at all; and the **remembered setup bit**, which is not machine-derived at all — it is the user's own instruction — though §11.1 lists it beside machine-derived values. **What is owed is one clause naming the host key as outside the rule, or its refusal** — and a refusal would make Row 36's first connection unimplementable as `Security and licence` §7 already writes it.

  **The admission point and the session count are counted per different things.** `Server protocol and transfers` T5's admission point is per server; `MaxSessions` is per connection; and `Servers` 1.4 gives a period two connections at a low cap. **Nothing breaks** — the per-server point stays one point and counts against two pools, with the reserved command session on the primary alone — and what is owed is one clarifying clause in T5, or nothing at all if it is read as already permitting it.

  **The unfiltered offer.** `Queue & History` A5 does not say whether an adoption offer is made for work the current login cannot act on. `Servers` 11.3 answers it — the offer is unfiltered and no pre-check is made, because a refusal is discovered by trying — and A5 admits that reading without moving. **Nothing is owed unless a later reader wants that document to say so from its own side.**

  **And the Specs question above**, which is the item immediately before this one and is the Creator's rather than a seam.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between the named documents, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **ALL THREE CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close; marked 2026-09-02.** **The carrying rule:** `State, identity and recovery` §2.3 now names **two values in the server row as outside it, each for its own reason** — the remembered host key, read before any connection presenting Smoozy's credential, and the remembered setup bit, which is the user's own instruction and not machine-derived at all. **So Row 36's first connection stays implementable**, which a refusal would have broken. **The admission point:** `Server protocol and transfers` **T5a** gives the per-server point and the per-connection session count their different scopes. **The unfiltered offer:** `Queue & History` **A5a** states it from that document's own side, which this entry said was owed only if a later reader wanted it — a later reader did.

- **`Server Setup` was written owing three seams to closed documents, and two of them are closed enumerations that are now short.** *(From the document's filing, 2026-08-19. Recorded rather than repaired; no closed document was edited.)*

  **The two enumerations, both in `Job files and outputs`.** Its M1 manifest field list is closed and **does not name the build path**, which `Server Setup`'s Row 96 matching rule records at submission — the rule is *path beside version*, because two builds of one version answer the same version when asked, so a version number does not identify a build. And its 1.2 four-area layout has **no home for the Blender area** `Server Setup` composes as a fifth sibling under the root, which its N1 path-ownership rule also has to admit. **This is the third and fourth time a closed enumeration in that document has come up short** — `Rendering`'s D9 seam of the same day was the first pair — so the shape is worth naming as a shape rather than logged again as an incident.

  **The third is a reading rather than a contradiction.** `Server Setup` takes the dedicated Telegram entry point on a **never-prepared** machine to place the package first, which is a reading of the spec's *"adds Telegram's piece and does nothing else"* rather than something any document says. It is the honest reading — there is nothing to host a role worker otherwise — but nobody wrote it down.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between `Server Setup` and `Job files and outputs`, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **ALL THREE CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close; marked 2026-09-02.** **The two enumerations:** `Job files and outputs` **M1a** carries the build path beside the version, on the *path beside version* matching rule, and its **L6** now says **five** areas with the Blender area among them, recorded at that document's §14 item 2. **The third**, the never-prepared machine's package-first reading, is written down at `State, identity and recovery` §1.2, which carries the job record directory as a store (§14 item 3). **The shape this entry named as a shape rather than an incident** — a closed enumeration coming up short — was named again by `Render Settings` two days later and is now the seam close's most repeated repair.

- **`Server Setup` closed its Round 1 owing three more seams, all to the same closed document, and one of them is a requirement rather than a gap.** *(From the Round 1 close, 2026-08-19.)* All three were recorded rather than repaired, and `Rented-server runtime` was not edited.

  **The exception nothing carries a field for.** Its §9 P2 and §7.2 A11 are written absolute — while the machine-level setup-in-progress state stands, nothing is admitted, at machine level, with no exception named. `Server Setup` requires exactly one: **the recovery run's own worker start**, made while the state stands, because the run that takes the state over has to be admitted under it. A builder implementing A11 as written refuses that worker, the state is never cleared, and Rows 106, 112 and 114 fail together. The admission point needs one field for it.

  **The declaration with nowhere to go.** §7.1 closes an owning document's declarations at four and the contended resources at three, and `Server Setup`'s Y9 declares a fifth thing — one setup run at a time per machine — against a mechanism with no per-kind machine-level exclusion in it. Until that document gains one, the rule is Smoozy's own and enforced at the admission point by agreement rather than by a field.

  **The reap this document now requires.** §16 item 4 records, as that document's own parked Build-side fix, that the supervisor does not reap the workers it starts. `Server Setup` S5's refusal has **no route out** while a defunct group reads as live on a machine whose supervisor is up: the group cannot end because it is already gone, no restart is issued because this module issues one only as part of a step that needs it, and on a container no restart is issued at all. So the reap stops being somebody else's parked item and becomes a requirement of this document — the thing Row 114's recovery rests on in that case.

  **This seam was checked at the Architecture stage close on 2026-08-29 and this entry never said so. Noted 2026-08-30.** The report is `Audits/Architecture/Gates/Stage close 2026-08-29/Seam — Server Setup and Rented-server runtime.md`, and it is one of only two seams that close checked. **It corrected the routing note it was given** — the seam is recorded at `Rented-server runtime` §7.1's A1 item 5, not at §16 item 5 — and it establishes what breaks concretely: before the setup-in-progress state is set, and for every additive run, **the only thing between two setup runs on one machine is that fifth declaration**, which §7.2 never applies. **Whether that check covered all three of the parts below is not settled here** — it was scoped to the fifth declaration, and the exception with no field and the reap are the other two. **And it named three more documents that describe that clause as four things or omit the fifth** — `Dependency Upload & Remap` §1, `Job files and outputs` B24 and `Server protocol and transfers` — handing them to a runner rather than filing them, which is why they are written here too.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between `Server Setup` and `Rented-server runtime`, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **TWO CLOSED, THE THIRD IS BUILD'S; marked 2026-09-02.** **The exception with no field closed 2026-08-22** at the Architecture seam close: `Rented-server runtime` **A11a** reads *"One thing is admitted under it, and exactly one."* — the recovery run's own worker start, made while the machine-level state stands. **The fifth declaration closed 2026-08-29** and is recorded at `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 28: all three of the documents the stage-close seam report named as describing that clause as four things — `Dependency Upload & Remap` §1, `Job files and outputs` **B24a** and `Server protocol and transfers` — now carry the fifth. **The reap closed 2026-08-22 too, and it stopped being Build's.** `Rented-server runtime` **X9a** — *"The pair is the *gone* test and the wait is the *ended* test, and the supervisor performs the wait"* — routed from `Server Setup` §13 item 16, with `State, identity and recovery` §5.3 step 6 requiring the supervisor to wait on every worker it starts and take the ending from the wait status. **So `Server Setup` S5's refusal has a route out**: without the wait, a setup machine's S5 refuses for ever with no act inside the app that clears it. **X9 itself is unchanged** and keeps its safe-direction job, and §3.2's one-restarter rule is untouched, a wait not being a restart. *(A work list of 2026-09-02 called this unverified because §16's numbering had shifted under it; the clause was there, and this is that check corrected.)*

- **`Queue & History` closed its Round 1 owing two new seams, and one of them has no owner at all.** *(From the Round 1 close, 2026-08-19.)* Both were recorded rather than repaired, and neither closed document was edited.

  **The one with no owner. No document produces a completeness reading for an ending written by reconciliation.** `Queue & History` H7 requires that **every recorded outcome ending a job carries a completeness reading taken as a step of that ending, whoever writes the outcome** — Row 54's automatic drop and Row 83's last-frame figure both rest on it. Two of the four endings are written by reconciliation with no worker alive and no ending step run: *given up* after a second interruption, and *left stopped* written from a durable *should be stopped*. `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 17 already holds the neighbouring half — that *what the reading is* is `Queue & History`'s, and that neither document today requires it to be taken at that moment or carried with the outcome. **Routed by the architecture runner to `Rented-server runtime` §3.3**, because that document already carries the neighbouring half and M17 is the worked analogue: reconciliation writing *collection incomplete* for the captured streams. So the step is buildable and what is missing is a document to own it. **What breaks until it settles:** a job given up after two interruptions has no last-frame figure though the spec names that ending among *"stopped, failed and given up alike"*, and a waiter on such a dependency is not dropped though the spec names it. **What holds:** nothing is vouched for wrongly and nothing is dropped wrongly — the hold stands rather than dropping against absent evidence, the entry states the dependency's ending, and both answers stay offered.

  **The other. The paired stop's subject is resolved on the machine, and `State, identity and recovery` §5.3 step 1 and §5.1 as they stand do not admit it.** Step 1 has the caller submit a stop naming the target operation's identity, and §5.1 makes that a definition — a stop's subject identity *is* the operation identity it names — so a stop whose target is resolved later has no subject identity when its command record is written and no field the resolution could occupy. **Row 59's promise cannot be kept from the desktop**: the reading it composes from is period-held, and the job rendering when the clear's write lands may not be the one it named — in which case the machine the user asked to empty starts a fresh render instead, which is the outcome the spec's ordering exists to prevent. **The runner ruled on 2026-08-19 that step 1 binds**, so `Queue & History` L13–L15 state the requirement in full and the seam is recorded at its Section 18 item 16. **What is owed there:** one sentence in §5.3 carving the composite's stop out of step 1, and one in §5.1 permitting a stop command record whose subject identity is written by the supervisor at resolution — **or the refusal of both**, which sends Row 59 back to a promise nothing can keep and makes it `Method`'s first escalation.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between the named documents, or at the Architecture stage close, whichever is first.~~ **BOTH CLOSED; marked 2026-09-02.** **The one with no owner** closed at `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 17, which now records that `Queue & History` **H7** states the completeness requirement in full and in the width this entry said no document reached — so the step is owned and the two reconciliation-written endings carry their reading. **The paired stop's subject** closed at `State, identity and recovery` §5.3 step 1, which now carries **one carve-out, routed from `Queue & History`**, permitting a stop whose subject identity the supervisor writes at resolution. **So Row 59's promise is keepable and `Method`'s first escalation was not reached**, which was the other branch this entry named.

- **`Rendering` closed its Round 1 owing two new seams, both to the same closed document, and one of them is a closed field list that is now short.** *(From the Round 1 close, 2026-08-19.)* Both were recorded rather than repaired, and no closed document was edited.

  **The heavier one. `Job files and outputs` D9 says the launch material carries *"exactly this, and nothing that could be an argument instead"* — a closed list — and `Rendering` L10 hands that material more than the list names.** The settings pairs and Simplify's four values have no command-line argument in the version the job runs, so the material is the only place they can be delivered, which is what Row 22 requires. **D9's list is the one that is short**, not L10's material, and the line it owes is one sentence in a document whose round is closed. Until it is written, a builder reading D9 as the closed list it says it is composes a material without the settings pairs, and the render runs at the file's own values at success — silent, and reaching Rows 6, 13 and 22 at once. Recorded at `Rendering` §9 item 18.

  **The lighter one. `Job files and outputs` C18 says the reckoning's list is *the previous attempt's* frame list, and an attempt that ended at a hold or failed before the render instruction writes no such list.** `Rendering` P1 step 6 resolves it — the reckoning reads the latest attempt record that carries one, and walks past those that do not — and now cites C18 where before it did not. The resolution is sound and it is still a clarification of a closed document's clause made outside that document. Recorded at `Rendering` §9 item 17.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between `Rendering` and `Job files and outputs`, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **BOTH CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close; marked 2026-09-02.** **The heavier one:** `Job files and outputs` **D9a** states that *nothing that could be an argument instead* is a rule about **route** rather than a ceiling on the list — so a builder composing launch material from D9 no longer leaves the settings pairs and Simplify's four values out, and Rows 6, 13 and 22 are safe. **The lighter one:** **C18a** states the walk-back rule from that document's own side, so `Rendering` P1 step 6 is no longer clarifying a closed clause from outside it.

- **Two renders observed in Blender 4.5 print a message and then never end, and a paid machine keeps billing with no signal of any kind.** *(Raised as a finding by Round 1 of `Queue & History` on 2026-08-19, ruled a **want** by the consolidator and dropped by that word, so it is weighed here on its own merits.)* They read as live work because they are live processes; `Queue & History` E5 records the observed cases and does not manufacture an ending for them, and the idle machinery never fires while a render operation is running. **Every promise is kept** — no spec says Smoozy notices a render whose readings have stopped moving — and the stop is the user's route, which E1's rule already gives them.

  **What the want asks for** is a new promise: that Smoozy says something when a render's readings stop advancing for long enough. **What it would cost** is a judgment about how long is long enough, on a machine where a heavy frame legitimately prints nothing for a long time — and a wrong judgment interrupts a paying render or cries wolf. **What holds without it:** the user sees the entry sitting still, the machine's own idle message never fires because the render is live, and stopping it is one action they already have.

  **Due: fired and serviced on 2026-08-19, and the want itself stands.** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` is written and K14 is the ending classification the Due named. **K15 answers the question the Due actually asked — whether an ending that never arrives is one of the classification's cases — and the answer is no, deliberately**: such runs are live processes, they read as live work, the entry stays *rendering* while the group validates, and **no timer of Smoozy's ends a render or manufactures an ending for one**, because a legitimate render has no bound and a wrong judgment interrupts a paying render. K16 states what bounds the cost: the classification refuses to hide it, the user sees an entry whose readings have stopped moving, the idle machinery correctly never fires while the render operation runs, and the stop is one action they already have.

  **That document checked the want against `Method`'s first escalation and read it as the want it was ruled to be** — no spec promises that Smoozy notices a render whose readings have stopped moving, so no promise is failing — and its Section 10 records the check. **What is left here is the want alone**, unchanged: it asks for a new promise, and granting it costs a judgment about how long is long enough on a machine where a heavy frame legitimately prints nothing for a long time.

  **The want now carries three arguments rather than two, and all three are about the cost rather than about the promise.** *(The third recorded 2026-09-02; the second was recorded 2026-09-01 with `Rendering` K15b.)* **One:** a render that never exits bills by the hour with nothing said. **Two:** it can start by itself after a reboot, as a machine-initiated re-run, so it is a render the user did not choose to leave running and may have nobody connected to it. **Three, found 2026-09-02:** the cost is not one job's night. `Queue & History` Q4 makes the render kind hold the cards **exclusively** and fires the admission point on one trigger only — the previous operation's recorded outcome being written — and a render that never ends never writes one, **so every job queued behind it never starts.** The queue shows them waiting, correctly; the idle machinery correctly never fires; every promise is still kept; and the bill is scaled by the length of the line rather than by the one job. It compounds with the second rather than replacing it. `Rendering` **K16** carries the same argument, written there the same day, and that document's decisions record carries it too.

  **Due, re-pointed:** the Creator, whenever they want to weigh it. **No document owes it anything and nothing is waiting on it.** **It stays the Creator's to grant**: nothing has been built for it, K15's answer has not moved, and no watcher, threshold or judgment about how long is long enough exists anywhere.

- **`Dependency Upload & Remap` closed its Round 1 owing five seams to four other architecture documents, and one of them is new.** *(From the Round 1 close, 2026-08-19.)* Each was recorded rather than repaired, because in every case the fact's one home is the other document's.

  **The new one, opened by the round itself.** How a recorded location's **bytes** cross from the machine-side reading to the desktop is not stated anywhere, and it is asked of `Job files and outputs` R1's reading shape. The joined Blender answer states the exposure outright: *"reading a location out can fail where reading anything else out would not, because a location is exempt from the rule that a file's text is all written one way — real disks hold names that are not"* (*Dependencies 9*). **It breaks both ways and both silently**: a reading that raises rather than carrying the bytes makes a file *could not be read* with the version named, permanently, for a file Blender opens perfectly well — and the user is told something untrue about the version; a reading that substitutes or skips the offending bytes keys the wrong record, compares the wrong bytes, mirrors a wrong spelling and matches no pair at the remap, so the asset is silently missing at render. The desktop end is byte-disciplined throughout and `Job files and outputs` N15 already carries the neighbouring discipline for listings; **R1's payload is the half that is owed.**

  **The four already standing when the round opened.** The uploaded cache or bake read against `Job files and outputs` D20, where **two Creator rulings of the same day name one setting with opposite values** — pointed under the job's folder the render recomputes the simulation and reports success with the uploaded bake never read; pointed at the mirror the render writes into the asset area, against L7 and L8. Until it is ruled the material carries both pairs so the ruling changes a selection rule and not the material's shape. The local record's store inventory, which is the entry in *Parked* below and is where the tally of how many documents have found it short is kept. The stored-name reading, asked to live in `Desktop app and delivery`'s platform file adapter rather than becoming a second route to the filesystem, which F10 forbids by name. And `Rented-server runtime`'s closed worker-kind set, which houses neither the contents reading nor the fixing; the declarations are written to stand under whichever housing is settled.

  ~~**Due:** as a seam round between the named documents, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **ALL FIVE CLOSED; marked 2026-09-02.** **The new one** — how a recorded location's bytes cross — is `Job files and outputs` **R4a**, added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close: *"a recorded location crosses as bytes, and no reading of one may raise, substitute or skip"*, which closes both silent failures this entry named. **The store inventory** is `State, identity and recovery` §1.2. **The stored-name reading** is `Desktop app and delivery` §7.4 **C15**, which `Dependency Upload & Remap` **F13** already records as carrying it — so it lives in the platform file adapter and is not a second route to the filesystem. **The worker-kind housing** was filled at the same close, in `Rented-server runtime` **W1**'s three *not yet stated* entries. **The uploaded cache or bake** — the one this entry said could become a Creator item — is `Job files and outputs` **D21b** to **D21g**, written 2026-08-21 to 2026-08-31; **it did not become a Creator item**, because what resolves it is a selection rule and not a change to what the user gets.

- **A rented machine whose disk fills stops its supervisor, and on a systemd machine nothing on that machine ever starts it again.** Observed on Batch E, 2026-08-18: with the state mount at a true zero the supervisor cannot start at all — its first act needs a directory entry — the service manager's start limiter gives up, and the unit **does not recover when the space is freed**. One plain start command recovers it completely, and nothing on the machine performs one. On a container the keeper loops and heals by itself. **Two clauses were struck on 2026-08-20 and this entry was not told until 2026-08-22.** *Stays failed across a reboot* was never posed — the one reboot performed was with the disk still full — and *ten attempts in about two seconds* does not match systemd's documented default of five starts in ten seconds (`systemd-system.conf(5)`), so it wants re-reading against the raw record before it is repeated. **And the limiter is not a property of the machine kind**: `StartLimitIntervalSec=` and `StartLimitBurst=` are unit settings the placement itself writes, so the asymmetry between the kinds is a consequence of the unit this project left at its defaults rather than of systemd. `Preparation/Architecture/Rented-server runtime.md` Section 16 item 12 carries what breaks and what holds and **deliberately does not answer it**, because a second restarter would be a second restarter beside the one §3.2 exists to have exactly one of.

  **Half of this was answered on 2026-08-25, on the Pile 3 rental, and the half that was answered is the other one.** A supervisor arrangement **already running** when its mount fills **survives** — same process, same run identifier, unbroken heartbeat, reads truthful, writes refused loudly and moving nothing, and the pending write accepted the instant space returned. **That is the container kind only**, this rental having been one; **the systemd start case above is untouched and stays open.** `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 25 carries the answer. **And the run turned up a hazard nobody had posed**, now item 26 in the same section: on a full mount an append to an already-open file goes on succeeding until that file's last block is exactly full, so *the disk is full* and *the journal refuses* are not the same moment — which is also why this entry's Batch E observation and the new one disagree without either being wrong.

  **What is already established.** Every refused write refused loudly with errno 28 and nothing was silently accepted; every read stayed truthful including free space as zero; the held job stayed held throughout. **The one thing nobody can see is the ending**: the arrangement's own death is recorded nowhere Smoozy writes, the machine's own log had no entries for that boot, and from outside it is indistinguishable from a machine that is briefly busy.

  **Checked against all three of `Method`'s escalations and it reaches none** — the user meets the supervisor-unavailable condition `Desktop app and delivery` already presents, with the disk-full carve-out `Whole app` already grants as the diagnosis. **What is new is a condition the automatic repair trigger must be able to act in.**

  **Half answered on 2026-08-18, by `Desktop app and delivery` — which was the first of the two to be written.** Its Section 12 answers **the reaching half**: the automatic repair trigger fires at **every command-path attempt that finds the supervisor still silent**, rides on the pre-package surface, which reaches a machine whose supervisor is dead, and is **not consumed by a failed attempt** — so a user who frees the space through Smoozy gets the repair on the next attempt, with no watcher and no timer. **What it cannot answer is what the machine does in that condition** — starting a supervisor whose own restarter has given up is `Rented-server runtime` §2's, and a second automatic restarter would be the second restarter that document's §3.2 exists to prevent. That document's Section 16 item 12 names this trigger and deliberately stops there; the desktop document's own unresolved list records the gap with what breaks.

  **The machine half is answered on 2026-08-19, by `Server Setup` — the second of the two to be written, and the document `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 12 named as the owner.** Its user-facing fallback re-run issues **the plain start of the registered service over the command channel**, where package inspection finds the package healthy and the supervisor dead. That is the one plain start command Batch E observed recovering the machine completely, performed by a person's act rather than by a second restarter — which is what keeps `Rented-server runtime` §3.2's one-restarter rule intact.

  **What is left is one line and it is `Desktop app and delivery`'s.** The automatic route still cannot start a verified-package supervisor: its request answers *verified* and acts on nothing. Whether that document routes its own trigger through the same start step is a decision inside it. ~~**Due, narrowed again:** that one line, as a seam between those two documents or at the Architecture stage close.~~ **It blocks nothing being written now**, and the user-facing route out of the condition now exists.

  **THE TRIGGER FIRED AND THE LINE IS STILL OWED. Marked 2026-09-04, at Build's step 6 close, and confirmed by opening the document rather than on a claim.** The Architecture stage close ran on 2026-09-02 and this Due was never marked. `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` §22 item 15 still records it open in as many words — *"what it does not answer is what the machine does with it in that condition"* — so the automatic route still answers *verified* and acts on nothing. **What has changed since is that the case is narrower than it was**: V6 was narrowed on 2026-08-31, and a supervisor already running lives through its mount filling, so the trigger never fires in the ordinary shape of this case. **What holds:** the user-facing re-run is built and is the route out, at `App/Server Setup/src/fallback.rs` step 3. ~~**Due, re-pointed:** whenever `Desktop app and delivery` §12 is next opened. It blocks nothing.~~ **CLOSED 2026-10-16 as permanently unresolved, on the build runner's ruling R6 (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The runner's rulings.md`), marked by owed piece 9 of Build step 10c.** The automatic trigger does not start a supervisor whose restarter gave up. `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 15 now says so, with what breaks: a running supervisor lives through a full disk, so the case is narrow; the user-facing re-run (`App/Server Setup/src/fallback.rs`) is the way out; and a second automatic start cannot be proved without a rental.

- **Whether the three system web views actually break lines where the typography rules say they will, checked at Build.** `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` applies both typography rules as **character substitutions** rather than as layout instructions, precisely so nothing depends on a stylesheet feature — and X6 says outright that nothing there claims what any renderer does with the character once it is in the string. Section 22 item 19 records the gap with what breaks: a line that breaks where Design said it would not, on one of three engines. **Raised as a want by Round 1 of that document on 2026-08-18**, dropped from the Verdict by that word and parked here to be weighed on its own merits.

  **What the want asks for** is that checking the three engines' handling becomes a verification point at Build rather than a thing anybody hopes about. **What it costs** is one pass over the built interface on three machines. ~~two of which this project does not have.~~ **Corrected 2026-08-30: that clause was false for one of the two and misleading for the other.** **A Windows machine is rentable** — a Kamatera account, used four times and worked on three, described in the equipment section of the [Blender Research method](../Blender%20Research/Method.md) — **but every machine it has produced boots a Server edition**, and whether a Server edition carries the same system web view as a desktop Windows is not established, so the Windows pass is **untried on the reachable edition and unreachable on the one the user will actually run**. **Linux is a rented machine with a display server**, which this project has had several of since 2026-08-26 — but every one drew in software, which is the one machine property that bears on how anything is laid out, so a line-breaking result from one is worth less than it looks. **What holds without it:** the substitution travels with the text into every surface including native ones, which is the whole reason it was chosen over a layout rule.

  **Due:** at Build, when the interface is drawn. It blocks nothing. **What changed is the reason, not the answer** — the pass is untried rather than impossible, and the machine question now splits three ways rather than two.

- **The supervisor never reaps the workers it starts, so a dead group reads as live.** Observed on Batch E, 2026-08-18, on the throwaway rather than on any planned arrangement: a killed worker persists as a defunct entry whose start-time field still reads what was recorded, **so the recorded execution-group pair goes on validating for a process that no longer exists.** `Rented-server runtime` X9 reads *a pair that no longer validates* as *the group has ended*, and that never fires for a dead worker while its supervisor lives. **It errs in the safe direction** — reconciliation waits where it might have acted, never the reverse — and it bit two separate tests on that box, blocking the identifier-reuse demonstration and reporting a dead setup group as live until a reboot cleared it.

  **It is a property of how the supervisor is built rather than of the pairing mechanism**, which is why it is parked rather than repaired: `Rented-server runtime` Section 16 item 4 names it and says what closes it — the supervisor reaping the workers it starts.

  ~~**Due:** at Build, when the supervisor is written. Named here so it is not discovered there.~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, and it stopped being Build's; marked 2026-09-02.** `Rented-server runtime` **X9a** states it from that document's own side — *"The pair is the *gone* test and the wait is the *ended* test, and the supervisor performs the wait"* — and `State, identity and recovery` §5.3 step 6 requires the supervisor to **wait on every worker it starts and take the ending from the wait status**. **So the defunct-entry hole is closed in the architecture rather than left for a builder to find**: without the wait, a killed worker goes on validating, a termination is absorbed by a defunct entry, and on a setup machine `Server Setup` S5 refuses for ever with no act inside the app that clears it. **X9 is unchanged** and keeps its safe-direction job; the wait adds the positive that a group has **ended**. §3.2's one-restarter rule is untouched — a wait is not a restart. *(That document's §16 item 4 now records the close in place. A work list of 2026-09-02 read the shifted numbering as evidence the reap was unverified; the clause was there.)*

- **Two documents owe a reading at a moment neither of them names, and a hold's release turns on it.** `Preparation/Architecture/Rented-server runtime.md` U19 fixes the moment a hold's release predicate is evaluated, and U30 fixes that the reading it evaluates is **taken by the dependency job's own worker as a step of that job's ending and carried with the outcome it reports** — because `State, identity and recovery` §1.3 rule 5 leaves the supervisor nothing to read afterwards. **What the reading is** stays `Queue & History`'s Row 54, with `Job files and outputs` Section 10's completeness statement behind it, and **neither document today requires it to be taken at that moment or carried with the outcome**. **Raised by Round 1 of `Rented-server runtime` on 2026-08-18 and recorded as a seam rather than repaired**, because the predicate's one home is the other document's.

  **What breaks if it is not carried:** Row 275's negative case either never fires, leaving a hold standing while every package commit behind it is blocked, or fires against a reading taken before the render — which drops a waiting job whose first frame did arrive, irreversibly, since a hold record is written once.

  ~~**Due:** when `Queue & History`'s architecture document is written, or at the Architecture stage close where the seams between architecture documents are checked, whichever is first.~~ **CLOSED; marked 2026-09-02.** `Queue & History` **H7** states the requirement in full and in the width this entry said no document reached — **every recorded outcome ending a job carries a completeness reading taken as a step of that ending, whoever writes the outcome** — and `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 17 records that it does. **So the two endings written by reconciliation, with no worker alive and no ending step run, are covered**: *given up* after a second interruption, and *left stopped* written from a durable *should be stopped*. **Row 275's negative case can therefore fire**, and it fires against a reading taken at the right moment rather than one taken before the render — which is the irreversible harm this entry named, a waiting job whose first frame did arrive being dropped for good.

- **A second Access question is with the Creator as of 2026-08-17, and it comes out of the retirement exception they ruled that day.** The spec promises that at three days without a successful check Smoozy says so. After a planned retirement, a copy holding a permission with no end date is **forever** more than three days past its last successful check, on a licence working exactly as promised. Showing the notice puts a permanent complaint in front of somebody whose licence is fine; suppressing it withholds something the spec promises. The exception's *nothing is shown to them* is tied to the permission's **arrival** and says nothing about the count afterwards, and `Licence service` §12 answers only the lock. **Found by the required re-sweep of the changed spec**, not by the round that made the change.

  **Due:** the Creator's ruling, then one sentence in `Preparation/Specs/Access.md` and the Rule 7 line. It blocks nothing being written now.


  Superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04; recorded 2026-10-26. Paid-layer work is withdrawn. Earlier rulings and closures remain as history.

- **The shape now has three filesystem and ready-action facts that must follow its concrete operations.** Fact 78 covers package pointer-and-registration recovery and server-side final-name publication on a rented machine. Fact 79 covers final-name publication for downloads on each of Mac, Windows and Linux. Fact 80 keeps the separate question of whether the chosen driver operation in the ready action actually requires a restart; Fact 51 already answers that Smoozy can perform such a restart over its held connection.

  **Due:** when the package, publication and ready-action operations are concrete. Ride Fact 78 and Fact 80 on the first suitable rental without adding one; document and run Fact 79 on Mac and Linux, and leave its Windows third explicitly unposable until a Windows computer exists. Carry the answers into `Server protocol and transfers`, `Desktop app and delivery`, `Rented-server runtime`, and `Server Setup` before those documents harden the affected claims.

  **Two of the three are done as of 2026-08-18 and Fact 79 is what remains.** **Fact 78 is answered** — the boundaries were interrupted by real forcible kills at each of the four commit points on **ext4, xfs and overlayfs**, and none of the four harms occurred on any of them; carried into `Rented-server runtime` Section 16 item 3, which names the one boundary that could not be posed and two smaller things it does not close. **Fact 80 is answered** and its rule is carried in that document's preamble: run the check after installing and restart only if it fails, never on the package manager's word. **Fact 79 is where this item now lives** — the Mac documentation route is answered from Apple's own manual, Linux stands through Fact 78, **and the throwaway route is untouched on all three systems because the publication operation has not been chosen.** Windows is unread on both routes. **A Windows computer exists** — a Kamatera rental since 2026-08-26 — so this is untried rather than untestable; but **every machine that account has produced booted a Server edition**, so whatever turns on a desktop edition is still out of reach. *(Corrected 2026-08-30.)*

  **The operation is now concrete, 2026-08-18.** `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` 6.5 chooses it: **the platform's atomic rename of the staged file onto the final path, staged on the same filesystem** — `rename(2)` on macOS and on Linux, **Windows unread on both routes**. That section states what would demonstrate it — repeated forcible kills at the publication boundary, reading both paths after each — and the exact negative result, and says outright it is **not claimed demonstrated**. **The Linux run wants a desktop Linux computer**, which this project does not have; a rented Linux server does not answer for it.

  ~~**Due, narrowed again:** the throwaway itself, on a Mac now and on a Linux desktop computer if one appears; its Windows third stays explicitly unposable.~~ **MARKED 2026-09-02, at the Architecture stage close, and its three thirds part company.** **The Windows third can never fire** — it is already explicitly unposable, every machine this account produces boots a Server edition, and the Creator ruled on 2026-08-31 that there will be no further rented machine for answering a question, so **it is permanently unresolved rather than waiting.** **The Linux desktop third can never fire either**: it wants a desktop Linux computer, this project has not got one, a rented Linux server does not answer for it, and no rental is coming. **The Mac third is the only reachable one** — the publication operation is now chosen, `Desktop app and delivery` 6.5 having taken the platform's atomic rename of a staged file onto the final path, staged on the same filesystem — and **it stays a hand test on the Creator's own Mac rather than an agent's work.** **What breaks, written down:** 6.5 states outright that the operation is **not claimed demonstrated**, so the download-publication promise rests on the operation's own documentation rather than on repeated forcible kills at the boundary, on all three systems and permanently on two of them. **What holds:** the Mac route is answered from Apple's own manual and Linux stands through Fact 78, which was interrupted by real forcible kills at each of four commit points on ext4, xfs and overlayfs with none of the four harms occurring. It is left here in full so what it costs stays visible.

- **The Telegram webhook fact now returns to the deployment shape.** Round 2 on `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md` found that a positive Fact 63 answer does more than settle Telegram's receiving method: it permits an inbound, internet-reachable endpoint on the one machine currently holding the registered webhook. No other prepared server binds a listener and no rental becomes ineligible. What the paragraph below still owes is only for a webhook, and none is built.

  **Fact 63 was answered on 2026-08-16, and the answer is not the shape it was written for.** A webhook works and needs nothing bought — the earlier Hyperstack box registered one and Telegram genuinely connected to a self-signed certificate on a bare address. But **a URL naming a Vast container's own mapped port is refused**: the provider assigns the inbound port from its own high range, Telegram accepts only 80, 88, 443 and 8443, and it refuses everything else in as many words. *(Narrowed 2026-08-21 — this said *cannot host one at any price*; Telegram's page constrains the URL, not the machine, and whether a relay can be put in front of such a container is not settled and nothing needs it.)* So a webhook is **a provider-dependent extra, not something Smoozy can rely on**, and the receiving call is the route that has to work everywhere. **The shape needs no correction, and that was checked rather than assumed.** Its deployment paragraph already says the webhook route is available only where a machine can host the endpoint, that the receiving slot remains the route where none can, and that no rental becomes ineligible either way — which is exactly what the answer turned out to be. **The carry into `Security and licence` is done, 2026-08-16.** That document's Section 1.3 writes the boundary knowing a webhook is a provider-dependent extra, so the ordinary case is that **no rented server binds an inbound endpoint at all**, and its Round 1 close gate checked it. See `Preparation/Architecture/One box — the Telegram capture and the port that cannot be chosen.md`. **Still owed on the fact itself**, and only where a webhook is possible at all: the POST wrapper, its secret-token header, and the retry cadence, which that document carries as its own unresolved item 12 and `Telegram` inherits.

- **Whether one frame's writes can overlap the next frame's first write is not established, and the whole of `Download`'s live finished-file judgement rests on their not overlapping.** *(From `Download` Round 1, 2026-08-20.)* `Preparation/Architecture/Download.md` J1 states that the first write belonging to a later frame comes after every write of the earlier frame is complete, and J3 judges a frame finished on exactly that. **The three observations under it are real and generously observed — the frame order, each frame's files written as that frame finishes, the placeholder appearing at the instant the previous frame's picture completes — but the no-overlap conclusion is a derivation from them and nothing tested for it.** The joined answer `Preparation/Blender Research/Across topics/While a render runs.md` names the limit itself: the evidence rests on a folder watched at intervals, and a no-overlap rule is a statement about the instants between samples. **What breaks if it comes out the other way is the one thing in that document that fetches wrong rather than late** — a frame judged finished with a write still pending is fetched torn, recorded *fetched*, and never fetched again. **The document carries it as an unresolved item rather than hiding it**, and it is not a stop: both mechanisms still work in every case where writes do not overlap.

  **The question, in the shape the research would need:** on a windowless Linux render, in each of the four versions, does any write belonging to frame N ever occur after the first write belonging to frame N+1 — with a file output node present, with Cache Result on, with multi-layer or multi-view output, and watched at an interval short enough to see it?

  **ANSWERED, and the answer is the favourable one. *Writing 14*, 2026-08-30.** **Every write belonging to one frame is finished before any write belonging to the next frame begins** — all four versions, on the Creator's Mac and on a rented Linux machine whose four Blenders matched the Mac's build hash, with no window and again with Blender's own window open and the render started the way the menu item starts it. **The negative was made believable rather than assumed**: the write was slowed on purpose to about 144 megabytes a frame and the folder read every two milliseconds, catching sixty-three readings inside one write on the Mac and thirty-one on Linux, and on Linux every file call was traced thread by thread with no polling rate at all. **Four things underneath it bear on `Download` directly**: the file is complete at the moment Blender announces it written; nothing is ever written under a temporary name and no rename appears anywhere in the trace; a frame's other files — a File Output node's file, a preview, a second view, Cycles' tile cache — all fall inside that frame's own turn; and **frames given as a list are written in the order given, not in numeric order.**

  **Two bounds travel with it.** The system-call trace **settles Linux and says nothing about macOS**, which matters only for the desktop side. And where two events fell inside one two-millisecond reading nothing is claimed about which came first, so the Mac's tile-cache ordering is the looser statement.

  ~~**Due: now, at Architecture.** `Preparation/Architecture/Download.md` J1 and J3 are supported and should be re-read against the answer rather than left standing on a derivation.~~ **DONE 2026-09-01; marked 2026-09-02.** `Download` §14 item 17 was rewritten that day, so J1 and J3 stand on the answer rather than on a derivation. The answer is at question 14 of `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Writing.md`. **And the out-of-order frame list this entry calls "the part nothing anticipated" was already there** — `Download` line 177 reads *"a typed list in the order written"*, so no judgement in that document assumes ascending frames.

- **Three Blender questions the verification round named and stopped at, all wanting a fresh blind researcher and none of them answerable by anybody who has read this project.** *(Filed 2026-08-21 by the architecture runner from `Verified — raw-queue-rendering.md` and `Verified — raw-manuals.md`. Each is written here as the shape of the question only; the sheets must be written blind, and in particular **the researcher must not be shown this project's own readings**, or the findings come back as echoes.)*

  **1. What naming one GPU render mode rather than the other costs or saves, at scale.** For the same file, the same sample count and the same card, what does naming one at launch cost against naming the other, and does the answer change with scene size? **This project holds one pair of readings already** — one small scene on a machine that was shared with another test while they ran, where the mode usually assumed slower was the faster — and Fact 107 is re-posed on the narrower gap: a scene big enough that kernel-build time is not a large share of it, on an uncontended machine. **The general claim is the researcher's; the measurement is a rental's and rides free on any batch with such a card.** **Blocks nothing** — `Rendering` L23's chosen value is untouched either way, and the mode also changes the picture, which is a second reason not to probe it per machine.

  **2. Whether a background render's progress arrives at a reader on a pipe while the render is running, or only when it ends.** Posed as something to watch rather than something to read, with arrival times recorded. **Why it is asked:** a stream on a pipe is fully buffered by the standard, which this project has now observed on its own Mac, so a render whose progress sat in a buffer would look exactly like a render that prints nothing at all. **No rental** — one machine with a card is enough. **Blocks nothing:** `Queue & History` R4 now names the case, no clause infers an ending from silence, and what is at risk is liveness of a display rather than any state.

  **3. Which unpack tool Blender's own archives need.** Carried from `Verified — raw-manuals.md`. **Blocks nothing now.**

  **A fourth was named and deliberately not sent:** whether the device argument has a form that names a single card. Nothing in this project depends on it — the promise is every card, one render owns the machine, and no row asks for per-card selection — so `Rendering` L21 records that no per-card form is established either way and the question stops there.

  **ALL THREE ARE ANSWERED, 2026-08-30, and the first is the heaviest single question that round carried.**

  **1 is *What a render demands 7*, run on the only machine this project has ever rented that was checked to be uncontended rather than assumed to be** — an RTX 3080 in Austria, the card idle and the whole processor free before anything ran, three repeats of every measurement. **The answer changes direction with the size of the scene, sharply.** At 322 triangles the two methods are within a tenth of a second of each other. At 10,485,762 triangles one is about four times faster. At 125,829,122 triangles **the choice decides whether there is a render at all** — every run of one finished and wrote its frame in all four versions, every run of the other was killed by the system part-way through building its structure, with no frame written. **And the choice changes the picture**, in every version on both machines, against a same-method floor measured over 120 pairs. **Two bounds:** the huge-scene runs hit the **host's** memory ceiling and not the card's, so the fall-back the manuals describe was never exercised there; and the long noise-floor measurement is macOS and Apple's graphics alone, so the NVIDIA rows rest on a floor measured once.

  **2 is *Reports 14*, and it splits where nobody expected.** **A render's own printed lines reach a pipe exactly as they are produced**, in dozens of separate arrivals across a run, in all four versions on both machines — a pipe is no different from a terminal for them, and a plain file grows at each frame boundary. **A Python script's own printed lines reach neither a pipe nor a file until the run ends**, all at once, though behind a terminal they tick once a second. **So what decides the timing is which part of Blender did the printing, not whether anybody is watching.** The two switches that fix it are named. The reader was proved able to show both patterns before anything was concluded from it.

  **3 is *Builds 10*, and it is sharper than the question sounded.** **A tar program on its own does not open a Blender archive** — on a machine with no `xz` all four refused, at exit code 2, with nothing landing on disk, and no version's sources say so. One further package makes all four open silently. **7-zip breaks the build in all four versions and says it worked**: the runnable mark is gone and every symbolic link has become a plain file, with no warning of any kind. And **the line between opening the package and running what comes out is real and wide** — the unpacked Blender named nine libraries the bare machine did not have.

  ~~**Due: now, at Architecture.** The answers are at question 7 of `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/What a render demands.md`, question 14 of `Reports.md`, and question 10 of `Builds.md`. `Rendering` L23's chosen mode, `Queue & History` R4's silence case, and `Server Setup`'s unpacking route should each be read against them.~~ **DONE, marked 2026-10-16 by owed piece 9 of Build step 10c, from `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Open Threads' Due now and the board's owed lines — what is still owed, in pieces.md`.** Setup installs `xz-utils` (`App/Server Setup/src/ready_action.rs` line 557, `elevated.rs` line 235); the card mode is OptiX on the Creator's ruling; `Queue & History` R4 names the silence case. **The fourth question stays unsent** — *What a render demands 7* establishes that the technology is chosen and the devices under it are ticked, which is not the same as a form naming a single card, and nothing here settles it.

- **Two more Blender questions the Architecture stage numbered and cannot answer, both wanting a fresh blind researcher and neither wanting a rental.** *(Filed 2026-08-23 by the architecture runner. Written here as the shape of the question only; the sheets must be written blind, and in particular **the researcher must not be shown this project's own readings or what the plan hopes the answer is**.)*

  **1. Whether an ordinary save over a file leaves an untouched reference's recorded bytes exactly as they were.** **Fact 128**, exposed by the fresh reader's pass over `Dependency Upload & Remap` on 2026-08-23. **This is the sharpest open Blender question the stage holds and it is not a liveness question:** that document's M4 composes every remap pair on the recorded location's expected bytes as the check read them, and M8 matches on those bytes across exactly the re-upload the specs promise. **If a plain save re-spells references, every pair of every re-uploaded file misses at once, all are reported *not applied*, the render finishes, and it reports success.** The document carries it as §12 item 22 with what breaks written down, and M8 no longer states the narrow *reference re-pointed* reading as established. **Blocks nothing today; it is the one silent wrong render this stage knows about and cannot close.**

  **2. What Blender's sample-count setting accepts, and what it does with a number outside that.** **Fact 127**, exposed by the Rows 435–438 implementer on 2026-08-23. The map names no question that establishes it; *Settings 15* answers the same shape for the **percentage** setting, and carrying that across to a different setting is the assembling the Architecture method forbids. `Render Settings` J11 invents no range, so a typed count outside whatever Blender accepts could be silently corrected and rendered at a count nobody asked for, at success. **Blocks nothing** — J11's other two refusals and the whole of J12 rest on questions already answered.

  **BOTH ARE ANSWERED, 2026-08-30. Fact 128 is *Dependencies 20* and has its own item at the top of this section. Fact 127 is *Settings 18*, and the thing J11 was written to guard against is exactly what Blender does.**

  **A sample count outside the accepted range is silently corrected to the nearest limit, nothing whatever is reported, and the corrected number is what the render works to.** Ask Cycles for 99,999,999 and it stores 16,777,216 and settles in to render that many — no error, no warning, no line of any kind, in all four versions on two machines. **The only place the corrected figure appears anywhere is the ordinary progress line**, as the number after the slash. **Each count is corrected against its own limits**, so the same number is cut for one engine and taken whole by another. **A value that is not a whole number — a decimal, text, or nothing at all — is refused outright and loudly**, and so is a number too big to be a whole number, which is refused rather than corrected. The control that makes the silence a real result was run: the same runs made one deliberately wrong assignment and it was refused loudly, so the output was being captured.

  **The ranges themselves are now on paper**, count by count, with what each starts at — Cycles' final render takes 1 to 16,777,216. **Every one of these results came off a Blender started with no window and driven by a script handed in at launch, which is exactly how Smoozy renders.**

  ~~**Due: now, at Architecture.**~~ **DONE 2026-08-30; marked 2026-09-02.** `Render Settings` **J11 item 3 was rewritten on the answer that day**, with the range per engine and the correction-versus-refusal boundary stated exactly — so the decision this item said it unblocked was taken the same day the answer landed, and the *Due: now* has been spent ever since. The answer is at question 18 of `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Settings.md`. **What is not established stays named in J11:** a driver or a keyframe carrying a count out of range, a file loaded holding one, and what a field does when a number is typed or dragged.

- **Blender 5.2 carries two view-layer denoising settings the other three versions have not got, and nothing has a home for them.** Found in this round's hand-test evidence while answering **Settings 16**, and left out of the comparison on purpose: they are about denoising rather than about what decides a sample count, so they sit outside that question and outside the comparer's remit. They are a real four-version difference all the same, and this project has no denoising question anywhere.

  ~~**Due:** next time a researcher-runner picks up **Settings** or opens a topic where denoising would sit — as a question of its own with its own number, since nothing already numbered covers it.~~ **ANSWERED as *Settings 19*, 2026-08-30 — it got its own number and was asked, which is exactly what this item said was needed.**

  **The two settings are real and 5.2 has them, and four other differences came with them.** A view layer carries two denoising settings in 4.5, 5.0 and 5.1 and **four in 5.2**, the extra pair being one about following reflections and one about weighting the albedo by roughness, both starting on and both drawn only when two developer preferences are on — though **the preference governs only whether they are drawn**, and both read back and accept a value with it off. **What either does to a picture was never measured.**

  **Three things beside them matter more to this project than the pair that raised the item.** **5.2 stores five denoising feature passes where the other three store three, and 5.1 orders its three differently from 4.5 and 5.0** — so **anything picking a pass out by its position rather than by its name will pick the wrong one**, which reaches whatever reads inside a multi-layer file. **The compositor's Denoise node carries a quality of its own in 4.5 and none from 5.0 on**, so the condition every manual attaches to the two scene-level quality settings describes something only 4.5 has. And **a file naming a denoiser the render machine has not got still renders, saves, and is still denoised**, with the warning appearing only when a script reads the setting and never in the render's own output.

  **One result was deliberately bounded and is the item below this one:** on one card on one driver, 5.2.1 could not use one rendering path where the other three versions could.

Answered 2026-08-30.)*

- **Both rounds of research were carried into the specs on 2026-08-10, and this whole section is what that closed.** Ten documents were swept in one sitting — What a job writes, Dependency Upload & Remap, Render Settings, Server Setup, Rendering, Queue & History, Download, File Browser, Whole app and Telegram — each with its sources file, its decisions record and its signature. **Eight markers cleared, five statements corrected, and six decisions taken by Smoozy rather than by the Creator**, every one of them labelled in the document that carries it so the Creator can pull any of them. The six: what a video job counts and re-runs; that a render is ended the gentler way that leaves whole frames; that Smoozy's resolution box holds to the range Blender accepts; that none of Blender's other cost settings is offered, with the texture-resolution group left off pending the Creator's ruling; that Smoozy names the card technology when a render is meant to use the cards, turning a broken driver into a refusal in seconds; and that every render is asked for the line naming what did the work, so the finished entry can say it.

  **The five corrections are the part worth reading, because each was a promise this project had already made.** An interruption does **not** leave whole frames only — an abrupt ending left a short last image every time in the largest set of runs, at exactly zero bytes on Linux, so the re-run's last-frame rule is the ordinary case again rather than a safety net. A file output write that runs out of room does **not** fail the job: the render finishes at a successful status with the lost file's saved line printed, so Download's file count is the only place the loss shows. The frame preview does **not** say a job's textures loaded — only that a texture feeding colour is missing. A submitted job's files **can** change under it, and a picture replaced mid-render lands from a later frame onward. And *what values the resolution percentage accepts* is answered, so Render Settings' one unfinished section is finished.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10. What is still owed out of these rounds is listed below and is short.

So the parts of this gap that are about desktop editions alone remain closed to it.

It is left here in full so what it costs stays visible. *(Corrected 2026-08-30: this said there is still no Windows computer, four days after the first one was rented and used.)*

- **What a Blender render failure usually is.** Reports closed. What it settles is where a failure is announced and what it says — three places at once inside the window, and on the terminal outside it. The sharpest finding for the message this item is about: **the same failure is worded differently depending on whether Blender has a window**, so whatever Smoozy shows the user is not what an artist would see on their own machine. What it does **not** settle is the Creator's own claim that nearly every failure is insufficient graphics-card memory — that was deliberately kept away from the researchers, and stands on the Creator's word as it always did. See `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Reports.md`.

  **Due:** done for the carrying. Carried into the specs on 2026-08-06. **What is not done, and is not this item's**, is the Creator's own claim that nearly every failure is insufficient graphics-card memory — kept from the researchers on purpose and standing on their word as it always did. What that should change about the message is the separate item below on re-submitting a failed job, which is still open. **DONE, marked 2026-10-16 by owed piece 9 of Build step 10c, from `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Open Threads' Due now and the board's owed lines — what is still owed, in pieces.md`.** The claim stands on the Creator's word, and Simplify on a failed job is built.

- **`File Browser` raised two seams against `Server protocol and transfers`, and both are housings rather than contradictions.** *(From `File Browser` being written, 2026-08-20. Recorded rather than repaired; no closed document was edited.)*

  **The publication-intent parameter.** `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` X7 gives every transfer a declared publication intent — **create-intended**, published by the base rename, which the protocol itself refuses over an existing target, and **replace-intended**, published by the extension route that replaces deliberately. That is what keeps a collision judged on a filesystem that folds two spellings, or something landing in the race window between the pre-flight walk and the landing, from becoming a silent overwrite: the filesystem itself gets the last word at the only moment a wrong answer could destroy anything. **`Server protocol and transfers` S15 and S16 accommodate it mechanically** — S16 already states both operations and when each suffices — **but that document names no such parameter today**, and S15 says outright that nothing else in a transfer touches the final name. Until it does, this is a requirement handed to its mechanism rather than a decision taken inside it.

  **The copy staging path kind.** A machine-side copy never writes at a final name, for the same reason a transfer never does — a copy killed part-way is Fact 18's partial arriving without a wire. So a copy stages and publishes by one rename, under a path synthesised from the copy's **operation** identity rather than a transfer identity. Whether that path kind lives beside S4's in the Smoozy staging directory, or that document blesses a sibling directory, is a routing decision. `File Browser` M7 is written to stand either way.

  **Neither blocks anything and neither is urgent.** Both are named in `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` Section 13 item 12.

  ~~**Due:** at a seam round between the two documents, or at the stage close.~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close; marked 2026-09-02.** **The publication-intent parameter** is `Server protocol and transfers` **S15a** — *"Every publication carries a caller-declared intent, and the intent chooses the operation"* — routed from `File Browser` §13 item 12, so the requirement this entry said was handed to a mechanism is now a decision taken inside it. **The copy staging path kind** was settled in the same routing.

- **One thing `File Browser` observed is a bound rather than a rule, and it is the weakest ground under a mechanism this stage now leans on.** *(From `File Browser` being written, 2026-08-20.)* The base rename's refusal of an existing target — the backstop under X7's create-intended publication, and therefore under the collision rule of Row 192 — **is observed on one server, and that server is the caller's own**. `Server protocol and transfers` S16 states the base operation suffices only where the final name does not exist, which is the same claim from the specification side. **A rented server whose base rename replaced instead of refusing would turn the backstop into a silent replace, in exactly the two cases it exists for**: the folding filesystem's twin, and something landing in the race window. Nothing observed on any rental says it would. **It rides free on any later rental** and justifies none of its own; it is carried at that document's Section 13 item 13.

- **Blender 5.2.1 alone has now failed on an older NVIDIA driver on two separate machines, and nobody has asked whether that is a pattern.** *(Raised 2026-08-30 by the runner, at the close of the Blender Research stage.)* On a rented machine in Poland with an RTX 3060 on driver 550.120, 4.5.12, 5.0.1 and 5.1.2 rendered through OptiX and **5.2.1 failed to load its kernel and wrote no image**, ending in an internal compiler error. That is written into all eight Settings findings for this round with its bound stated plainly: one card, one driver, one machine, one day, run once. **And the equipment section of `Preparation/Blender Research/Method.md` records a second, unrelated machine, rented and rejected before the Norway machine, where "Blender 5.2.1 could not use the ordinary NVIDIA rendering path on it at all while the other three could."** Different machine, different path, and no raw output behind it.

  **Neither record was allowed to lend weight to the other, and that was right** — importing a link between two separate observations is exactly the invented step this stage exists to prevent. **But two machines is no longer one**, and what is at stake is not small: a render farm that picks a machine by price gets whatever driver the seller has, and if the newest Blender silently fails on older drivers while the others work, that is a rule Smoozy needs and a trap every test run on such a machine falls into.

  **What would settle it:** the same four Blenders on two or three rented machines chosen for their driver versions, one deliberately old, asking each which rendering paths it can actually reach before anything is rendered. It is a Blender Research question and it has no number yet.

  **Due:** when the Blender Research stage is next opened, or sooner if anything rents a machine with a driver older than the current one.

  **ANSWERED 2026-10-11, at Build: it is a pattern, and Blender's own pages state it.** Blender's stated OptiX driver minimum is 535 for 4.5 to 5.1, 570 for 5.2.0 and 575 for 5.2.1 and later (**documented**; `Rendering` L23a, sources C17). A rented A6000 on 550.163.01 failed 5.2.2's OptiX the same way, and 570 and newer rendered (**observed**, `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Making OptiX work — what we found.md`). The plans now carry it: `Rendering` L23a and §9 item 3, `Server Setup` R1 item 5 and B12b, `Servers` H11b.
