That section also states what protects it, when it is updated and clamped, and that **a missing mark is no floor rather than an error** — never blocking, recreated at the next update. So the concern this item raised — a reader having to work out from §12.3 that a third file exists — is answered by there being nowhere else for it to be.


  Superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04; recorded 2026-10-26. Paid-layer work is withdrawn. Earlier rulings and closures remain as history.

- **What the user is shown when the machine misbehaves.** Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-06, while the Blender Research stage was being re-run on rented machines. Their words: how it will be displayed in Smoozy to the user is also very important.

  It is not a research question and it was not answered as one. What the research can supply — and now does — is what a program can actually observe at each failure: the exact words, the exit status, how long it takes, and whether one failure can be told apart from another. Several of those turn out to be sharp. A machine that has been restarted and one that has been destroyed look identical for the first few seconds and can only be told apart by waiting. Blender on a full disk announces the save, exits 0, and leaves a picture that does not end. An add-on built for the wrong computer installs, switches on, and says nothing until something uses it. An output folder after a restart holds more files than there are pictures, the extra ones being zero bytes with ordinary names. In each case the honest thing to show the user is not obvious, and in some of them the truthful answer is that Smoozy cannot yet tell.

  **Due:** at Design, which owns what the user sees. It touches Architecture too, since some of these can only be told apart by watching over time, which is a thing to be built rather than a thing to be drawn.

- **The specs' sources files were checked in full on 2026-08-10, and the trimming this item asked for turns out to have been done already.** The item was written on 2026-07-30, when ten sources files stated their Blender facts in full while the [Method](../Blender%20Research/Method.md) had just settled that a spec's sources entry should carry a label plus a pointer at a question number instead. *Every fact has one home* forbids the two copies, and one disagreement had already come of it.

  **What the check found.** Every entry in every one of the twelve sources files was read. **The rebuilds of 2026-08-06 and the carry-in of 2026-08-10 had already done the work**: the Blender entries carry a label, a question number and what the answer does not cover, and none of them restates the fact the comparison owns. The entries that carry **no** question number were looked at one at a time, and every one of them is correctly without one — they are outside facts that are not Blender's and have no question to point at (rented machines, SSH, Telegram's own behaviour, the licence service), or facts the Creator supplied and ruled not to be researched, or entries that say plainly they are owned and cited by another document.

  **What was genuinely stale was three labels, and they are fixed.** Two in the [Server Setup sources](../Specs/Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md) — whether installing an add-on disturbs a running render, which **Add-ons 12** answered on 2026-08-07 while the old *unresolved* entry stayed beside the new one; and how elevated access is obtained, whose product half was settled on 2026-08-10. One in the [File Browser sources](../Specs/Sources/File%20Browser.sources.md), the same elevated-access half. Each now says what moved and what is still open, rather than reading as though nothing had. A fourth entry, the diffuse colour pass in the [Queue & History sources](../Specs/Sources/Queue%20&%20History.sources.md), is unchanged in its question and now says that **Images 9** made it worth less than it was.

  **The lesson worth keeping, since this will happen again.** The risk was never the trimming. It is that a carry-in writes a **new** entry beside an old one and leaves the old label saying *unresolved* — which is what happened three times here. Whoever carries the next round in should look for the old entry before writing the new one.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.

- **The operating-system line asks every question and aims at none.** Every research answer carries a standing line asking whether it holds on every operating system Blender runs on. The Creator questioned it on 2026-08-01, having watched *The machine* come back full of Windows and macOS detail Smoozy will never use. Two operating systems are genuinely in play and they are **different ones**: Smoozy renders only on Linux machines, and the app itself runs on Mac, Windows and Linux ([module map](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)). So a question about what a machine needs to run or render is a **Linux** question and nothing else, while a question about paths, file names or what a `.blend` points at is about the **artist's own computer** and reaches all three. The line as worded invites a survey of all three either way, which is how one topic came back with driver numbers for cards nobody will rent and a manual's Windows install page quoted four times. Proposed shape, not yet settled: keep the line, but have the sheet say which of the two the question is about, so an answer covers what it must and stops. Two things to weigh against it — a blind researcher told "answer for Linux" has been given a hint about the product, which is exactly what blind framing forbids; and the wide answers are cheap to skip and impossible to add later. *(Creator-raised, 2026-08-01.)*

  **Due:** when the Creator returns to it. They ruled on 2026-08-01 to leave the line exactly as it is for now, having weighed it and judged the leak worse than the waste — so nothing schedules this, and the next sheet is written under the wide wording.
- ~~**A hand test can never reach the machine Smoozy actually uses.**~~ **It can, and it has, hundreds of times. Corrected 2026-08-30 at the stage close.** This item said that the Creator has one Apple-silicon Mac, that every render Smoozy runs happens on a rented Linux machine with a graphics card of another make, that the operating system that mattered most was the one **no hand test can touch** — *"not by oversight but structurally"* — and that *"this does not get better by trying harder"*. **Every part of that is now false**, and the sentence it ended on is what actually happened: *"The cheapest answer is probably a rented machine used for research the way the Creator's own is."* **That answer was taken.** The equipment section of the [Blender Research method](../Blender%20Research/Method.md) now describes **seventeen rented Linux machines**, all destroyed, each written in because a finding points at it, and the Creator ruled on 2026-08-06 that a rented machine of that kind counts as equipment a hand test may use. Almost every answer this stage holds is now two machines rather than one. *(Raised while closing The machine, 2026-08-01.)*

  **What is left of it is real and much narrower, and it is why the item is corrected rather than deleted.** A rented machine is somebody else's hardware and a share of a bigger chip; most of them misreport their own size; **exactly one was ever checked to be uncontended**; and several of the machines with a screen drew it in software. So a hand test reaches the *kind* of machine Smoozy uses and does not reach a quiet one unless somebody looks. **And the Creator's Mac is not one fixed machine either** — it changed its operating system and its 5.2 build under this stage, which the machines sweep of 2026-08-30 established.

  **Due:** nothing. **The structural claim is withdrawn**, and what replaced it — which machine a result came from, and whether that machine was quiet, sized honestly, or drawing in software — lives in the equipment section and in `Preparation/Blender Research/Across topics/What the research cannot say.md`, which gained headings for all three on 2026-08-30.
- ~~**Code-audit templates.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-03, at Build's step 4 close, and opened to check rather than taken on a claim.** The three code versions were written on 2026-09-03 against the first module ready to audit — `Preparation/Groundwork/Templates/Code Auditor.md`, `Code Consolidator.md` and `Code Implementer.md`, with `Build Gate.md` beside them — and `Preparation/Groundwork/Auditing.md` says so at the paragraph that used to carry the `[PENDING]`. That marker is gone from that document. *(The original entry: the Auditor, Consolidator and Implementer templates in `Templates/` are written for document Subjects; their code versions belong to the Build stage. **Due:** at Build.)*
- **Coverage between stages.** Nothing yet guarantees every spec gets a design, every design gets an architecture, and so on. A light checklist will do; decide its shape at the end of the Specs stage, when the first full set of documents exists to check. Two things to build on when it is designed, and two now settled. Settled: documents contradicting each other is handled — a seam is a Subject, and a stage's seams are checked before it is ticked complete (see Auditing and Start Here); and part of the flagging half, because a round's runner writes whatever that round put on other documents into this file, so the checklist should lean on that rather than repeat it. Still open, beyond the coverage question itself, is a question the Creator raised: **knowing which documents have gone stale enough to need an audit at all.** Today the Creator picks Subjects by feel, and a runner reports how much unexamined material has piled up only for the one Subject being audited. Nothing looks across all of them, so a document nobody owns can quietly become the most load-bearing thing in a stage without anyone proposing it for a round. The decision stays the Creator's; what is missing is the information reaching them without their having to remember. Note one constraint when this is designed: anything tracking audit state cannot live in a `Preparation` document, because auditors read those and must stay blind to the audit trail — it belongs under `Audits/`. *(Creator-raised, 2026-07-20, while closing the Keys spec round 4.)*

  **Due:** at the close of the Specs stage. **A first version of the coverage half has now been run, on 2026-08-13, and it worked.** Every Design document was read against the Specs it serves, one agent per module, looking only for three things: a promise with no home on a screen, a contradiction, and a screen decision no spec grants. It returned 68 items across ten modules and cost one sitting. Three things are worth keeping when this is designed properly. It is **not an audit round** — no blindness, no verdict, no trail — because a mismatch is checkable and extra independent readers buy almost nothing; the Creator's own reasoning for cutting a seam round to three auditors applies harder here. It has to be run **module by module**, because the size rule forbids reading a whole stage against another in one pass. And every item must **quote both sides**, which is what lets a single reader be checked. What it cannot do is judge whether the design is any good; that is still the scenario replay's. *(Run 2026-08-13, at the Creator's direction.)*
- **Whether one read of a file answers for a view layer switched by a driver or by a script, and it now carries more than it did.** The [Render Settings spec](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) has held this as unresolved since the view layer section was written: reading a file tells you whether each layer renders at a named frame, including where that is keyframed, and **neither a driver nor a script inside the file was tested in any version**. Not that Blender refuses to say — nobody asked.

  **What changed on 2026-08-13** is that the same question now also carries the whole-job progress bar. The Creator settled that both bars are a plain sum of samples, which means the job's total needs every frame-and-view-layer pairing the job will actually render, worked out before it starts. For a keyframed switch that is answered. For a driven or scripted one it is not, and the total would be wrong in a way the user sees — a bar that runs past its end or stalls short of it.

  **It does not have to go back to Blender Research, and deliberately should not.** That stage is closed and neatly ordered, and reopening it to add one question costs more than the answer is worth. The fact is Architecture's anyway: that stage builds the read, and the Rules already have it record its own outside facts with a label and a citation like any other. So it is answered where it is needed, or it stays unresolved and the design says so.

  **If it stays unresolved, the shape of the honest answer is known**: the total is a plan rather than a promise, and the bar is written to survive being wrong — which it has to be regardless, since a frame with adaptive sampling on can finish well short of its sample count. *(Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-13, while settling how both progress bars are counted.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, by `Render Settings` — answered on the shapes posed, and by testing rather than by assumption.** That document rented a container for about five cents and put the question to Blender 4.5.12 and 5.2.0 directly. **A driver: yes** — frame-stepping inside a headless read evaluated a scripted-expression driver identically to the render, frame for frame. **A script inside the file: yes, on both sides of the preference that gates it** — with stored scripts off, which is every version's starting state, neither the read nor the render runs the file's handler and they agree; with it on, both run it and they still agree. The ground is structural: the read and the render share one evaluation machinery under one preference. **So `Queue & History`'s plan total is computable from the same read for these files**, and the boxes are drawn from a reading that describes them.

  **The residue is numbered rather than left loose: Fact 113.** A Python-function driver, which 5.x documentation says needs the automatic-running permission, was not posed; nor was any stored-script shape but the one. `Preparation/Architecture/Render Settings.md` Section 13 item 7 states what breaks — a shape where the read's stepping and the render diverge makes the boxes and the plan total wrong for that file, silently — and item 9 carries the related corner where a file's own stored script redrives a switch the page has pinned. **The bar's *a plan rather than a promise* shape stands either way**, as this item always said it would.

- **Screen decisions no spec grants, left standing and marked.** The 2026-08-13 sweep found four places where the Design gives the user something no spec mentions. None was removed, because each is Design's own ground and plausible; each now carries a marker where it sits. They are: *copy address* in every server menu, which waits on the Servers spec's own parked question about a server action beyond connect, edit and remove; *Check for updates* in the Smoozy menu, where the spec has the app keep itself up to date and gives the user no update action at all; the folder column and its link in Server Setup's version list; and the count of switched-off warnings on the restore control, where the spec grants the control and not the number. Each is a one-line spec addition or a deletion, and the Creator decides which.

  **Due:** at the close of the Design stage, together — four one-line rulings rather than four separate sittings.
- **The Access spec owes a line about what the licence service reports.** The Settings design shows each of the two computers with when it last checked in, and uses that time as the only thing separating two machines with the same name. The `Audits/Specs/Access/Decisions record.md` lists what the outside service must be able to do — hold two slots, release one on request, report a key as no longer valid and why — and a last-check-in time is a fourth thing not on it. The whole licence plan rests on that list, so either it gains the line or the design finds another separator. *(From the specs-against-design sweep, 2026-08-13.)*

  **Half of this is now settled and it is the half that could have broken.** Writing `Access` on 2026-08-20 checked it: the last-check-in time **is** on `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md` §1.2's check-response list, carried field by field with every slot row, and `Licence service` §4 and §7 have the service write it at every successful check. **So the design's separator exists and the architecture supplies it** — nothing has to be redesigned. What is still owed is only the spec's own line: `Preparation/Specs/Access.md` lists three things the outside service must do and a last-check-in time is a fourth.

  **The design's side of this was paid on 2026-08-25 and the debt got wider rather than closing.** The Creator ruled that each computer in the release offer shows **two** things beside its name — **when it took its slot** as well as **when it last checked in** — because two slots both reading *MacBook Pro* give the user nothing to choose by. **So the outside-service list is now short by two capabilities rather than one**, and neither is knowable on the asking computer or named in Fact 9's headline. **Nothing is unanswered**: `Licence service` §3 and §7 and `Security and licence` §1.2 supply both, and `Preparation/Architecture/Rows/Design — Settings.md` Row 338 carries the whole of it. *(Widened 2026-08-29 by the second stage-close re-sweep. Row 338's own note had said the spec was untouched, four days after it was touched.)*

  ~~**Due:** at the Architecture stage close, with the other spec sentences the rulings of 2026-08-25 were not carried down into — they are listed together in `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/The second stage close re-sweep — what came back.md`.~~ **FIRED 2026-09-02, at the Architecture stage close, and the edit lands in a Specs file — so it is routed rather than done here.** **What is owed is one sentence in `Preparation/Specs/Access.md`**, which lists three things the outside licence service must be able to do and is now short by **two**: **when a computer took its slot**, as well as **when it last checked in**. **Nothing is unanswered underneath it** — `Licence service` §3 and §7 and `Security and licence` §1.2 supply both, and `Preparation/Architecture/Rows/Design — Settings.md` Row 338 carries the whole of it — so this is a spec catching up with a ruling rather than a gap in the plan. **It costs one sentence naming two dates and blocks nothing.** It goes with the other spec sentences the rulings of 2026-08-25 were not carried into, listed together at the audit file named above.
- ~~**Audit-system refinements.**~~ **LIFTED INTO *DUE NOW* on 2026-09-03, at the step 4 close, because its Due read *at Build* and Build is running.** One thing remained open — a module-level tracker for additions made after the initial build — and it is now in that section with what is missing and what is already settled. Nothing about it changed in the move.

  Superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04; recorded 2026-10-26. Paid-layer work is withdrawn. Earlier rulings and closures remain as history.

- **Stronger blindness for auditors, if the instruction leaks.** Auditors are told not to browse the project or open `Audits/`, which keeps them blind to each other's judgments. That's an instruction, not a wall. If it ever proves leaky, hand each auditor a copy of the project with the `Audits/` folder removed — then blindness is enforced, not trusted. Not worth building until there's a reason to. **Blender Research met this problem for real on 2026-07-30**, and the answer there is worth knowing before this one is built. Its first researcher walked straight into the project's own material — not by disobeying, but by obeying `CLAUDE.md`, which orders every agent to read `Start Here` first. The fixes were an exception in `CLAUDE.md`, a blunt ban written first in the brief and repeated at the end, a declaration the researcher must make in its own output, and moving its sources to a top-level folder so "read only your sources" points away from the planning documents rather than beside them. The Creator ruled that a bare folder outside the project — the wall — was rejected on purpose: it is one more thing to remember every time, and a rule that must be remembered eventually is not. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-30). If auditor blindness ever proves leaky, weigh the same trade rather than assuming the wall is better.

  **Due:** only if auditor blindness proves leaky. Nothing schedules this.
- **Producing and shipping the app builds.** The downloadable binary is produced by a build-and-release pipeline (GitHub Actions is the intended tool), which the Creator — a non-coder — will need hands-on help setting up. This is not a module; it's a Whole-app infrastructure concern. It surfaces across stages: Specs owns only the user-facing sliver, now settled — the user downloads a binary, and Smoozy keeps itself up to date thereafter (Creator-stated, 2026-07-27); Architecture owns the substance, which now includes where an update comes from, how it is checked and how it lands (how cross-platform binaries are produced, code-signed, versioned, and hosted for download); Build owns doing it (writing the workflows, producing real builds). The user-facing sliver is written into the [Whole app spec](../Specs/Whole%20app.md); revisit the substance at Architecture.

  **The substance is answered as of 2026-08-18 and this item now owes only the writing.** **Fact 74** covers producing, signing and hosting per system, and what an application must do to replace itself while running; the answer of record is `Preparation/Architecture/The user's own computer — where a download lands, what it will take, what it will show, and how the app replaces itself.md` §5. **The promise is reachable on all three systems and not by one mechanism** — **corrected 2026-08-22 at the counted gate, having been overtaken twice**: macOS replaces the complete signed bundle at the installed path, **Linux hands a signed package to the distribution's own mechanism and asks the user for their computer password once** (Creator-stated 2026-08-22), and **Windows is side-by-side**, because Microsoft documents the unattended outcome and documents that replacing a running image at its own path fails. **The expensive half is not technical:** Windows signing runs through an identity gate documented as open to individuals in two named countries only, and Microsoft's own pages say a signed new binary still warns for "several weeks and hundreds of clean installs from a wide audience" with no mechanism to submit for review — at this project's scale, the documentation says the warning does not go away on its own. That is a cost the user meets and no spec mentions, so **whoever writes `Desktop app and delivery` checks it against `Method`'s third escalation rather than absorbing it.**

  **The check was run on 2026-08-18 and the escalation was declined, with the reason recorded rather than the finding.** `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` §19.2 rules that **no permitted answer removes the warning** — signing does not, extended validation no longer bypasses it, and the store route is forbidden by the download promise — so it is not a cost the architecture's own choice imposes, which is what an escalation is for. The identity gate's money falls on the Creator, which the shape already settles does not return to Specs. **The warning is stated plainly in that document** so nobody reads the download promise as promising a silent first run. **A new fact came out of the check: Fact 97**, whether that warning fires on a build the application launches itself, which no page read joins and which no reading can close.

  **The Mac app is still not notarized as of 0.1.8 (2026-10-02), so 0.1.8 also needs the quarantine command once after install** — `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine "/Applications/Smoozy.app"` — **until notarization lands**; *Open Anyway* does not appear for it. Its release notes and the Release Runner template's Creator line say so (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/Piece 11 — the release of 0.1.8.md`). *Noted by the records writer holding `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Brief — the paperwork after 0.1.8.md`.*

  **Due:** the writing is done and only **Build** is left — the workflows and the real builds.
- **Keep the desktop app relatively small.** The Creator wants the final downloadable app to be relatively small and does not set a fixed size ceiling. When Architecture chooses the desktop technology, it compares real release artifacts for Mac, Windows and Linux and prefers a route that uses an operating-system web view or another materially smaller shell. A route that bundles a complete browser engine is not chosen merely because it keeps more of the existing promises. If the smaller route cannot keep one or more promises, Architecture stops and brings the exact user outcome and its smaller replacement back to the Creator; any promise they remove or narrow is changed in Specs before the technology choice proceeds. The Creator is prepared to give up promises for the smaller app, but no unspecified promise is removed in advance. This is a Smoozy constraint, not a claim that any named technology has a particular size. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-13).

  **DONE, 2026-08-18, and the ruling was honoured rather than worked around.** Six candidate shells were read on 2026-08-18 against their own documentation and their own release listings, **51 real published artifact sizes** taken across all three systems and independently re-checked to the byte — the register carries it as **Fact 96** — and `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` §2 takes the choice against them: ~~**Wails v2**~~ **Tauri v2 — corrected 2026-09-02, see the closure below; Wails was the provisional direction on this date and the Build measurement reversed it** — a compiled shell over each operating system's own web view, with a written fallback order behind it (**Wails v2, then Electron**) and **Qt 6 and Flutter disqualified** on that document's own required properties rather than ordered by size. **No promise was offered up for size, the stop was run and not reached, and nothing came to the Creator.** The bundled-engine route's larger size was found to buy nothing, because Row 280 is kept below the framework by each operating system's own replacement mechanism on every route.

  **One thing the choice does not rest on, stated because the figures invite the opposite reading:** three of the six publish no artifact of their own, and the two web-view routes are both compiled, so **no published figure predicts what Smoozy itself will weigh.** That document's own unresolved list carries it, and what closes it is building one throwaway on both and reading the two artifacts — a **Build-stage** measurement, not owed before then.

  ~~**Due:** nothing further at Architecture. **Pruned at the stage close unless the Build measurement above is wanted as its own item.**~~ **FIRED 2026-09-02, at the Architecture stage close: the Architecture half is done and the item is kept rather than pruned, for the one Build measurement inside it.** **Nothing is deleted from this file**, so the whole entry stands as the record of how the size constraint was honoured — six shells read against their own documentation, 51 real published artifact sizes across three systems, and `Desktop app and delivery` §2 taking Wails v2 with a written fallback order. **What is still owed, and it is Build's:** **no published figure predicts what Smoozy itself will weigh** — three of the six candidates publish no artifact of their own and both web-view routes are compiled — so the only thing that answers it is **building one throwaway on each of the two and reading the two artifacts.** **Due, narrowed:** at Build, that one measurement. It blocks nothing; the choice does not rest on it and never did.

  **CLOSED 2026-09-02, and the measurement changed the answer.** Build built the throwaway on each of the two candidates — the same page, the same source icon, the same processor target, one fresh runner image per system — and **Tauri v2 came out materially smaller on the comparison sheet's own rule**: Wails larger by 68.2 per cent and 1.605 MiB at the comparison the rule names, against a threshold of 10 per cent and 1 MiB, and Tauri smaller on all three systems in both the packed and the unpacked measure. **So the shell is Tauri v2, not Wails v2, and everything above naming Wails as the choice is the record of a provisional direction rather than the answer.** `Desktop app and delivery` H4, H5, H7 and Section 22 item 1 close on it; H5b carries the fourteen figures; the record is `Preparation/Build/Shell measurement.md`.

  **What this item got exactly right, and it is why it was kept rather than pruned:** no published figure predicted what Smoozy itself would weigh, and the only thing that answered it was building one throwaway on each. **It also said the choice did not rest on the measurement — and the measurement moved the choice.** That is not a contradiction: the choice of *route class* never rested on it and still does not, and the choice of *name inside that class* was always the sheet's. **The Creator's ruling is honoured either way** — the smaller of the two preferred routes is taken, no promise was offered up for size, and nothing reached the Creator.
- **Confirm the Product brief's Blender specifics at Blender Research.** The brief names Blender behaviours — the Cycles and EEVEE engines, samples, resolution percentage, view layers, scenes, and which settings come from the file itself. These are Creator-stated (Rule 2). **Two of them closed on 2026-07-30**: the Creator ruled that the engines being Cycles and EEVEE, and the resolution percentage being a percentage of the output size the file names in pixels, are settled and are not to be researched — both are recorded in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md) among the questions deliberately not asked. The rest stand, and each is carried by a question in that register.

  **Due:** as each carrying topic's findings land.
- **Keys: the SSH facts and safekeeping, for Architecture.** The Keys spec deliberately leaves two things to Architecture, both to be sourced and labelled per Rule 2: (1) which SSH key types Smoozy offers and which is the sensible modern default — the spec only promises "a good default, plus an advanced choice for another type"; (2) how a remembered key passphrase is stored safely. Settle both when Architecture reaches the Keys module.

  **The second half is no longer only Keys', and it is one question rather than several.** The whole-app **security** concern, settled on 2026-08-10, names six things Smoozy holds and says outright that how each is kept safely is Architecture's and is one question: the private half of an SSH key, a remembered key passphrase, a **remembered machine password** (new on 2026-08-10), the licence key, the **Telegram bot's token** — which is the whole of what lets anything speak as that bot — and each machine's remembered identity. Settle them together rather than one module at a time. Where an answer costs the user anything — a place to look, something to type, a thing that can be lost — that comes back to Specs.

  **DONE, 2026-08-20, and both halves closed the same day the `Keys` architecture was written.** **The first half:** `Preparation/Architecture/Keys.md` Section 2 fixes the supported set at three types — `ed25519`, `ecdsa`, `rsa` — makes the **offered** set a runtime reading of the installed binary rather than the manual's list, and takes `ed25519` as the default, **labelled a judgement rather than a documented fact**, on the manual's own no-argument behaviour and its not-recommended label on SHA1 `ssh-rsa`. Fact 32 is its source and the sourcing rule was kept. **The second half was already answered before Keys reached it**, exactly as this item asked: the six held things were settled as one question in `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md` §2 to §5, their cost to the user came back to Specs on 2026-08-16 and is a Creator-stated promise in `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md`'s Security concern, and `Keys` 8.1 is a **clause-by-clause pointer into that answer rather than a second arrangement**. **What Keys added of its own is only the keying** — one store item per key under a subject issued against the local key identifier, so the passphrase belongs to the key by construction and the path appears nowhere in it.

  **Due: done.** The spec's two markers are cleared by the runner with their decisions-record line; **pruned at the stage close.**
- **What a locked Smoozy lets the user do in Keys, beyond mending a key.** *(Raised by `Preparation/Architecture/Keys.md` on 2026-08-20 and recorded rather than settled, per that document's Section 13 item 9.)* `Preparation/Specs/Access.md`'s locked list names **mending a key in Keys** as staying open and says nothing about making a key, deleting one, removing one, copying a public half, or clearing a remembered passphrase. That list is `Access`' *"and lives there only"*, so `Keys` neither decided it nor assumed it, following `Servers` P17's shape — stating which of its own acts a lock could close and leaving the rule elsewhere. **Nothing is blocked either way**: every act in `Keys` is reachable as its own control, so any subset can be closed without that document moving. Two of them are worth the Creator's eye when it is settled, because they are the two that are not obviously *new work*: **deleting a key destroys files**, and **making a key** is the one act on the empty-list guidance's own path — a locked user who has no key yet cannot follow the route the app lays out for them.

  **Due: ruled, 2026-08-20.** `Preparation/Architecture/Access.md` §2 P7: **a lock closes none of the seven acts.** The grounds, each one line — `Keys` never talks to a server, so no act of its is new work on a machine and none costs the user money; the spec names mending as open and its closed enumeration names no `Keys` act at all; **a locked new user with no key cannot otherwise follow the empty-list guidance's own route**, which begins with making a key; and pointing a new key at anything is already stopped where it would matter, at *adding a server* and at *choosing a different key*. **Both acts this item flagged were weighed against `Method`'s third escalation and neither reaches it**: deleting a key while locked is the ordinary warned act and the lock changes nothing about how it asks, and **the opposite ruling on making a key is the one that would put something in front of the user that no spec grants**. **The Creator's eye is still available and costs nothing**: every act is its own control, so any subset can be closed later without `Keys` moving.

  Superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04; recorded 2026-10-26. Paid-layer work is withdrawn. Earlier rulings and closures remain as history.

- **Server actions beyond connect / edit / remove.** The Servers spec keeps its per-server action set deliberately open. **Half of this closed on 2026-07-29**: the [Server Setup spec](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) settles that Smoozy restarts a machine itself where a setup step needs it, without asking, which is where the pressure for a reboot came from. What is still open is whether the user gets a **standalone** reboot they ask for by hand, and any other per-server action that surfaces. Settle in the Servers spec, or wherever the pressure for one turns up.

  ~~**Due:** at Specs, in the Servers spec, or whenever pressure for one turns up.~~ **RULED 2026-09-02, under the Creator's standing delegation: the set is settled at three — connect, edit and remove — and a reboot the user asks for by hand is not offered.** Smoozy already restarts a machine itself where a setup step needs one, which is where the pressure came from, and a standalone reboot control gives the user a way to interrupt their own paid render for no promise anybody made. **Marked here at the Architecture stage close**, having been found still open by the sweep that ruled it. *(Recorded at `Audits/Architecture/Routed elsewhere.md`.)*
- **Do the four connection fields reach every server?** The Add Server dialog takes host, port, username and key. That covers a direct SSH connection; some servers instead sit behind a gateway that needs its own details (a "jump host"). Whether any provider the Creator would actually use needs that is an outside fact (Rule 2) — confirm it against the real providers in use, and settle at Architecture, where fast-changing provider facts are sourced. Until then, Smoozy supports the direct case only. Confirm one more thing in the same check: that a rented machine commonly hands out several containers, each on its own port. The Servers spec carries it as a labelled assumption and leans on it in two places. *(The containers half is from the Servers spec round 6, M47.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, and both halves came back the same way.** **Fact 61 is answered by reading**, on three providers' own documentation with the exact words, addresses and dates in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Servers.sources.md`. **No provider read fronts SSH with a gateway needing its own details**; where a proxy route exists it is an address and a port rather than a second set of credentials. **And the several-containers-on-one-address arrangement is documented rather than assumed** — the assumption the Servers spec carried and leaned on in two places is now sourced. **So the four fields stand**, and `Servers` Row 24 rests on documented ground. **What the reading does not cover is named rather than glossed:** three providers on one date, a fourth this project has rented from unread, and a bastion at a corporate or academic host outside it entirely — carried at `Servers` Section 16 item 8. **A fifth field would be a Specs change**, and nothing asks for one today.

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02.** This line stood under the closed one above it and had gone on reading as owed since 2026-08-19 — the same shape the runner removed once at the item on whether a machine can be read for work left sitting on it. **Six of them were found in this section at the Architecture stage close and all six are now struck.**
- **What Server Setup needs from Blender Research — the biggest single set any module owes.** Every Blender question in it now lives in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md), in the **Builds**, **The machine** and **Add-ons** topics, each with its origin and what breaks if it comes back wrong. The [Server Setup spec](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) carries each fact's label in its body and the fuller statement in its [Sources](../Specs/Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md). Nothing more is repeated here.

  What is left in this item is the part that is **not** a Blender question, and does not go to research at all. Three assumptions about rented machines remain for **Architecture**. The former fourth — whether Smoozy can restart a rented machine over the connection it holds — is answered yes by Fact 51 and carried into Server Setup. **Whether a restart really stops everything in flight, and whether a running render dies with it** — the busy-machine warning, the re-run that follows, and the promise that the rest of a setup finishes first are all written for this moment; work stopping in some other way, a transfer left half-written or a render neither finished nor gone, reaches the specs that say what a lost connection does to each. **Whether installing drivers requires a restart** — only the reason a restart was wanted at all; a negative answer means the rule never fires, since it is written for any step needing one. **Whether a rented machine can be a locked container** whose drivers are fixed and which Smoozy cannot change — inherited from the Servers spec, and to be confirmed with the container assumption already cited in the [Servers sources](../Specs/Sources/Servers.sources.md). *(The first two raised by the Server Setup spec round 1, 2026-07-29.)* **The container assumption itself is now sourced rather than assumed, 2026-08-19, by Fact 61** — several containers on one machine, each on its own external port, on two providers' own documentation.

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, by `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md`.** All three remaining assumptions are settled and the document carries each with its evidence. **Whether a restart really stops everything in flight** — Fact 55's transfer half and **render half are both answered**, the render half against a real Cycles render on 2026-08-15/16 with two real machine restarts, which also settles that Blender does not write to a temporary name and rename. **The clause left unposed is the dependency check**, and it closes as unresolved with what breaks named. *(This entry, the register and the document's writing brief all carried the render half as untried until the before-audit gate caught it on 2026-08-19; the batch had answered it four days earlier.)* **Whether installing drivers requires a restart** — Fact 80, answered yes by two different mechanisms that disagree about the signal, so the rule is *run the check after installing and restart only if it fails*, never on the package manager's word. **Whether a rented machine can be a locked container** — Fact 48, answered yes, and such rentals are the norm **on that provider's own service, by that provider's own words** *(Narrowed 2026-08-23, out of the 6.8 re-run repair: the *norm* half is one seller's own documentation about that seller's own instances, and the three-seller batch of 2026-08-22 recorded the other two shipping virtual machines. Nothing turns on which kind is commoner — every rule reading it is written per machine kind off a reading taken at every placement. `Facts.md` Fact 48's headline was narrowed the same day and seven restatements with it.)*.

- **Two more things nobody has checked about driving a rented machine.** Both are the same shape as the four above, both are assumptions with no source in this project and neither from the Creator, and **neither is a Blender question** — they do not go to Blender Research.

  **Whether a rented machine can be searched for every Blender on it.** That a machine cannot be *asked* which Blenders it holds is settled and observed; only a search of its files found every one, and the [Server Setup spec](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) hands delivering that search to Architecture without anything standing behind it. What falls over is written up in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md): the list of Blenders a machine offers, the version match in [Render Settings](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md), and the mismatch warning that rests on that pick — and with no list, no job can be sent from that machine at all.

  **Whether the dependency check's read can be carried out on a rented machine.** That a `.blend` can be read for what it points at is settled on the Creator's Mac and on the rented Linux machines the research used; that **Smoozy** can drive that read on a machine it never touched, over the connection it holds, is not. What falls over is written up in the [Dependency Upload & Remap sources](../Specs/Sources/Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.sources.md): the check itself, and with it every row, every located file, the record of what each reference resolved to, the remap, and what stops [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md)'s missing-assets warning.

  A negative answer to either is a hole in the spec that leans on it rather than a narrowing of it. *(Raised by the scenario "The user renders the same project on a new machine", round 1, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due: the Blender-search half done, 2026-08-19, by `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md` 5.4.** Fact 45 answered it in part and the document carries both the answer and its ceiling: a whole-disk file search crossing filesystems, plus one asking-run per candidate under the version-reading discipline, with Fact 45's three traps each closed by a stated rule — the version parsed from the `Blender ` line and never by position, deduplication never by resolved path (a snap's path resolves to the snap launcher), and the search never restricted to one filesystem (which silently loses a snap on a loop device). **The ceiling is named rather than hidden:** a renamed binary is found only by the asking-run, and a non-root login found three of four Blenders and was refused the fourth outright. **The dependency-check half is `Dependency Upload & Remap`'s and was answered on 2026-08-18.**

  **THREE THINGS FROM `Server Setup`'s ROUND 1 CODE AUDIT SIT BESIDE THIS THREAD RATHER THAN AS NEW ITEMS. Parked 2026-09-04, at Build's step 6 close, on that Verdict's own instruction** — it says the two unchecked facts *"belong beside it"*, and this is the entry it names. The third is the same module's version ask, which is this entry's own material. **None of the three is a Blender question, and none of them is answered here.**

  **1. Whether `ubuntu-drivers install` accepts an apt option, or passes one to the apt it drives.** **What breaks:** on Ubuntu the driver install has no bounded wait, so `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md` S7b's busy-before-broken rule is defeated for that one step — a machine two minutes into its uptime, running its own automatic updates, fails the step at once for a reason that is not a fault. **Ubuntu is the family whose own documentation S7b quotes for the busy case**, so the family where the collision is documented is the family whose driver install is unprotected against it. **The gap is recorded unkept at its own site** — `App/Server Setup/src/elevated.rs`, `THE_UBUNTU_DRIVER_INSTALL`, the one package command in that crate with no `-o DPkg::Lock::Timeout=120` — and it is owed a line on the Build board. **What closes it is one reading of that tool's own publication.** No machine, and nothing written from memory. **Due:** whenever that tool's publication is read, or whenever `Server Setup`'s driver step is next opened. Nothing else waits on it.

  **2. What `-type f` selects on the `find` published by Ubuntu and Debian, and whether the snap path Fact 45 records is a link.** **What breaks: nothing today.** The narrowing was taken out of the search in that round, so B10 stands as written — a whole-disk search restricted by nothing — and the cost is extra candidates, one asking-run each, with the ones that answer nothing named on B11's did-not-answer list. **The answer decides only whether a narrower search can be put back later on evidence rather than on memory.** The first half wants one reading of that tool's publication. **The link half wants no publication at all:** this project's own `Facts.md` and `Six machines` record that `/snap/bin/blender` *resolves to* `/usr/bin/snap` and never what kind of file it is, so the record is already as far as reading takes it. **Due:** whenever a narrower search is wanted, and never by a guess in the meantime.

  **3. The version ask's kill half is unkept, and it is recorded rather than claimed.** The ask now carries a time limit of sixty seconds, decided rather than measured, and **that limit ends the desktop's own wait**. `Server Setup` B1 asks for more: something outside Blender ready to kill *"the invoked Blender process and nothing above it"*, which is on the machine and not at the desk. **Nothing in these plans establishes a route for it** — a command naming that one process would carry a machine-origin value into a command string, which `Server protocol and transfers` 1.1 forbids, and writing one from memory is what Rule 2 forbids. **What breaks:** a run that hit the limit leaves **a Blender running on a machine the user pays for**, until the machine restarts or the process ends by itself. **Nothing hangs and nothing is claimed** — the user is told the candidate did not answer. Recorded at `App/Server Setup/src/blender.rs`, `B1S_KILL_HALF_IS_UNKEPT_AND_WHAT_BREAKS_IS_A_BLENDER_LEFT_RUNNING`, and owed a line on the Build board. **What has never been established is whether the limit ends the run on the machine or only the desktop's wait**, and only a machine can say. **Due:** the machine Build rents to watch step 6's milestone, which already carries this on its list. **That is Build's own rental to run the app on, not a machine rented to answer a question** — the two were ruled apart on 2026-08-31 and only the second is foreclosed.

- **Two more again, this time behind stopping a render and deleting a history entry.** Same shape as the ones above: assumptions with no source in this project, neither from the Creator, and **neither a Blender question** — they do not go to Blender Research.

  **Whether a render running on a rented machine can be reached over a connection and told to end.** The Blender research settles Blender's own side — what an ended render leaves behind, and that no version offers a stop that waits for the frame — and says nothing about whatever Smoozy puts on the machine being reachable and tellable to end at all. That is a fourth ability of the same kind as the three already parked. [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) labels it, and what falls over is written up in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Rendering.sources.md): the user's stop from the queue, Servers' stop-and-clear on a removal, the group stop, and Telegram's stop from a phone — with a job at the wrong settings left to render its remaining frames on a machine billed by the hour.

  **Whether the machine's own copy of a history entry can be removed.** Reading the note a machine keeps against a job is already parked; taking something off it is not. [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) labels it, and its [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Queue%20&%20History.sources.md) records what falls over: the promise that deleting reaches both copies, and with it the guarantee that a deleted entry does not reappear on a later connection.

  A negative answer to either is a hole in the spec that leans on it rather than a narrowing of it. *(Raised by the scenario "The user stops a running job", round 1, 2026-08-09.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-23, and both halves were answered days before this line was written.**

  **The stop was answered at the promise level on 2026-08-18** — Fact 2, tried on rented machines of both kinds against a real Blender render rather than a stand-in. The stop reaches Blender's own workings and nothing restarts the render. **What no evidence covers is a heavy frame's latency to honour an interrupt**, which `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` §9 item 7 carries as an assumption behind its named deadline and re-poses as Fact 106.

  **Taking the machine's own copy of a history entry off was answered in part.** Fact 41 is recorded **ANSWERED IN PART** — the subject ends and the bytes stay — and `Preparation/Architecture/Queue & History.md` Section 18 item 5 keeps the remainder unresolved, with X3 saying outright that the widened erase has never been observed to complete.

  *(Closed by the architecture runner at the Part 6.3 re-sweep, 2026-08-23, which found this entry still reading* Due: at Architecture *for two questions the specs had already settled. Both specs were repaired the same day.)*

- **Queue & History's no-scenes-without-a-read rule read a little too broadly. Fixed on 2026-08-10.** The spec said an entry states no scene name at all where the file was never read; a job submitted again from a history entry carries its old scene name across even without one, because the name is Smoozy's own record rather than something read out of a file. The sentence is now bounded, and it says plainly that the picture size is not carried the same way and still needs a read.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.

- **Whether a machine can be read for the work left sitting on it.** Smoozy promises to offer to resume any untracked queued or running work a connection finds, and both the Servers and Queue & History specs rest on that being possible at all. It is an outside fact, so it comes from sourced research rather than memory (Rule 2). Settle at Architecture. If a machine turns out not to be readable that way, the promise is a hole in both specs — to be fixed there, not quietly reinterpreted. *(The rest of this thread closed with the Queue & History spec, which settles what stop-and-clear and leave-it-be each do, what each leaves behind in history, how resumed work rejoins the live queue, and what last-known record of a disconnected server's jobs Smoozy holds.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, and the promise holds.** Facts 1 and 40 answered the reading at the promise level on both machine kinds, `Preparation/Architecture/Queue & History.md` A1, A2 and A5 build the reading and the offer, and `Preparation/Architecture/Servers.md` 11.2 and 11.3 answer this side of it: the read runs on any connection **after the installation identity confirms**, tracked is told from untracked by the desktop's own record rather than by the machine's store, and **the offer is unfiltered** — no pre-check is made against what the current login could act on, because a refusal is discovered by trying. **One case the promise does not reach is now named:** after a username edit the supervisor refuses the new login, so no reading is taken and this is not a route back — the item below carries it.

  *(A second, stale* **Due:** at Architecture *line stood here under the closed one above and was removed by the architecture runner at the Part 6.3 re-sweep, 2026-08-23. It had survived the 2026-08-19 close, so an item that was done went on reading as owed.)*

- **Whether a queue put on a rented machine survives with nobody connected, and comes back after a reboot.** The [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) rests on it from its first paragraph: whatever Smoozy puts on the machine has to keep rendering with no connection to it, start again by itself after a reboot or a crash, and read back the line of work it was holding. Nobody in this project established any of that — no source was consulted and it did not come from the Creator, so it is an outside fact under Rule 2 and belongs with the other rented-machine assumptions at **Architecture**. It is not a Blender question and does not go to Blender Research. What it holds up is recorded in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Queue%20&%20History.sources.md): the whole of *When the machine goes down*, the automatic re-run, and the promise that nothing in the queue depends on the user being connected or on Smoozy being open. A negative answer is a hole in the spec rather than a narrowing of it. *(Raised by the Queue & History spec round 2, M17, 2026-08-03.)*

  **Due: done, and the promise holds with one named asymmetry.** Fact 1 answered it at the promise level on rented machines of both kinds: what Smoozy puts on the machine keeps rendering with nobody connected, starts again by itself after a reboot, and reads back the line of work it was holding. **The disk-full form came back no**, and the two machine kinds part company on it — `Preparation/Architecture/Queue & History.md` Q8 records the container healing by itself once space was freed while the systemd machine did not recover merely because space was freed, and `Preparation/Architecture/State, identity and recovery.md` §13 item 1 attributes the asymmetry to a per-unit setting Smoozy's own placement writes.

  *(Closed by the architecture runner at the Part 6.3 re-sweep, 2026-08-23. The spec's own sentence was corrected the same day, where it had stated the no-recovery half flatly for both kinds.)*

- **Whether a rented machine bills by the hour.** **CLOSED 2026-08-21 by reading Vast.ai's own billing page** — *"You are charged the base active rental cost for every second your instance is in the active/connected state"*, at a rate quoted in $/hr (read 2026-08-20). The rate is hourly, the charge is per second. Settles Vast.ai only; the blanket form is what is refuted, and the spec's sentence is corrected. Nothing about the feature moved. The [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) asserted it as a blanket fact and argued the half-hour idle message from it. It is an outside fact about providers rather than a Blender one, so it belongs at **Architecture** with the other provider facts (Rule 2), and it is labelled unresolved where it stands. The feature does not depend on the answer — a machine doing nothing costs money however it is billed — but finer billing would make the spec's stated reason wrong, and that reason would have to be corrected rather than left standing. *(Raised by the Queue & History spec round 2, M18, 2026-08-03.)*

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture, with the other provider facts.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02.** The item closed on 2026-08-21 by reading Vast.ai's own billing page, as its own heading says, and this line stood under that closure reading as owed.

- **What a re-run after an interruption can actually salvage.** The Queue & History spec settles that a machine coming back up re-runs the job it was interrupted on, and that the re-run covers only the frames that are not already there. Six Blender questions sit under that, and all now live in the **Frames** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). If any answer differs from what the specs assume, that is a hole in them, to be fixed there. Two consequences are already settled whatever research finds, both in [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md): the highest frame present is always re-rendered, and one of the questions decides whether Smoozy works out the re-run set at all or Blender does. *(From the Queue & History spec round 1, M13, M22 and M23; extended by the Rendering spec 2026-07-28 and the Telegram spec 2026-07-29.)*

  ~~**Due:** when the **Frames** topic's findings land.~~ **Frames closed and its answers were carried into the specs on 2026-08-10; the Due line was never updated.**
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **Whether changing a server's username still reaches work already running on it.** A server's username can be edited while jobs are running, and the Servers spec assigns this question to Queue & History — which cannot answer it, because it is not a product decision. It is an outside fact (Rule 2): whether a render started under one login can still be seen and stopped after Smoozy logs in as another. Settle at Architecture. Until it is answered, nothing states what the user actually gets — the jobs may stay visible and controllable, or become as unreachable as work on a machine Smoozy never knew about. *(Raised while writing the Queue & History spec, 2026-07-21; the Servers spec hands it over under "Editing a server".)*

  **Due: answered 2026-08-19, and the answer is the harder of the two outcomes this item anticipated.** Fact 60 is closed against a real render — **seen yes, stopped no** — but `Preparation/Architecture/Servers.md` 11.4 establishes that **Smoozy's own reach is narrower than *seen yes***: the supervisor refuses any login but the one the installation was placed under, and the process-listing route Fact 60 saw the work by is forbidden outright by the rule that nothing is ever found by name, pattern or process search. **So after a username edit the work is neither seen nor stoppable through any route this architecture has.** `State, identity and recovery` §2.3's fourth branch is taken, nothing is discarded, the supervisor-unavailable condition is raised and presented, and **the untracked-work offer is not a route back**. Nothing is destroyed: the work keeps running and editing the username back restores every route at once.

  **What that leaves is a Specs question and it is the Creator's.** The spec says a server may be edited while it has jobs on it *"but never quietly"*, then withholds the edit-with-jobs warning from a username change on the reason that it *"does not move the entry to another machine"* — while hedging, in the very next sentence, that whether such a change still reaches the work was not settled there. **The hedge is now settled against the reason.** The outcome is the same abandonment the host-or-port warning exists for, and it arrives with no warning at all. **Due: the Creator's ruling on whether that warning is extended to a username change, then one sentence in `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md` and the Rule 7 line.** The architecture is written either way and nothing is blocked; `Servers` Section 16 item 7 and Section 17 record the check and the decline.

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02.** The Architecture half was answered on 2026-08-19 and this line stood under it. **The live Due on this item is the one above** — the Creator's ruling on whether the edit-with-jobs warning is extended to a username change — and it is theirs, not Architecture's.

- **Whether Smoozy notices a dropped connection by itself.** The [Servers spec](../Specs/Servers.md) states that Smoozy learns of a drop without being told — the connection is its own to hold, so when it ends Smoozy knows — and everything Smoozy does about a lost connection starts at that moment. It is an outside fact about what the end of a connection over a network looks like to the side still holding it, and nobody in this project established it: no source was consulted and it did not come from the Creator (Rule 2). It is labelled unresolved where it stands, and what breaks if it is wrong is written out in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Servers.sources.md) — the list showing the true state, the reconnecting state, the repair that now carries on for as long as the app is open, the whole-app notification for a connection Smoozy could not restore, and every module written for the moment Smoozy knows it is offline. If Smoozy is not told, that is a hole in the spec rather than a narrowing of it. *(Raised by the scenario "The connection drops while rendering", round 1, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, and Fact 57 inverted the question rather than merely answering it.** Both ways a machine goes away — closing politely, and powered off from a provider's console — were announced to the caller **in the same second**, keepalives making no difference. **The case that needed answering is the user's own line**, detected in 56 seconds with keepalives on and **not detected at all for 21 minutes 45 seconds** with them off. So the promise holds, and what it rests on is the caller's own keepalive rather than anything the machine does — the machine had not learnt within the five minutes it was watched. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this said *never learns at all*.** The observed box ran `tcpkeepalive yes`, whose own manual says the server *will* notice; the kernel's defaults put that at about two hours plus eleven minutes of probes, and the run watched 5 min 10 s. `Preparation/Architecture/Facts.md`'s Fact 57 entry carries the sources. `State, identity and recovery` §8.5 already required a mechanism reporting a break without using the connection first, and `Preparation/Architecture/Servers.md` 2.1 supplies it. **Two things stay open and both are carried in that document rather than here:** every observation ran through a provider's proxy, and a genuinely abrupt loss is not posable on any rented machine.

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture, with the other connection questions.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02.** Answered on 2026-08-19 by Fact 57 and carried into `Servers` 2.1, as the line above says; the two things that stay open are carried in that document rather than here.

- **Every fact about Telegram itself is unconfirmed, and two of them hold the module up.** The [Telegram spec](../Specs/Telegram.md) rests entirely on how Telegram bots behave, and **every one of those facts was raised by an agent from its own knowledge**, with no source consulted, in the conversation that settled the module (2026-07-29). Each is written out in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Telegram.sources.md) with what breaks if it is wrong. These are fast-changing outside facts rather than Blender ones, so they belong to **Architecture** under Rule 2 — with one exception that cannot wait, below.

  **The two that cannot wait.** That only one holder of a bot can receive what the user sends or taps, while any number can send as it; and that any holder can read a conversation's pinned message at any time without receiving anything. Together they are the whole reason the spec can promise **one bot for life, covering every machine at once** — the machines pass notes to each other through the conversation, and the user never meets any of it. The Creator chose that promise over the honest alternative of one bot per machine run at the same time, so if either fact fails, the promise is a hole in the spec and the module goes back to the alternative rather than being quietly reinterpreted. Confirm these **before Architecture starts on this module**, not during it.

  The rest — how a bot is made and what the maker asks for, that a bot can edit a message it already sent, that it can send a short moving picture, the limit on what it can send, and that it can be made to answer one conversation and ignore everything else — each cost a feature or a rewording rather than the module. Two neighbouring questions are Blender's rather than Telegram's and sit in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md): whether the machine can turn finished frames into that moving picture at all, in the **Images** topic; and whether a render can be told to stop once the frame it is working on is finished, in **Frames**, added by the spec's round-1 audit on 2026-08-03. *(From the Telegram conversation, 2026-07-29.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The two that hold the module up were tested against a real bot and both hold**, so the one-bot-for-life promise stands and the fallback of one bot per machine is not needed. Three rented Linux machines held one token at once; the run is recorded in `Audits/Specs/Telegram/Behaviour tests 2026-08-12/Findings.md` with the scripts beside it, and every entry in the [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Telegram.sources.md) now says what was seen and what it does not cover.

  **Most of the rest went the same way, and three of them changed something.** The moving picture works but **must be a video file rather than a GIF**. The contact sheet has to show a **sample**, because a message holds ten pictures and no more. And the size limit is real and has a second half nobody had thought of — a picture is capped on its **dimensions** as well as its weight, so a large frame is refused for its shape first. Editing, pinning and the message pace were all comfortable: nothing in any run was refused for going too fast, and Telegram slowed down instead. **One is only half done**: that the bot can be made to answer one conversation and ignore everything else — the filtering is known to be possible, but no stranger's account was available to produce the thing being filtered, so that half stays for Architecture.

  **Telegram's own documentation could not have answered any of this**, which is worth recording so nobody repeats the search: it never addresses more than one program holding one token, for sending or receiving. **Two new problems the tests exposed are parked below**, both Architecture's, and both about machinery the user never meets.

- **Architecture must decide which single machine listens, and it is a requirement rather than a refinement.** Only one holder of the bot's token can receive at a time, and **the newest request always wins** — so a machine takes the listening slot simply by asking, and a machine that retries beats one that asks once. Two that both retry destroy each other. Three machines all polling for a minute gave **61 attempts, 60 conflicts and one success**, with nobody listening for about 92% of the time. Nothing in Telegram arbitrates this.

  The spec promises one bot covering every machine and that promise is sound — but it is sound only if exactly one machine is listening at any moment, and nothing in this project has said who decides. The free half is that hand-over costs nothing: a machine takes over instantly, and a machine that has died releases the slot by simply not asking again. *(From the behaviour tests, 2026-08-12.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19.** `Preparation/Architecture/Telegram.md` Section 3 settles it, and the rule came from two documents rather than one. `Rented-server runtime` §10 wrote the machine-side asking rules first — **a machine refused for conflict stands down rather than retrying**, which alone removes the observed disaster, and what tells it to try again is a **heartbeat in the shared note** rather than a timer, since the newest asker always wins and a periodic probe would take the slot from a healthy holder. That document recorded writing them as a seam, because the shape had assigned this to `Telegram`; **`Telegram` took them whole and unchanged rather than writing a second rule for one piece of machinery**, and added the conversation's own conduct: the note is one pinned **board**, created fresh at every hand-over, and staleness is read by comparing the heartbeat value for equality over a full interval **on the reader's own clock**, never by subtracting across two. **One half was nobody else's and is now written**: where a webhook holds the slot instead, removing a **stale registration** is `Telegram`'s, because one `409 Conflict` covers both a poll against a live registration and two pollers competing and nothing else can tell them apart.

- **The shared note the machines pass instructions through can lose one silently, so it needs a protocol rather than a place.** Two machines wrote different instructions to the same pinned message **ten milliseconds apart**; Telegram told both they had succeeded, and a third machine reading every 1.5 seconds only ever saw the later one. The earlier instruction was gone with nothing anywhere to show it had existed.

  Everything else about the note is sound: any holder can read it without disturbing the machine that is listening, any holder can edit any message the bot sent including one another holder sent, two machines editing the *same* message were never refused, and pinning is a stack so an older pin is hidden rather than destroyed. Reading is cheap — three machines at one read a second for a minute, no refusals. **The whole chain was run end to end** and works: with two machines a tap reached the right one in 1.4 seconds, and with three machines and two jobs, in 0.9 and 0.7 seconds. So what is missing is only the rule for writing when two machines want to write at once. *(From the behaviour tests, 2026-08-12.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19**, with the listening question and by the same two documents. The loss is made impossible by construction rather than guarded against: **a machine writes only into a message it created itself**, so the two-writers-one-object failure cannot occur; the board is written only by the slot holder; **a write is confirmed by reading it back**, since the service reports success for a write about to be overwritten and offers no conditional write; and **an instruction between machines is a request with an operation identity that is never the only record of itself** — durable at the sender before the update that caused it is confirmed, recorded at the addressee before it acts, and resent until the acknowledgement is observed. So a lost write costs a delay and never an instruction. Each machine speaks to the holder through **one re-used machinery message of its own**, pinned with the notification suppressed. **Corrected 2026-08-22** — this ended *so none of it leaves a trail in the user's conversation*, and that is false: suppressing the notification does not suppress the service message the pin itself posts into the conversation. What it costs the user is the item added below.

- **Make the decisions-record line mechanical, if the habit proves unreliable.** Rule 7 asks an agent to log every change made to an already-audited document. The tooling could enforce it rather than trust it — a check that fires whenever such a document is touched. Not worth building on speculation: it would only cover one of the tools in use, and the rule may well hold on its own. Build it only if the line actually starts getting skipped, and only with evidence that it is.

  **Due:** only if the line actually starts getting skipped, with evidence. Nothing schedules this.

- **The live readouts a queue entry shows.** What a rendering job shows is settled in the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md), and the Blender behaviours it leans on are now in the **Reports** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). One of them is not Blender's and stays here: that what the **machine itself** reports for a graphics card's memory differs from what Blender reports, because memory is allocated there for other things — whether that reading can be taken over a connection at all is **Architecture**'s, with the other hardware reads. *(From the queue-entry discussion, 2026-07-24.)*

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture, with the other hardware reads.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02.** The card's live memory read closed on 2026-08-20 with `Queue & History`, recorded in this file at the hardware-reads item below — *"the three reads closed in three different documents"* — and this line went on reading as owed for thirteen days.

- **The rest of the scenario family.** One scenario is written; the family it belongs to is not. The intended shape is a single journey — rent a machine, set it up, upload, render, download, remove the server — with the **connection dropping at a different point each time**, plus the two endings where the work finishes cleanly and the machine is destroyed, in either order. Write them before the Specs stage closes, keeping each to the one-thing-changed discipline in [Auditing](Auditing.md).

  **Due:** before the Specs stage closes.

- **Servers owed the control that clears a remembered machine password. Written in on 2026-08-10** — cleared where a server is edited, cleared without asking when the username changes, since a password belongs to a login, and gone when the server is removed. The item is kept only for the record of where the rule came from.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10. What the item said when it was parked: The whole-app **security** concern, settled on 2026-08-10, gives the user three choices when Smoozy asks for a password on a rented machine — use it once, hold it while the connection lasts, or remember it — and settles that a remembered one belongs to the **server** rather than to the key, so it is cleared where the server is edited and goes when the server is removed. [Servers](../Specs/Servers.md) does not yet say either of those things. It is one sentence in the editing section and one in the removal section, and it is named here rather than written in because that document has not been opened since. *(From settling the security concern, 2026-08-10.)*

  **Due:** whenever Servers is next audited, with the three other items already waiting for it.

- **Servers needed the same condition on the stop-and-clear offer that Queue & History states. Fixed on 2026-08-10.** Servers now says outright that a connected server with nothing queued and nothing running is forgotten with no jobs warning and no offer — a plain removal — where before it only said what happens when there are jobs. That also closes the candidate seam the scenario "The machine is destroyed", round 1, raised: whether the jobs warning appears at a removal when nothing was queued. It does not.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.

- **Servers described a partial stop-and-clear the wrong way round. Fixed on 2026-08-10.** It now carries the order Queue & History settled — the waiting jobs are cleared first, because stopping the render sets the machine off on the next waiting one — and says plainly that where the waiting jobs cannot be cleared the running render is not stopped at all. The half-done case this document can genuinely report is the other one.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.

- **The module map stated the stop-and-clear offer as unconditional. Fixed on 2026-08-10.** The Servers entry now carries the condition and says that a removal with nothing on the machine never reaches Rendering or Queue & History at all.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.


- **What the Download spec leans on, the part that stays here.** The third of its Blender questions was never about Images: **how the number of output files a job will produce is worked out** from the `.blend` and the settings it was submitted with. The Creator holds the method and will supply it, and it is **Architecture**'s to record; what the count is made of is settled in [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md). *(From writing the Download spec, 2026-07-22. The fourth question — what a fixed, non-relative output path means for the job's folder — closed on 2026-07-28: Smoozy redirects every destination under that folder. The other two questions closed with the Images topic, 2026-08-04 — see Due now while it is there, then the Download sources file.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-17, and confirmed at the round close on 2026-08-18.** `Preparation/Architecture/Job files and outputs.md` 7.2 and 7.3 carry the method — worked out frame by frame and never by multiplying, from the one reading of the file, with a collision counted once because it is two writes and one file. Row 128 holds the answer. **One correction came out of the round**: the count is the number of the inventory's per-file entries, and the bake and cache places the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-18 brought under the redirect sit in that same inventory as folders with no per-file entries and add nothing to the count, because nothing can name ahead of time what a simulation writes.

- **A user's job label ends up in a folder name on the machine.** The [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) settles that a job's optional label is carried in the name of its output folder. Nothing says what happens to a label containing characters a folder name cannot hold, or to two jobs given the same label. Almost certainly Architecture's to solve, but the commitment that creates the problem is made at Specs. *(Raised while assessing the Download spec, 2026-07-22.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-18, and it took the round to get there.** `Preparation/Architecture/Job files and outputs.md` 2.2 N3 composes a job output folder's name from **the job identity's own textual form and, where a label was given, a bounded rendering of that label** — the identity alone carrying uniqueness, the label bounded in bytes inside 2.3's budget, and **what cannot enter a name substituted rather than refused**, with the full label staying authoritative in the job's record. So both halves this item asked are answered: an impossible character is substituted rather than refusing the job, and two jobs given one label get distinct names because the identity is in each. A job record directory's name is the identity alone.
