  **And the landing side is closed too, 2026-08-20.** That answer was about the folder's name **on the machine**; the same label reaches the user's own computer when the job's outputs come home, on a system whose name rules are not the machine's. `Preparation/Architecture/Download.md` 5.5 P10 composes the landing name **by the same rule against the receiving system** — the identity's textual form plus a bounded rendering of the label, judged by the platform adapter's own name check and judged again like any other composed name — so a label legal on Linux and unwritable as given on the destination (a trailing period on Windows is the case that will bite) costs the label's rendering and never the folder or the files, and two jobs given one label land in two folders because the identity is in the name. **Nothing parses a folder name back into anything**, in either direction.

  **How it got there is worth keeping.** The document as first written struck the label out of the name entirely, citing a rule that forbids deriving an **identity** from what the user typed — which a name made of identity *and* label does not do. The runner found this item unlifted when it opened the round, routed it to all three auditors as a named judgment, and all three converged: a fix inside Architecture rather than a promise the spec has to give up. **It is the clearest case yet for reading this file when a round opens and not only when one closes**, since nothing else in the process was looking at it.

  **One residue is parked below** — what the user sees where a label was substituted, which is not this document's.

- **What a Blender render failure usually is.** In the Creator's experience Blender is predictable: it either renders or it does not, and when it does not it is insufficient GPU memory about 99% of the time. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-21) — a fact about the Creator's own projects rather than a documented Blender behaviour, so it stands on their word under Rule 2 and needs no confirming. It is also **kept away from the researcher**: on the Creator's ruling of 2026-07-30, the register asks what can cause a render to fail at all, rather than what happens when memory runs out, because the narrower question hands over the expected answer. The question sits in the **Reports** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). The whole-app concern *how a failure reaches the user* is settled (2026-07-27): Smoozy shows the reason the machine gave and does not diagnose the render. What is left here is what comes after the answer — whether knowing that nearly every failure has one cause should change what the message says. That is a Design question with a Blender answer.

  ~~**Due:** when the **Reports** topic's findings land.~~ **Reports closed on 2026-08-06 and again on 2026-08-30 with question 14; the Due line was never updated.**
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **Whether a machine permits elevated access. The other half of this closed on 2026-08-10.** The [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) settles that when a read is refused for lack of permission, Smoozy may offer to retry with elevated access where the machine allows, and [Server Setup](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) reaches the same moment when an install is refused. That Smoozy *offers* is its own decision. **What that costs the user is now settled**, under *Security* in the [Whole app spec](../Specs/Whole%20app.md): Smoozy asks for a password at the moment a step needs one and never before, and the user chooses whether it is kept for that one action, for as long as the connection lasts, or until they clear it. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-10). **What is still open is the machine's own half** — whether a given machine permits elevated access at all — which is an outside fact for Architecture. Label: **unresolved**. **It grew heavier on 2026-07-29**: the [Server Setup spec](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) leans on it far harder than browsing does. A browse refused for lack of permission loses one folder; an install refused may mean the machine cannot be set up at all, which takes the whole product with it. *(From writing the File Browser spec, 2026-07-23.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-20.** `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 2.2 P5–P8 answers Row 180 on Fact 27, which came back **answered at both weights and different by machine kind**. So the offer has **three outcomes rather than two**: passwordless elevation, offered and run with nothing asked; a **container under a non-root login, where there is no offer at all**, because Fact 27 established there is no elevation route there and a password prompt would stand in front of a door with no lock; and a systemd machine, where accepting starts `Server Setup` §9's flow. **That is the negative this item said would remove the retry offer entirely rather than weaken it, arriving for one machine kind rather than for all of them.** The heavier weight this item warned about — the ready action — was closed by `Server Setup` on 2026-08-19. **One thing rides on rather than blocks it:** Fact 27's traps are carried at `File Browser` Section 13 item 7 — the `docker`-group silent root-equivalence on one image, and provider images not surveyed.

- **How Smoozy knows which files a running or waiting job depends on.** The [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) settles that Smoozy warns before a delete, move, or rename that would disturb a file a live job needs, and leans on Queue & History for which jobs are live. But which files a given job depends on — its `.blend` and every asset it references, some placed by Dependency Upload & Remap — is knowledge that spans those modules, and no document yet says where it lives or how the set is worked out. **Half of it now exists**: the [Dependency Upload & Remap spec](../Specs/Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md) settles that Smoozy keeps a record of what each of a file's references was resolved to, held on the user's computer. What is still open is whether that record is what the live-job warning reads, and what it does for a job whose file was never checked, since nothing is checked at a submission. The stress test adds three cases the same rule must settle: a checked main or nested file replaced after the check, one shared asset replaced while several jobs need it, and a dependency selected later to render as a new root. Architecture must decide when a record becomes stale, whether a live job holds a snapshot or a mutable file, and when a fresh root check is required. Settle it in the module map and at Architecture. The render-work model sharpens this: since **a job is always one scene** (see the scene-selection item above), the set a job depends on is a *scene's* dependencies rather than the whole file's, and because **scenes can share assets**, the sibling scene-jobs of one all-scenes batch may depend on overlapping sets — so the same asset can be needed by several live jobs at once. Whether rendering one scene actually pulls only that scene's assets is a Blender fact rather than a safe assumption, and sits in the **Dependencies** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). The reload half of replacement now sits in **Reading a file 11**; it does not answer Architecture's ownership and invalidation rule. *(From writing the File Browser spec, 2026-07-23; the scene-dependency connection added 2026-07-24; sharpened by the stress test on 2026-08-07.)*

  **Due:** the Blender half is done, 2026-08-10 — both answers are in Dependency Upload & Remap, and what a replaced picture does to a running render is now in File Browser beside the warning it justifies. **Still at Architecture for the ownership rule.** **Reading a file 11 and Dependencies 19 were both answered on 2026-08-09.** Reading a file 11 settles what a later render uses after a file already read is replaced, and the answer is not uniform: a changed picture, an image-sequence frame and an external script all reach the very next render with nothing done at all, while a linked blend-file, a movie clip, an archive and a packed image do not. Dependencies 19 settles that different spellings of one path are one library while a symbolic link or a second mount makes two, and that letter case decides it differently on the two operating systems. ~~Architecture still owns when a record goes stale and who holds it.~~ **Stale, struck 2026-09-02** — the paragraph immediately below closed exactly that on 2026-08-20, and this *Still at Architecture* line went on reading as owed underneath its own closure.

  **Done, 2026-08-20, and the ownership rule this item was left open for is closed.** `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 8.3 W5–W7 answers Row 195 on two supplied readings — `Queue & History` Q2's live set, and the manifest's `.blend` plus the per-job resolved set — and settles the three things this item said Architecture owned. **A live job's set is a snapshot, fixed at submission and never re-derived**, because the manifest and the remap material are immutable, so *what does this job depend on* has one durable answer for the job's life; **it goes stale only by Smoozy's own recorded acts and never by watching the machine**; and **a fresh root check is required only where a file is submitted as a new root**, which is `Dependency Upload & Remap`'s own rule and no invalidation at all. The three stress-test cases fall out of that: a checked file replaced after the check does not move the snapshot, one shared asset replaced while several jobs need it warns for **every holding job**, and a dependency later selected as a new root is the fresh-check case. **The question this item kept asking loudest is answered plainly rather than patched:** a job whose file was never checked has no resolved set, so the warning covers its manifest's `.blend` and its job folder — the spec's own *the set Smoozy has may be smaller than the set the job really uses* arriving at its widest. **The job's own folder is the architecture's addition**, required by the spec's *the folder and files a running job is writing to* and named by neither supplier. **One limit is declared rather than papered over**: the comparison reads recorded paths, so a file reached by an alias the record does not spell fires no warning — modelling the machine's link structure was declined as the same trap as modelling a folding rule.

- **Whether a file deleted on a rented machine is truly unrecoverable.** The [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) warns that a server-side deletion is permanent — no recycle bin or undo on the machine. That Smoozy keeps no copy is its own decision; whether the machine itself offers any recovery is an outside fact (filesystem and operating-system behaviour, Rule 2), labelled an assumption. Confirm before anything leans harder on it than the delete warning does. *(From writing the File Browser spec, 2026-07-23.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-20**, and it was answered before the document that needed it. **Fact 31 came back no on 2026-08-13** — no trash tooling, no trash folder, no snapshotting filesystem — so the spec's *is gone* wording stands unchanged rather than softening, and `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 7.1 M1 records it as observed rather than assumed. **The limit rides with it**: one machine, one filesystem, carried at that document's Section 13 item 4. Had it come back the other way the wording would have had to soften, which would have been Specs' work and not Architecture's.

- **The compositor and file outputs — the heaviest set of research questions in the project.** The product side is settled: the conversation the Creator asked for was held on 2026-07-28 and produced [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md). The questions are now in the **The compositor**, **File output nodes**, **Writing** and **Images** topics of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). Two things about this set do not live there and are worth keeping here.

  The Creator has asked that **every Blender fact behind that document be confirmed with more rigour than anything else in this project**, because the file output node's behaviour changes from version to version. Nothing in it is to be treated as settled fact.

  And **the Creator's own recollection must never reach a researcher.** It sits in that document's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/What%20a%20job%20writes.sources.md), complete with version numbers and how each behaviour is thought to work — exactly the hypothesis blind framing exists to withhold, held loosely, and possibly wrong. *(From the compositor conversation, 2026-07-28.)*

  ~~**Due:** as each of its four topics' findings land — **File output nodes** and **The compositor** both closed on 2026-08-03, and **Images** closed on 2026-08-04, all three held to the extra rigour asked for. **Writing** is the one still owed.~~

  **All four have landed. Writing closed on 2026-08-30**, all seven steps, with questions 13 and 14 folded in and its comparison check ruling sixteen things. **So the trigger this item was waiting on has fired for the last time**, and what the Creator asked for — every Blender fact behind `What a job writes` confirmed with more rigour than anything else in this project — is now testable rather than aspirational. **The rigour asked for was met on both of Writing's new questions**: question 14 ran two separate instruments and kept them apart, saying twice that the system-call trace settles Linux alone, and its citation check repeated the resolution measurement the whole answer rests on rather than believing it.

  **The Creator's own recollection is still quarantined and that half of this item stands unchanged**, for as long as anybody may write a sheet for this material.

  **Due: now** — not for more research, but for the carry-in. **The answers of this round have not been swept into the documents that rest on them**, which is the item added at the top of this file at the same close. This item is what says where the rigour bar sits when somebody does that sweep.

- **What a render produces where a missing image is feeding something other than colour.** The Images topic tested a colour image and named this one untested — see `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Images.md`, question 2. A displacement map, a bump map, a roughness map and a mask are all ordinary work, so the untested case is the common one rather than a corner.

  One question, drafted blind and ready for a sheet:

  1. What does a render produce where an image a file points at is missing, and that image is feeding something other than colour?

  It belongs in the **Images** topic, beside question 2, which is its neighbour. Two promises rest on the answer and both are written up in the [What a job writes sources](../Specs/Sources/What%20a%20job%20writes.sources.md), which is where what breaks if it comes back badly is recorded: that document's own frame-preview rung, and [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md)'s promise that the preview says the textures loaded, which now points there for its limits. Nobody answers it from memory, and nobody hands a researcher what this project hopes the answer is. *(Raised by the scenario "The user adds a texture and renders again", round 1, 2026-08-08.)*

  **It sat here for a day with no number and no row in [Coverage](../Blender%20Research/Coverage.md)**, which is exactly the failure that section was built to catch and the second time it has happened. It is now **Images 9** in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md), with a Coverage row of its own, and the wording above went onto the sheet word for word. Recorded rather than quietly fixed: parking a drafted question is not the same as registering it, and whoever parks the next one should do both.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10 — carried into What a job writes, Queue & History and Telegram, each of which has narrowed its promise to a texture that was feeding colour. **Images 9 was answered on 2026-08-09** and the answer is the unfavourable one this item feared. A missing image feeding something other than colour does **not** come out magenta — the substitute is handed over before anything knows what the image feeds, so the render shows a plausible picture with one property silently wrong. A missing bump map produces no bump at all; a missing displacement map changes the object's size uniformly; a missing normal map tilts the shading everywhere; a missing alpha renders fully opaque; the compositor and geometry nodes get zero instead. And **4.5 says nothing whatever about it** while 5.0 and 5.1 print an error and 5.2 a warning and an error — at exit 0 with the frame written, in every version. So the frame preview cannot be promised to show that the textures loaded, and on 4.5 there is nothing to read anywhere. See [Comparison/Images.md](../Blender%20Research/Comparison/Images.md), question 9.

- **What values a resolution percentage takes, and what a render does with one outside them.** Asked as **Settings 14 and 15** in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md), where both are worded blind and ready for a sheet, and owed by all four versions in [Coverage](../Blender%20Research/Coverage.md) since Settings had already closed.

  The [Render Settings spec](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) **cannot finish its *Resolution percentage* section until the answer lands**, and carries a marker saying so. The percentage is the only handle that page gives on how big a render comes out, so a narrow answer takes away the user's only route inside Smoozy to a render bigger than the file asks for; what breaks is written up in that spec's [sources file](../Specs/Sources/Render%20Settings.sources.md) rather than repeated here.

  **The Creator's ruling of 2026-07-30 is narrowed, not reversed.** What the percentage means stays settled and stays unasked; the range of values it takes was never covered by it, and the register says so where the ruling is recorded, so the next researcher-runner does not read a blanket ban and go past this. **The Creator's own statement about the range is quarantined** in that sources file and must not reach a researcher, in the wording of the questions or anywhere else. Nobody answers it from memory. *(Raised by the scenario "The user renders the same project on a new machine", round 1, 2026-08-08, and ruled in by the Creator as one of that round's two decisions.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10 — the *Resolution percentage* section is finished, and Smoozy's own box now holds to the range Blender accepts rather than letting a value be silently corrected. **Settings 14 and 15 were answered on 2026-08-09.** The box takes whole numbers **1 to 32767**, starting at 100 — so a render larger than the file asks for is possible, and the Creator's recollection was right. Dragging stops at 100; anything above it has to be typed. A number outside the range is **silently pulled to the nearest allowed one**, at exit 0, with nothing printed — so a wrong value sent from outside is swallowed without a signal. A fraction is refused outright. Under a pixel, the render refuses with *Image too small* and writes nothing. And 40000% neither refuses nor warns: it simply starts a render of 53,284 tiles, which is the paid-night case. All of it the same in all four versions on both machines. See [Comparison/Settings.md](../Blender%20Research/Comparison/Settings.md), questions 14 and 15.

- **Whether Smoozy should let a job it knows will write no files run at all.** [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md) settles that Smoozy says so before submitting, and again on the queue entry where the file was not read first. It stops there, so an artist can be told and still watch a machine they are paying for render for six hours and keep nothing. The Creator ruled on 2026-07-28 that this cannot be settled yet, because **nobody knows what such a render does leave behind** — it still runs, and something comes of it. So the research question comes first, and the product decision follows it: if what it leaves is worth having, the warning is enough; if it is not, Smoozy should be stopping the job rather than watching it burn hours. The question is in the **Writing** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md); the Creator's own guess at the answer is quarantined in the [What a job writes sources](../Specs/Sources/What%20a%20job%20writes.sources.md) and must not reach a researcher. **When:** the product decision after the answer lands, in that document or in **Rendering**. *(Raised by an audit of the compositor change set, 2026-07-28; scoped by the Creator the same day.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10 — the research landed and the Creator ruled on 2026-08-06. What such a render leaves behind is nothing anyone can keep: the picture dies with Blender and the temporary folder is empty throughout. On that, Smoozy warns and lets the user go ahead, with the option to stop. Settled in [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md), which owns it, and **Rendering** now points at it instead of carrying the question.

- **Rendering from a copy, as a way of redirecting without touching the artist's file — closed 2026-08-15, and the *When* line below is spent.** [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md) promises that everything a job writes lands under the job's folder and that the artist's `.blend` is left as they uploaded it, and whether an output path can be imposed on a render without being written into a file is unresolved. The Creator's idea, if it cannot: Smoozy makes a temporary copy of the `.blend` with the redirected paths in it and renders from that, so the artist's own file is never touched. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-28) as an idea to weigh, not as a decision. Two things to settle with it. A `.blend` can carry paths written relative to where the file sits — already open on the redirect side — so if that is so, a copy in a different folder may not find the assets, and the copy would have to sit beside the original; that is an outside fact and unconfirmed. And what such a copy means for everything that names the file afterwards: history, *show outputs*, and the file browser all speak of the `.blend` the user uploaded. **When:** at Architecture, where the redirect's own feasibility is settled. *(Raised by the Creator while reviewing the Rendering spec, 2026-07-28.)* **Closed 2026-08-15 — see the entry below, which is the record; this one is kept only because that entry names the two things it says would have to be settled.** *(Marked by the architecture runner, 2026-08-18, after the before-audit gate on `Dependency Upload & Remap` found two documents calling this idea parked.)*

- **Rendering from a copy, closed.**

  **Due: done, 2026-08-15 — the idea leaves this file, and it leaves alive rather than dead.** *(The entry above it is kept, marked closed, because this one names the two things it says would have to be settled.)* The condition it was parked against has been answered: the redirect **can** be imposed without writing into the artist's file, because a script handed to Blender at launch reaches a file output node's values and the render that follows uses them (*Writing 9*, hand-confirmed in all four versions on the Linux machine). So the copy is no longer a fallback waiting on a fact. It is one of two available routes, and which route Smoozy uses is a decision nobody has taken — carried by **Row 120** in `Preparation/Architecture/Rows/What a job writes.md`, together with the two things this entry said would have to be settled if the copy were chosen. A list of open threads has nowhere to hold a live design option that a row already owns.

- **Where a job's folder actually sits on the machine.** [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) gives every job a folder and says only that it is the parent-most one; nothing anywhere says where on the machine it goes. That mattered less while the `.blend`'s own output path chose the disk. It matters now, because [What a job writes](../Specs/What%20a%20job%20writes.md) redirects everything into that folder, so Smoozy is choosing the disk on the artist's behalf — and a rented machine often has a small system disk beside a large one meant for work. Renders are big. **When:** at Architecture, unless it turns out the user should be offered the choice, which would make it a Specs question and Queue & History's. *(Raised by an audit of the compositor change set, 2026-07-28.)*

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture — unless the user should be offered the choice, which makes it Specs' and Queue & History's.~~ **DONE 2026-08-20; marked 2026-09-02 at the Architecture stage close.** `Preparation/Architecture/Job files and outputs.md` **L2** answers it and the user is **not** offered the choice, so it stayed Architecture's and never became a Specs question. **Smoozy chooses the disk by reading, not by convention:** the candidate set is the writable locations `Server Setup` was given, a **per-path free-space reading** is taken against each candidate's own path, the **available** figure is read rather than the free one, and **the largest available figure wins**. **No figure is ever derived from a filesystem list**, which is Fact 28's trap — one container's plain reading returned 51 filesystems, 48 of them memory-backed, one of them the host's own disk showing space the rental may not touch. **L4 is why the reading is load-bearing rather than tidy:** a rental's second filesystem may be a quota on one disk or a real second device, both are observed, and on one seller the large mount is **eight times the root** — so the conventional root would be the wrong choice by a factor of eight. **L4a places the package area on a separate mount where one is available**, so a render filling its own output disk no longer starves the machinery recording what that render did.

- **What it takes to make a machine's cards available to a render.** The promise has a home — the [Rendering spec](../Specs/Rendering.md) owns making the machine's GPUs available, at the moment a job is run rather than when the machine is set up — and the question is now in **The machine** topic of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md), together with the Creator's rule that a render must always use every card the machine has. *(Raised while writing the Render Settings spec, 2026-07-27; given its home by the Rendering spec, 2026-07-28.)*

  ~~**Due:** when **The machine** topic's findings land.~~ **The machine closed and its answers were carried into the specs on 2026-08-10; the Due line was never updated.**
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **What the Render Settings spec needs from Blender Research.** Its questions are in the **Settings**, **Frames** and **Reading a file** topics of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md), which also records the three facts the Creator holds as settled and asked not be researched. Two things stay here. **How each of the settings the spec offers is actually reached** — read out of a file and imposed on a render — is **Architecture**'s need rather than a Blender question, and none of the research above supplies it. And one register question cannot be answered by an isolated pass at all: **what a Blender version mismatch actually costs**, which the register marks and explains. The spec's version warning waits on it, so a thin answer blunts that promise rather than breaking it. *(From writing the Render Settings spec, 2026-07-27; updated by Round 1 audit, 2026-07-29.)*

  ~~**Due:** when the **Settings**, **Frames** and **Reading a file** topics' findings land;~~ **all three closed, and Settings was opened again on 2026-08-30 for questions 18 and 19; the Due line was never updated.** The how-each-setting-is-reached half stays at Architecture.
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **What happens to settled render settings when the `.blend` changes underneath them.** The [Render Settings spec](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) has Pre-load Settings read the file on the machine, after which the user tunes what it found. Nothing says what happens if that file is then replaced — a new upload over the top, or a save the user made mid-edit, which the [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) already handles for the transfer itself. The values on screen would describe a file that no longer exists, and the job would be submitted against the new one. **Two cases of this are now settled in [Render Settings](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) (Round 1, M5):** both the file changing mid-edit and the user changing the Blender version drop the file's values and keep the user's typed values, exactly as at a reconnect. What remained open was **how Smoozy notices a file change on the machine** while connected and watching; the spec's marker for it was rewritten on 2026-08-23 as a plainly stated limit, and the spec now says it, with no marker, under *The `.blend` is replaced or changed on the machine*, settled at Architecture. *(Until 2026-10-24 this sentence read "What remains open is how Smoozy notices a file change on the machine while connected and watching, which is marked … and parked", the marker named in its bracketed form.)* *(Updated by Render Settings spec round 1, 2026-07-29.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-19, by `Render Settings`.** Row 143 is answered at that document's 1.5. **What counts as changed is the four-value file identity `Job files and outputs` R10 already fixes — size, inode, modification time and change time together — compared afresh; the watch is only the trigger and the comparison is the test.** That is what survives the two traps: **a watch on a file follows the inode, so a save-by-rename kills it silently** (Fact 19), and a killed transfer leaves a piece of the file under the final name with no marker of any kind. The watching itself is free — 0.08 seconds of processor over 55 — with its card case still unposed. **The spec carries no marker for it** — on 2026-08-23 the architecture runner rewrote it, outside any round, as the limit the spec now states plainly, with its decisions-record line (`Audits/Specs/Render Settings/Decisions record.md`, *Part 6.4 of the standing check*). *(Until 2026-10-24 this read "The spec's marker still stands and clearing it is the runner's, with its own decisions-record line", the marker named in its bracketed form.)* **One seam came with it**, recorded in this file above: R12's *at exactly two moments and at no others* does not contemplate the display-only comparison this page makes.

- **What a `.blend` can depend on, and whether every one of them survives the trip.** The questions are in the **Dependencies** and **Reading a file** topics of the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). The spec can be written ahead of the answers, but every promise resting on one of them is unfinished until it lands.

  One thing is deliberately **not** in that set, so the two research efforts do not overlap or answer each other differently: **add-ons**. A `.blend` can need one to render, but it is not a file this module goes and finds on the user's computer, and it is owned by [Server Setup](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md) with its own topic. A researcher answering the dependency questions should not fold add-ons into them. *(From the dependency conversation, 2026-07-28; updated by the spec's Round 1 audit, 2026-07-29.)*

- **Every scenario's premise was checked against the specs on 2026-08-08, and five of the fourteen described something Smoozy cannot do.** All five had the same fault: the user clicks to start a render job and Smoozy then uploads the `.blend`. Uploading is its own act, done first in the file browser, and [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) states plainly that submitting starts no transfer. What was done about each: *The connection drops while uploading* was **retired** and its scenario file deleted, after a full round of five auditors proved it unplayable. *The server disk fills up during upload*, *The user deletes the local file during upload* and *The user quits Smoozy before a job starts* were **rewritten** — the situations were good and only the opening act was wrong. *The user queues multiple jobs overnight* was **left alone and marked not for replay**, because it had already been audited and its round is what put the missing rule into the Rendering spec; editing it now would change a story auditors have read.

  Four new scenarios were written the same day, grounded in the iteration loop the Dependency Upload & Remap module exists for. **Then the Creator cut the folder from eighteen to eight**, on the grounds that a scenario round is expensive, the project has to ship, and most of what was there asked one question several times over. Ten were deleted. What survives, and the full reasoning behind the cut, is written into [About this folder](../Scenarios/About%20this%20folder.md) — it is kept there rather than here because the folder will grow again and the same mistakes are easy to repeat. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-08).

  **The runner was proposed to check a scenario's premise before opening a round, and the Creator ruled against it on 2026-08-08.** The runner should just run. Making sure a scenario describes something Smoozy can actually do belongs to whoever writes it, with the Creator, before it ever reaches a round — adding it to the runbook would put the check at the last moment and on the wrong person. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-08). It is written into [About this folder](../Scenarios/About%20this%20folder.md), where scenarios are written, rather than into the runner's steps.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-08.

- **An interrupted transfer wants a thread seam rather than a scenario.** Five scenarios were cut on 2026-08-08 for asking one question between them: a transfer stops partway — the connection drops, the app closes, a disk fills, at either end — and the user wants to carry on without re-sending what already crossed. That behaviour runs through [File Browser](../Specs/File%20Browser.md), [Download](../Specs/Download.md), [Dependency Upload & Remap](../Specs/Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md), [Servers](../Specs/Servers.md) and [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md), and no single document owns it, which is exactly what a thread seam is for. One round would cover what those five were reaching for, at a fraction of the cost. Two known disagreements are already waiting for it, both parked above: the reason File Browser gives for an upload never resuming, and what the file browser shows while its connection is down.

  **Due:** at the Specs stage close, with the other seams — or sooner if the Creator wants it.

- **The File Browser spec gave a reason for not resuming an upload that does not hold. The reason was corrected on 2026-08-10; the product question underneath it is still open.** The spec had argued it from the explicit-connect model — nothing in Smoozy reaches out on its own — and that does not hold: [Servers](../Specs/Servers.md) has Smoozy notice a dropped connection itself and keep repairing it, unasked, for as long as the app is open, and a download set to come home automatically picks up again on that repaired connection. The reason now given is what an upload actually is: a request made at a moment rather than a standing arrangement, and a moment that has passed is not one Smoozy renews on the user's behalf. **The behaviour is unchanged.**

  **What was still open was whether it should carry on by itself** where Smoozy repaired the connection and the file has not changed. It was genuinely arguable and was argued both ways: one auditor called a home line dropping the user's own business under Rule 8, another that Smoozy is already holding the connection and would be finishing work the user asked for. It leans on the unresolved question of whether a part-sent transfer can be continued at all, already parked at Architecture. *(Raised by the retired scenario "The connection drops while uploading", 2026-08-08; see `Audits/Specs/The connection drops while uploading/What happened.md`.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled that it does carry on**, and the [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) now says so: where Servers repairs the connection, the uploads the drop interrupted pick up where they stopped, without the user asking again. The old reason — that an upload is a request made at a moment and a moment that has passed is not one Smoozy renews — is written up in that spec as reversed rather than deleted, because the argument the other way is the one that won: Smoozy is holding a live connection it repaired on its own and not using it. It lasts only as long as the app is open, and only where the file has not changed. **One thing this opens is named in the item below it**: a manual download is still governed by the reason that has just been overturned for uploads.

- **Nothing said what the file browser shows while its server's connection is down. Settled on 2026-08-10.** The listing stays on screen and says plainly that it is not live — it is not emptied, because throwing away what the user was reading helps nobody — and everything needing the machine is unavailable rather than failing on a click: opening a folder Smoozy has not already seen, uploading, downloading, deleting, renaming, moving, copying, making a folder. The free-space reading and each file's details read as stale too, and all of it returns when the connection does, by the user's own act or by Servers' repair. It matches what the server itself reads as while the line is down, so the two parts of the app do not disagree about one machine.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-10.

- **Nobody has looked at whether a squircle reads as different from a circle at the size a badge is actually drawn.** *(Raised by an auditor of `Preparation/Design/Whole app.md`, round 1, 2026-09-02, and parked by that round rather than answered.)*

  `Preparation/Design/Whole app.md` settles that a badge is a squircle and that every mark of state in Smoozy is a circle, so shape carries the difference between identity and state before the colour is read. **That is what frees the badge palette**: the eight hues sit 45 degrees apart around the whole wheel and include green, red and amber only because colour no longer has to do the identity-against-state work. Two earlier palettes squeezed into one narrow arc were thrown out for near neighbours that could not be told apart. `Preparation/Design/Design values.md` carries the same dependency for its eight tokens, and in the collapsed rail the badge is the whole row with no label beside it.

  **The claim is now labelled an assumption in the document** and recorded in `Preparation/Design/Sources/Whole app.sources.md` with what it does not cover — the size, the screen density and the theme it was judged at — and with what breaks: the eight-hue palette and the badge-only rail's justification.

  **It is answered by looking at the mockup's own badge at its real size on real screens, and by nothing else.** It is not a research question and it needs no machine.

  **Due:** at the Design close, or whenever anyone next looks at the drawing on a real screen.




### The desktop shell offers no quit-ask on one macOS route, and the run-mark's clause asks for one

*Added 2026-09-03 by the build runner, at the close of Build step 2. Tagged for `Preparation/Architecture/State, identity and recovery.md` §1.1 **I2**.*

**I2 gives the run-mark two writes and no third**, and the second is *"written durably **at the moment the user asks to quit** as (clean, this run's identity) — **the ask, not the exit**, so D6's second sentence is honoured rather than excepted — and **nothing is written on the way out**."*

**On macOS the selected shell raises no ask for one of the two quit routes.** Observed on the Creator's Mac on 2026-09-03: the application was built, run from its bundle, and asked to quit by another program; the events were `Ready`, a focus event, and then `Exit`. **No `ExitRequested` arrived at all.** The window's own close button takes the other route and does raise one.

**What Build did, and it is the *what holds* rather than the missing thing.** The mark is written on all three events — the window's close request, the shell's exit request, and the exit — so **every route writes**, and writing the same value more than once costs nothing. **What is narrower than I2 asks for is the macOS application-quit route alone**, where the write happens at the exit because that is the earliest moment the shell offers.

**Why it is worth a line here rather than only in a Build record.** I2 is `State, identity and recovery`'s clause and its reason is D6's — a fact is written when it becomes true. A clause whose promise is kept on one route and narrowed on another should say so in its own document rather than only where a builder happened to meet it.

**What it costs if left as it is: nothing today.** The value written is the same and the moment is later by the length of one shutdown. What it would cost is a crash *during* that shutdown reading as an unclean end at the next launch, which fires `Reporting a problem` §9's offer at a user who quit normally.

**Due:** whenever I2 is next opened. It blocks nothing and no work waits on it.

So the block is five, of which four sit here.)*

Whether any single operation renames a file and refuses an existing target — on Mac, Windows and Linux.**~~ **MOVED 2026-09-03, to where its own Verdict said to put it.** It was filed here as a new item while its own text said it belonged beside the Windows list's *publication rename* thread, which is that Verdict's instruction and not a preference. **It now sits inside that thread, in *Due now* above**, with the two things that thread does not say. Nothing about it changed in the move.

**2. Whether a file opened for reading only can be flushed to the device on Windows.** From the same round. **The claimed consequence — that every download on Windows fails just before publication — was disproved and the finding did not survive**: the result of that call is discarded by the loop, and the Windows runner's own download test passes through that exact call. **What is left is the bare question and it changes nothing today.**
  **Due:** nothing waits on it. It is here so the question is not asked a third time from memory.

**3. What a directory entry's own flush costs when it is not performed.** From `Job files and outputs` Round 1, U-1. The plan now says which three parts of the durability rule a write over the connection keeps and states plainly that the fourth is not performed and that **nothing has established whether it can be**. **What it costs is written where it is owed**: a record published and reported durable can be absent after a power cut, which is a third state `Rendering` Row 2 says does not exist, and an attempt record lost that way is re-rendered whole at the user's expense.

The flush operation takes an open file handle, the subsystem has no operation that opens a directory and returns one, and **whether a server would answer a flush against such a handle is unread.** Nothing is assumed here either way.
  **What would settle it is a reading** — the file-transfer protocol's own published extension text, and OpenSSH's server. **Not a rental**, so the ruling of 2026-08-31 does not reach it.

**4. What shapes a writable location can arrive in from `Server Setup`.** Whether a location handed to the layout can carry a relative segment, or be a link to somewhere else. **This belongs to `Server Setup` and it is step 6's**, so it is parked for that step rather than answered now. **What breaks while it is open** is that a root can in principle be placed under an area the machine wipes at every boot, past a test that only compares text. **The half that needs no outside fact is already closed** — a candidate path holding a `.` or `..` segment is refused, which is Smoozy's own arithmetic against Smoozy's own rule. **Nothing here knows what a link does on a rented Linux machine**, and nothing pretends to.
  ~~**Due:** step 6, when `Server Setup` is built.~~ **FIRED AND NOT SERVICED. Marked 2026-09-04, at Build's step 6 close, and confirmed by opening the code rather than on a claim.** Step 6 built `Server Setup` and its round-1 audit ran and closed; neither touched this. `App/Job files and outputs/src/layout.rs` still refuses a candidate path holding a `.` or `..` segment — Smoozy's own arithmetic, the half that needs no outside fact — and **still says at that site that the other half is not closed**: a candidate that is a link to somewhere else passes the same text comparison, and what a link does on a rented Linux machine is established nowhere in this project. **What breaks is unchanged**: a root under an area the machine wipes at every boot, and after one reboot the job records, the manifests and the attempt records are gone.
  **Due, re-pointed:** whenever `Server Setup`'s writable-location hand-over is next opened — a further round on that module, or the machine Build rents to watch a placement run for real.

The desktop's choice still compares text; what that leaves is written at `App/Server Setup/src/placement.rs`. A provider pass watches it.

**PARKED FROM BUILD STEP 8a'S AUDIT ROUND — 2026-09-06, on the Verdict's instruction. Two items, both readings, both answerable at this desk with no machine and no rental — and between them they decide whether Smoozy ever says anything on a rented machine.**

**BOTH CLOSED 2026-09-06, the same day, by a builder under the build runner. Both readings were taken and both confirm what the code already composes, so no code moved.** The full account — every quoted sentence, every address and the date each was read — is at `Audits/Build/Rented-server runtime/Decisions record.md`, entry of 2026-09-06, *the two readings the boot registration rested on and nobody had taken*. **The file that now carries both readings is `App/Rented-server runtime/src/registration_content.rs`**, in its own file-level comment, which is where both texts are composed.

**1. Does `su` read its options when they come after the user name?** *(Raised by the second seat.)* **The container boot hook this project composes puts them after the user operand.** The code quotes that program's description of the two options **but not its synopsis**, and **the only form this project has ever observed on a real container is a different one.**

  **What breaks if the answer is the other way.** The options and the loop become arguments handed to the login's own shell. **A shell given that first option reads its commands from standard input, which the hook has pointed at nothing** — so it does nothing and ends at zero. **The keeper never starts and the supervisor never runs.** **And the registration read-back confirms it anyway**, because the hook's bytes read back exactly as written — the plan itself says a container read-back cannot catch a provider that runs no hook, and this is a variant of that.

  **What settles it: one reading of that program's own published synopsis. No machine needed.** **The whole of the container boot return rests on it.**

  ~~**Due:** now. It is a reading, it costs minutes, and nothing else in this project is waiting on a cheaper answer to a larger question.~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-06. It does read them, so the hook is right and the container boot return stands.** **The synopsis alone does not settle it and is recorded as ambiguous rather than resolved by inference** — util-linux's own page at tag v2.41 writes `su [options] [-] [user [argument...]]` and says only that *"When _user_ is specified, additional __argument__s can be supplied, in which case they are passed to the shell"*, which is about arguments and not about options. **Two things settle it, both that project's own.** The same page's own worked example for `--pty` puts an option after the user operand: *"The entire session can also be moved to the background (e.g., `su --pty - user -c application &`)."* And its source, `login-utils/su-common.c` at the same tag, parses with an option string beginning with a letter — no `+` — and sets no `POSIXLY_CORRECT`, which the GNU C Library manual answers in one sentence: *"The default is to permute the contents of argv while scanning it so that eventually all the non-options are at the end."* **The one thing that would break it is `POSIXLY_CORRECT` in the provider's own boot environment**, which nothing here sets and which is written down at the site rather than assumed away. **Carried by `App/Rented-server runtime/src/registration_content.rs`**, whose comment now holds the synopsis, both addresses and the dates read.

**2. Where do a systemd user unit's standard streams go when the unit says nothing about them?** *(Raised inside the first seat's finding about the container, and separated because it is the other machine kind.)* **The unit this project composes writes no stream settings at all**, so where a supervisor's words land is the service manager's own default, **and nobody here has read what that default is.**

  **What breaks if they go nowhere.** Exactly the silence the round has just fixed on containers, **on the other machine kind, with nothing in code to show for it** — every reason a supervisor refused to start, written nowhere.

  **What settles it: one reading of that service manager's own publication. No machine needed.**

  ~~**Due:** now, beside the one above it.~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-06. They go to the journal, on a user unit as on a system one, so nothing was written nowhere and no directive is added to the unit.** `systemd.exec(5)` at systemd's own v257: of `StandardOutput=`, *"In other cases, this setting defaults to the value set with DefaultStandardOutput= in systemd-system.conf(5), which defaults to journal"*, the *other cases* being everything but a branch that needs a `StandardInput=` this unit does not set; and of `StandardError=`, *"This setting defaults to the value set with DefaultStandardError= in systemd-system.conf(5), which defaults to inherit"*, where `inherit` duplicates standard output's own descriptor. **The user half is answered from the user's own page and not from the system case**: `systemd-system.conf(5)` is the same page as `systemd-user.conf(5)`, it says *"when run as a user instance, it interprets the configuration file user.conf"*, it lists `DefaultStandardOutput=journal` and `DefaultStandardError=inherit` with no second value for the user manager, and where a setting on it does differ between the two managers it says so in terms. **Two limits are recorded rather than glossed**: reading a user's own journal back wants persistent storage (`journalctl(1)`, *"Note that --user only works if persistent logging is enabled"*), and nothing in Smoozy reads that journal, the machine-side event journal being `State, identity and recovery` §4's and not built. **Carried by `App/Rented-server runtime/src/registration_content.rs`**, whose comment now holds every quoted sentence with its address and the date read.

**PARKED FROM BUILD STEP 8'S AUDIT ROUND — `Download` round 1, 2026-09-06, on the Verdict's instruction. Four items, and not one of them blocks anything.**

**1. A pre-flight that marks a whole folder trains the user to click past the one that matters. This is Design's, and the plan question underneath it is settled.** *(Verdict R-1. An auditor filed it as a plan fault against `Download` J9; the consolidator set the plan half aside on its merits and parked this half.)*

  **The failure path, written out.** A user hand-downloads the first 100 frames of a 240-frame job while it is still rendering at frame 200. `Download` J9 marks every file in the output folder of a job with no recorded ending, so the pre-flight names all 100 as files Smoozy cannot vouch for. **They will meet that on every hand download from a running job**, unvarying, until they stop reading it — and the mark that matters is the one on a job that ended badly.

  **Why the plan half was refused, so nobody reopens it.** J9a weighed exactly this trade and chose the wide mark, because a frontier computed over part of a folder is *"a false claim in the vouching direction rather than a silence"*. J9b narrows it only where an exact per-frame statement is in hand, and **for a job still rendering there is none** — the queue entry holds a count, and the Design says outright it is *"the last frame Smoozy confirmed, not the range the job was submitted with"*. **A count is not a frontier**, and J2 makes that concrete: a typed list renders in the order written, so a list `50, 10, 20` confirms one frame after frame 50, and *the frames at or after the last confirmed* would clear 10 and 20 as sound before they were rendered. **That is the wrong claim in the vouching direction.**

  **So what is left is not the rule but the wording and the shape**, and how a whole-folder mark is put to somebody is Design's under Rule 9. **Nothing in the code moves for it.**

  **Due:** whenever Design is next opened. It blocks nothing.

**2. A label the composed folder name fails on is dropped whole rather than tried again — a want.** *(Verdict R-3. The auditor labelled it a preference in its own word.)* `Download` P10's composed job-folder name substitutes once, trims, re-checks, and falls back to the bare job identity where the re-check refuses. **That keeps P10's promise exactly** — *"costs the label's rendering and never the folder or the files"* — so nothing is broken. A second substitution before falling back would be nicer. **Weighed here on its own merits rather than as a fault, which is what a want is.**

  **Due:** nothing. It is a want and it waits for somebody to think it worth the code.

**3. The *could not be vouched for* mark has no durable home, and the plan does not say whether that is intended.** *(Verdict R-4, handed to the consolidator as an observation rather than filed.)* `Download` E1 step 2's mark is returned once from a turn and has no place in R4's five states or §12's diary counts, **so an arrangement that ends in one sitting and is read in the next loses the mark.** The Transfers design has finished requests go on closing, which is consistent with a sitting-long telling — **so this may be exactly as intended and nothing decides it either way.** **Nothing was decided at the round.**

  **Due:** whenever `Download`'s plan is next opened. It is a question for that document's owner and it blocks nothing.

**5. An arriving-later page cannot record a clause as *partly* built, and that gap refused a guard that would otherwise have been worth having.** *(Found 2026-09-06, by the agent asked to decide whether two more crates should carry `File Browser`'s forward-leg test.)*

  **What the shape is today.** Each crate's arriving-later page pairs a forward leg — every clause the plan defines is named by a working file **or** recorded as waiting — with a guard: **no working file may also name a clause recorded as waiting**. The guard is what stops the waiting list becoming a place to hide from the forward leg.

  **Why it does not carry.** `File Browser`'s one absence is whole, so the guard holds there. **`Server protocol and transfers`' absences are mostly partial**: five clauses — C11, C14, S13, R11 and R12a — are correctly named by working files at the sites that keep the built half, **and correctly recorded on the page as arriving later for the half that is not.** Under the guard as written every one of the five fails, and **the only ways to pass are to delete a true citation from the code or a true entry from the page.** Both are weakenings, and the agent refused both rather than improvise a local exception.

  **So the guard was declined for that crate on a reason rather than on cost**, and what went in instead is a counting test that fails the day the plan gains or loses a clause. **It counts and does not read** — it cannot say which clause moved and cannot see one unbuilt all along — and it says that at itself.

  **What would close this: a way to record a clause as partly built**, with what is kept and what is waiting, so the forward leg and the guard can both be true of it. **That is a change to the shape every arriving-later page shares**, which is why it is here rather than in one crate.

  **Due:** whenever somebody next writes or revises one of those pages, or the Build Gate template. **Nothing is wrong today and no code moves for it** — what it costs is that the strongest available guard cannot be turned on in the crate that would benefit most.

**4. Dozens of constants in the crate carry a clause's name and keep nothing, which makes the gate's count read better than it is.** *(Verdict R-5, noted and deliberately not carried as a must-do.)* `App/Download/` holds many `pub const … : bool = true;` items whose comment is a clause of the plan. **No clause was shown to be unkept by this** — where a clause is a rule about behaviour the rule is already in the function that decides, and the tests reach it there. **It is a warning about how the gate counts rather than a defect in the code**, and the gate's forward leg cannot see it, because a marker named at a constant is a marker named. **It belongs to whoever next writes the gate**, which is `Preparation/Groundwork/Templates/Build Gate.md`.

  **Due:** the next time that template is opened. **Nothing in this project's code moves for it.**

**Parked from Build step 5's audit round, 2026-09-04, on the Verdict's instruction. Three items. The first is the largest thing that round found and did not do; the other two block nothing today.**

**1. Paths cross the transfer seam as text, and the workspace should decide it once rather than eighty-four times.** *(From `File Browser` Round 1's Verdict, 2026-09-04, which routed it here rather than repairing it. That Verdict's own summary calls it the largest single thing the round found that the round is not doing.)*

  **What the disagreement is.** `File Browser` carries a name as bytes everywhere it owns the value, and its plan says so twice — N2, *"nothing rewrites, normalises or re-encodes a name in flight"*, and L1a for the machine side. The file-transfer subsystem's own calls take bytes. But `Server protocol and transfers`' transfer record holds the source path and the final path as **text**, and the whole surface of `TheUsersOwnComputer` takes paths as text. **So a name that is not valid text cannot cross that seam at all.**

  **How far it reaches.** Eighty-four sites, eleven files, three crates — including a rented-machine test, and the download side, which is not built. `File Browser`'s three reading traits — `ReadsADirectory`, `ReadsAPrefix` and `CanListAComponent` — sit on the same seam from the other side and have no real end yet, so whoever wires them meets this too.

  **Who owes it.** `Server protocol and transfers` and `Desktop app and delivery` as much as `File Browser`. **It is one decision taken once rather than five taken module by module**, which is why a File Browser round was the wrong place to take it and why it is written here rather than on that module's list.

  **What it costs while it stands.** A file whose name is not valid text cannot be uploaded at all. That round's must-do 3 makes the refusal honest — the file is named, the failure says the name cannot be sent, and the rest of the folder still lands — where before the name was quietly rewritten and the upload then failed for a reason about reading a file. **So waiting costs the user nothing they are not already losing; deciding late is what costs.**

  ~~**Due:** before `Download` is built at **step 8**, which puts a second direction on the same seam and doubles the cost of deciding late.~~ **THE DUE FIRED AND WAS MISSED. Marked 2026-09-06 by the build runner at `Download` round 1's close, where the round's consolidator found it — not by this file being read.** **`Download` is built, both halves, and the second direction is on the seam**, so the thing this Due existed to prevent has happened. **What it costs is exactly what the item said it would**: the decision is now taken across two directions instead of one.

  **THE RUNNER'S RULING, 2026-09-06: the deciding is separated from the carrying out, and the deciding happens now.** Under the Creator's standing delegation of 2026-08-31. **The reason the item rotted is that it was written as one job — decide and repair eighty-four sites — and a job that size never fits into a step that is busy.** Deciding is cheap and it is what everything else waits on; carrying it out across eleven files and three crates is its own piece of work and is scheduled rather than squeezed. **An agent is out on the deciding half now**, told to read the three plans, rule bytes-or-text, write the ruling into the documents that own it, and produce the site list **without changing a site**.

  **THE RULING LANDED THE SAME DAY AND THERE IS A WORK LIST BEHIND IT. 2026-09-06.**

  **The decision: bytes on the machine side, the receiving system's own path unit on the desktop side, text nowhere but display.** One place converts, and it is a judgement rather than a conversion — `Desktop app and delivery` C3's name check, which already runs before a byte moves and already has four outcomes. **Not bytes everywhere**, because that is false on Windows, where a name is UTF-16 code units. **Not text everywhere**, because that overturns three clauses written against watched failures and costs a name the machine can move and Smoozy cannot list. **It is written once, at `Server protocol and transfers` §2 P7, with `File Browser` L1b and `Desktop app and delivery` F10b citing it and restating nothing**, so the three cannot drift.

  **What it costs the user is almost nothing, and that is said plainly rather than dressed up.** The one real change is on the rented machine, where a name that is not valid text becomes listable, selectable and fetchable. On a Mac such a name still cannot be written locally, and **what changes there is that the refusal names the true reason** — *the name cannot be written on your computer* — rather than *the name cannot be sent*.

  **THE FILING ABOVE WAS WRONG SIX WAYS AND EVERY CORRECTION IS IN THE WORK LIST.** **Five crates arrange a transfer, not three** — the preview fetch and the asset upload both build transfer records and neither was named. **Eleven files is one crate's byte side reported as the whole seam.** **Eighty-four sites is unreproducible and low**: the local-computer surface alone has 58 call sites across nine files. **Five reading traits, not three, and two of them already take bytes.** **And *the three reading traits have no real end yet* is false** — all four are implemented over the real subsystem; what is true is that nothing outside the crate constructs one yet, which is a different thing.

  **Two things it found that were nobody's finding before.** The staging path **looks** exempt from all this and is not, its leading components being the user's chosen folder or the machine's arbitrary one. And **there is one silent rewrite standing in production today**: `App/Dependency Upload & Remap/src/adapter.rs:269`, where a Windows name is made lossy into text **and then used as a lookup key**. Both are named in the ruling.

  **Two things are owed and neither blocks the carrying out.** The six outside-fact entries in `Server protocol and transfers`' `Sources/` companion — the citations are inline in the clause, which is that document's own practice, and the signature line records the companion entry as owed. And **the transfer record holds a source path the architecture never named**, which is that document's next round's rather than a repair.

  **Due, restated:** the carrying out, at whichever step next opens one of the crates that hold the seam. **The list is `Audits/Build/Seam decisions/Paths across the transfer seam — the work list.md`, 233 lines, taken at a named commit, with ten reproducible searches printed in it and a build order.** **It is a work list rather than a question, and the question is closed.**

**2. Whether Blender accepts a `.blend` extension written with different capitals is unchecked.** *(From `File Browser` Round 1's Verdict, 2026-09-04. An auditor mentioned it rather than filing it, and the Verdict agreed it is not a finding: no code changes for it.)* `uploading::the_toolbar_for` matches the extension ignoring capitals. **Nothing rests on it either way** — the toolbar is presentation, and the rule costs the same whichever way the answer goes. **It is an outside fact and no answer is written here**; nothing in the research covers it, and it stays a question.
  **Due:** nothing fires it. It is recorded so the question is not asked from memory a second time.

**3. A store in a step 2 module writes and reads the same way must-do 4 repaired next door.** *(Named in `File Browser` Round 1's Verdict as a thing the runner should know. It arises from the fix that Verdict chose, not from any finding: no auditor raised it, it is outside that round's Subject, and it was not made a must-do. **The Verdict routed it to no document and set no Due; the Due below is the filer's**, written at the step 5 close so nobody reads it as the consolidator's.)* `App/Desktop app and delivery/src/record/transfer_records.rs` writes with `INSERT OR REPLACE INTO transfer_record` and reads with `ORDER BY rowid` — opened and confirmed on 2026-09-04. That is the pairing must-do 4 repairs in the neighbouring upload-request table, where a replace deletes the row and inserts a new one, so a record written again later moves to the end of the order. **Whether anything reads that order for its meaning is not established here and nothing is assumed.**
  **Due:** whenever that store's write order is next relied on, or whenever anyone is next in that file. Nothing waits on it today.

**Parked from Build step 8's audit round on `App/Download/`, 2026-09-06, by that round's implementer. Two unchecked facts, both from the Verdict's own list, and neither is answered in this project.**

**1. Whether every server this app renders on says which entries in a folder are folders.** *(`Download` code round 1, unchecked fact F-1. Both auditors doubted it; what carries it is not the headcount but that the field is optional in this workspace's own code, which was read rather than remembered.)*

  **What was read.** `App/Download/src/acting/wiring.rs` decides whether a listed entry is a folder from the Unix mode bits in the listing's permissions field. **In the protocol crate that field is `Option<u32>`**, filled only where the server set the permissions flag in its reply, and the revision-3 attributes structure the crate models carries **no separate kind field at all**. `Download` W1 treats *kind* as a field the route supplies; on this route it is one bit of a value a server may or may not send.

  **What breaks if it comes back the wrong way.** A job's `beauty/`, `mist/` and `depth/` sub-folders come back as files. The walk never descends into them, never reads them, and **still reports a complete walk, because nothing failed** — so the whole-walk discard cannot catch it. Every file under a sub-folder is then missing from the ending walk and becomes *never coming*, and a job whose frames are all safely on the machine reads **ended, 0 of 720**. Each such folder, unmatched and not marked as a folder, is also pushed onto the fetchable list and a file transfer is arranged for a directory.

  **What stands in the meantime, and it is built.** An entry whose kind the reply did not carry makes the walk **a reading that cannot be used**: it is discarded whole and retried at the cadence, with the cause carried. That costs one walk cadence per unanswered entry and **fails towards fetching late**, which is the direction `Download` claims everywhere else; reading it as a file fails towards a job reported as having produced nothing. **The code says outright that no publication has answered the question and guesses nothing** — `acting/wiring.rs` and `acting/walking.rs`'s `TheEntrysKind`.

  **Who owes it.** The owner of `App/Server protocol and transfers/`. The question is: does the listing carry an entry's kind on the servers Smoozy connects to, and if not, is there any other field on this route that carries it?

  **Due:** whenever `Server protocol and transfers`' subsystem listing is next opened, or before anybody removes the discard `Download` now takes. **It is not a Blender question and it names no rented machine**: it is answered by reading the protocol this workspace already implements, and it blocks nothing today.

**2. What form a Windows destination folder arrives in, and whether a forward-slash child is accepted on it.** *(`Download` code round 1, unchecked fact F-2. One auditor.)*

  **What was read.** Every path `App/Download/` composes on the user's side joins with `/` — the staging folder, the landing path, and the component split. **A Windows destination is whatever bytes the platform file adapter hands back**, and Windows has never been run (`Download` §15 item 1).

  **What breaks:** staging paths and landing paths on Windows. **What holds:** H5a's depth arithmetic, because a Windows destination with no `/` in it is one component on both sides; and nothing on macOS or Linux turns on it.

  **Who owes it.** The owner of `Desktop app and delivery`'s platform file adapter, which is where a destination folder is obtained and where a local write is made.

  **Due:** before the platform file adapter's Windows half is written, or before the first Windows release artifact carries a download. **Nothing waits on it today**, because no local write of this module has a producer at all.

**3. Does a plain start of a service-manager unit that is already active end and re-run its main process?** *(Step 8b's desktop side, code round 1, the round's one unchecked fact. Both auditors raised it and both read it the same way, which is not two readings: two models about one operating system is one training set speaking twice.)*

  **Where it bites.** `Rented-server runtime` G25 orders the commit so that the running supervisor is ended and then the restarter starts the one the pointer now names. **The desktop has no route to a signal**, so on a systemd machine the only act it performs is the start. Whether that start replaces the process that is already running is the whole question.

  **What breaks if the answer is no.** A systemd rental is already running a Smoozy supervisor whose protocol has fallen outside the compatibility window. The desktop stages and verifies the new package, renames the pointer, and asks for the start. Nothing happens, because the unit is already active. The health exchange reaches the **old** supervisor, which answers with the old package version, the versions disagree, and the whole placement rolls back. **No package could ever be replaced on a live systemd machine by this route**, and the user is told the repair failed with nothing said about why.

  **What holds meanwhile.** The code makes no claim about it: the sentence that stated it as fact carried no source and was struck on 2026-09-12, at `App/Server protocol and transfers/src/placement/performing.rs`'s commit step. **Nothing is guessed in its place** — the fix one auditor proposed, a stop of the unit followed by the start, would write a remembered fact about somebody else's software into the code, which is exactly what Rule 2 forbids and what this item is about. A first placement, which is every bare rental, has no running supervisor and is untouched by the question.

  **This is not the nearest item already parked.** That one is about a **dead** supervisor on a disk-full machine, where the project has an observation and a source and one plain start command recovers it completely. A live unit is a different case.

  **How it is answered, and it costs nothing.** A reading of the service manager's own published pages. **No rental, no machine and no Blender research** — it is a fact about an operating system, got the way `Preparation/Build/Method.md` says such facts are got: from the thing's own publication, quoted, with the address and the date read.

  **Who owes it.** The owner of `Preparation/Architecture/Rented-server runtime.md` G25, which is where the answer lands.

  **Due:** at the next close.

  **CLOSED 2026-09-22 by the build runner, sidestepped on both machine kinds rather than answered, and nothing in the code turns on it any more.** On a systemd machine the start step has been `systemctl --user restart` since 2026-09-22, whose own manual says it stops and then starts the unit — so the desktop no longer issues a plain start of an already-active unit anywhere. On a container the start step starts S5's keeper, which is not a service-manager unit at all, and the ending `Rented-server runtime` G25 orders is now asked of the supervisor itself over the ordinary command path. **The question about a plain start is still unanswered and is now unasked**: no route in Smoozy performs one on a live unit, so there is nothing left for the answer to break. Should a plain start ever come back, this item comes back with it.

### Whether a file picker opened from an ordinary command freezes the whole window

*Added 2026-09-17 by the build runner, from the Verdict of step 10a's screens round 1 (a server's files, setting up and render settings), which found it had been parked by step 10's round 1 (setting it up, and what the desktop keeps) and never filed. Tagged for `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md`, the picker.*

**What is not known.** Whether the shell runs a command that is not marked as running apart on the thread that draws the window. `src/picker.rs` says the system's file picker must not be opened on that thread, because it blocks until the user closes it.

**Where it can bite.** Step 10's desktop half, and two places in the screens: *Upload…* in Files (`src/surface/files.rs`, `an_upload_is_asked_for`) and *Somewhere else…* in Render settings (`src/surface/render_settings.rs`, `the_download_choices_are_made`).

**What it costs if the answer is the bad one.** Pressing either button freezes the whole window until the picker closes, or for good.

**How the Mac half is settled.** Press *Upload…* once in the milestone pass the Creator plays by clicking (step 10b's end). The shell's own publication may also say; read it rather than guess.

**Due:** at step 10b's milestone pass, or sooner if anyone opens the shell's command threading.

**FIRED 2026-09-27 — the bad answer, seen on the Mac.** In the seventh pass on Hyperstack, *Where they go* on Render Settings opened the folder panel, and when the Creator went to use it the whole window showed the spinning wait cursor and answered nothing. The runner force-quit the throwaway copy. ~~**Due now: a fix, with the faster-loop fixes after the seventh passes** — open the picker off the drawing thread, and check *Upload…* and every other picker for the same fault.~~

**FIXED AT THE DESK 2026-09-27, by step 10c's fix piece 5 — and not yet pressed by anybody.** All four commands that open a folder panel now open it off the drawing thread, on the shell's own published rule, and a check fails the build's tests if any plain command reaches the panel again. The account is `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Fix pieces 3, 4, 5, 7 and 8 — what landed.md`. **Nobody has opened a panel in the running app since**, so this is not closed.

**Due:** two presses, then close. **The Creator's**: the account's *piece-5 moment* — *Search folders → Add a folder…* and *Download folder → Choose…* in Settings, in the practice copy, with no server. **The eighth pass's**: *Files → Upload…*, and *Where they go → Somewhere else… → Done* on Render Settings, with a server connected. It passes if the window keeps drawing while each panel is up.

### Whether the Linux secret-store calls can hang, having a deadline they never use

*Added 2026-09-18 by the build runner, from the Verdict of step 10b's round 1 (holding a connection), unchecked fact (b). Tagged for `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md` S3 and S4, and §14 items 8 and 9.*

**What is not known.** `LinuxSecretService::put`, `get` and `erase` in `App/Desktop app and delivery/src/platform/secrets.rs` are handed a deadline and do not use it. Whether a Linux secret store can leave a call waiting for good is unread.

**What it costs if the answer is the bad one.** On Linux, a save, read or erase of a secret could hang whatever asked for it — sign-in, a key's passphrase, the licence.

**How it is settled.** Read the secret-service publication, or run the adapters against a real store on a Linux machine; they have never run against one (§14 items 8 and 9).

**Due:** before the first Linux release, or when anyone next opens the Linux adapters.


2026-10-26 — The licence clause alone is superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04. The secret-store deadline question and the real Linux store checks remain owed on their existing terms; earlier text is retained.
