### Offering a file whose name differs only in its Unicode form (2026-09-29)

*Added 2026-09-29 by the paperwork piece, from `Audits/Build/Step 10c/The dependency wave — what each piece is, which files it owns, and in what order.md`, fault 5. A want under Rule 7; nothing promised is broken.*

A name stored in decomposed Unicode on the user's disk is not found by the check, and nothing is offered. `Dependency Upload & Remap` F11 claims nothing for this, and the match is exact by Row 166. *Not found* is honest about the render: on a byte-exact rental disk Blender would not find the file under the recorded spelling either, and the user can locate it by hand. **The want**: propose the differently spelled file, the way a difference in capitals is offered. **What stays owed is not the want but the Vast pass's own question**: after a plain Files upload, does the render find the file by the spelling the `.blend` records?

~~**Due:** after the Vast pass answers that question; it enters at Specs (`Dependency Upload & Remap`).~~

**FIRED 2026-10-01: the thirteenth pass answered the question, and the answer is no.** The 5.2 battle project, uploaded whole by a drag into Files on Hyperstack and rendered with no check, came back pink: Blender reported `Текстуры/йод рассеивания.jpg` as missing, because the file on the machine is spelled `и` plus a combining mark, as the Mac's copy stores it, while the `.blend` records `й` (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The thirteenth pass — Hyperstack.md`; the fix map after the thirteenth pass, *The spelling question*). **Still a want, not a fault**: `File Browser` N2 carries names byte for byte, and the check's *Not found* is true. **It matters before the Creator's tests**, whose splash scenes use names in Cyrillic, Chinese and Arabic. **Offered to the Creator 2026-10-01, awaiting their word.** If taken, it enters at Specs (`Dependency Upload & Remap`): the check proposes the file whose name differs only in its Unicode form, as it already proposes a difference in capitals. If not, the Creator is told before their tests that such a texture may come back pink after a plain upload, and that *Check dependencies* names it. *Marked by the paperwork piece of step 10c (thirteenth-pass fix piece 4).*

**Due:** the Creator's word.

### Letting a job run the file's own scripts, switched on per job (2026-09-28)

*Creator-stated, 2026-09-28: ***"That's actually a very good idea, because scripts are something power users actually use."*** **Agreed in principle and parked for later**, on their word — a want under Rule 7; nothing promised is broken.*

Blender does not run the scripts stored inside a `.blend` unless told to, and Smoozy leaves that alone on purpose (`Rendering` L9): nothing asked for it, and switching it on for every file would run code nobody chose to run on the user's rented machine. **The splash scenes at the desk showed what that costs**: the 5.1 splash carries eleven programs set to run when the file opens (one re-points its bake folders) and three drivers that need full Python, so a render of it on a rental runs without them and may not match the published image (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The splash scenes at the desk — what was found.md`). **The want: a per-job switch, off by default, that lets this file's own scripts run, with a plain warning of what that means.**

**What the spec must settle:** where the switch sits (Render Settings, per job, is the lean) and whether it is remembered per file; the warning's words; whether the dependency check and *Read the file* run the scripts too, or only the render; and what Telegram or Queue show for a job that has them on. Blender's own documented switch for this is already quoted in `Rendering` L9.

**Due: when 0.1.7 is released — the runner closing that release offers it to the Creator as the first work after it.** **It enters at Specs** (`Rendering`, `Render Settings`), then Design, Architecture and Build.

**2026-10-08 — mostly overtaken.** The Creator ruled that every render lets the file's own scripts run, with no switch (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The Creator's rulings on add-ons and a file's scripts, 2026-10-08.md`, ruling 4); it is written into the `Rendering` spec (*The file's own scripts run*) and `Architecture/Rendering` L9. **Closed the same day:** *Read the file* and the dependency check run the file's scripts too — the Creator: *"Yes to running scripts when reading."* (the `Rendering` spec). ~~**Still open from this entry:** whether *Read the file* and the dependency check run the file's scripts too.~~ Until settled, a file whose stored scripts or Python drivers change a view layer's switch can read differently from how it renders (`Architecture/Render Settings` V3). *(Added by the plan writer holding `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Brief — add-ons parked and a file's scripts run, the plan pass.md`.)*

### *Last seen* meaning when Smoozy last had the server, not when it last connected (2026-09-27)

*Added by the plan pass before the fixes after step 10c's seventh passes, from the fix map (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The fixes after the seventh passes — what each piece is, which files it owns, and in what order.md`, carry-forward 2). **Not agreed** — a want under Rule 7; nothing promised is broken.*

On all three seventh passes a server's *last seen* showed the moment of its last Connect and did not move while it stayed connected, and after a reconnect it showed the old time for five to ten seconds. **That is the plan working as written**: `Servers` H2a makes *last seen* the moment of the last reading, and H15 takes one reading per connection, at its start. **The want is for *last seen* to mean when Smoozy last had the server** — the end of the last connection rather than its start. Nobody builds it from a fault list: a builder told *"last seen never moves"* would change a clause nobody decided to change.

**Seen again on the Vast pass, 2026-10-01**: after a provider reboot, *last seen* stayed at the reboot's 08:09 PM through the next five minutes of connection (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The Vast pass — the Creator's real-world tests.md`, *Smaller, as seen*). That is the same reading. Noted by the the paperwork piece of step 10c after the Vast pass.

**Due:** when the Creator asks for it, or at step 12's close. **It enters at Specs** (`Servers`) if what *last seen* means changes, or at Design if only its wording does.

### Files opening at the login's home folder rather than at `/` (2026-09-27)

*Added by the same plan pass, from the same map (fix-list item 5). **Not agreed** — a want under Rule 7.*

Files opens every server at `/`. On a machine whose login is not `root`, `/` cannot be written, so the first upload there is refused — which is how Hyperstack's seventh pass met it. **Where the list opens is written in no clause**, so opening at the login's home folder is a want, not a fix. **The refused upload saying why is a separate fault and is being fixed** (`File Browser` P1, P5); this item is only where the list starts.

**Due:** when the Creator asks for it, or at step 12's close. **It enters at Specs** (`File Browser`).

### A warning on quitting while a submission is still being sent (2026-09-27)

*Added by the same plan pass, from the same map (the list of things that are not faults). **Not agreed** — a want under Rule 7.*

Quitting while Render Settings says *Submitting.* gives no warning, and no clause asks for one. On Vast's seventh pass that quit lost the frames' download without a word; **that loss is a fault and is being fixed** — the download arrangement is to be written before the job is sent (`Download` R1) — and once it is, nothing is lost by quitting then. The warning would only tell the user something is still in flight.

**Due:** when the Creator asks for it, or at step 12's close. **It enters at Specs** (`Rendering`, which owns submitting; no spec yet says anything about quitting).

### Paste the provider's own connection line to fill in a server (2026-09-21)

*Creator-stated, 2026-09-21, raised during step 10c's first rental pass. A want, not a fault — nothing promised is broken.*

**Every provider, once a machine is made, shows a ready-typed line for connecting to it from a terminal. The user should be able to paste that whole line into Smoozy's Add a server dialog and have the address, port and login filled in**, instead of copying each one out separately. It changes nothing underneath: the fields are filled, the user sees them before saving, and **Smoozy reads the line and never runs it.**

**Three things the spec must settle, raised in the conversation rather than decided:**
- **Some providers are thought to offer more than one form of the line**, one of them through the provider's own relay. **If a form cannot carry files, filling the fields from it gives a server that connects and cannot upload.** Unchecked — each provider's own pages are to be read, dated and quoted before the spec leans on any form (Rule 2).
- **What the line carries that Smoozy does not use** — a key path of the user's own, forwarded ports, other options — is ignored, and the spec says so rather than leaving it to a builder.
- **A line missing a piece is said, not guessed** (Rule 8): the fields found are filled and the one that was not is named.

**Due:** once step 10c's rental passes pass, or when the Creator asks for it sooner. **It enters at Specs** (`Servers`, the Add dialog), then Design, then Architecture, then Build.

**ENTERED AT SPECS 2026-10-04, on the Creator's ask of that day.** Written at `Specs/Servers` *Adding a server* (the three questions ruled lean by the build runner under the standing delegation, the forms half waiting on one dated provider reading), `Design/Servers` *Adding a server*, and `Architecture/Servers` §13.3 PL1–PL3; Coverage Row 524. Building it is step 10c's piece 15 of `Audits/Build/Step 10c/The Creator's second notes on the test copy — what each piece is, which files it owns, and in what order.md`. Nothing is left here but the building.

### Three small wants from step 10b's audit (2026-09-18)

*Added by the build runner from the Verdict of step 10b's round 1 (holding a connection), For the runner item 7. Wants, not faults — nothing promised is broken.*

- **A *Connecting…* state for a server's first connect**, so the list says something between the tick and the first reading. Rule 9's cost would need writing in: one more state to draw and word.
- **A development mark that names the copy that wrote it**, so leftovers from agents' throwaway copies can be told apart.
- **A throwaway copy that also redirects its downloads, notifications and window data**, not only its record, home folder and secrets — the window data is what has been left in the Creator's own Library.

**Due:** at step 12's close, or when the Creator asks for any of them.

---

Each item here changes what the user gets, so under Rule 7 it enters at **Specs** rather than waiting for a later stage. They are grouped because they are a different kind of thing from the rest of this file — product ideas with no home yet, rather than research questions or process housekeeping. Most were raised in a whole-project review of the written specs on 2026-07-23, and the Creator agreed on that date that each of those is wanted. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-23). Later items say their own date.

**Being in this section does not mean it is wanted.** Two are marked in their own items as not agreed: **uploading**, which the Creator asked to discuss rather than settling anything, and **an hourly rate**, which they asked to think about. How any of these behaves is not settled either way, and settling it is the work each item names.

  ~~**Due:** when the **Dependencies** and **Reading a file** topics' findings land.~~ **Both closed, and Dependencies was opened again on 2026-08-30 for question 20; the Due line was never updated.**
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **A *Hide Smoozy* that works on all three systems, which today means building a way back first.** *(Raised 2026-09-12 by the code audit of step 10a's shared half — `Audits/Build/Step 10a/Round 1 — the window and the operating system/Verdict.md`. **Not agreed** — it is a want under Rule 7.)*

  **What was wrong, and it is already fixed.** The Smoozy menu carried a *Hide Smoozy* item on every system. On macOS the operating system itself brings a hidden application back. **On Windows and Linux nothing did** — there is no tray icon and no second-launch raise — **so a user who pressed it lost the whole application with the process still running.** The round removed the item on those two systems, the way the code's own key map already treated it as Mac-only.

  **What the want asks for** is the item back on all three, which means **building the way back before the way out**: something that survives the window going away and can bring it back. **What it costs** is a tray or notification-area presence on two systems, which no spec asks for and no design draws, and which changes what the user gets. **What holds without it:** the item is on the system that can honour it and absent from the two that cannot, so nothing anywhere is a control that makes Smoozy unreachable.

  **Due:** the Creator, whenever they want to weigh it. **It blocks nothing.**

- **Telegram publishes a way to make the bot for the user, and it would replace the whole walkthrough.** *(Raised 2026-08-25 by the Architecture stage's reading of Fact 133. **Not agreed** — it is a want under Rule 7 and it is the Creator's to rule on.)*

  Smoozy's Telegram setup today walks the user through Telegram's own bot-making chat: they are given two names to copy, they answer the chat's questions, and they paste the token back into Smoozy. **Telegram publishes an alternative.** A bot that Smoozy itself owns can hand the user a link that opens Telegram's **own pre-filled bot-making window** — name and username already in it — and, once the user confirms, that manager bot collects the new bot's token directly. **No copying, no pasting, and no token ever passing through the user's hands.**

  **What makes it worth raising rather than noting.** The availability check Smoozy cannot perform — nothing available to a bot can tell the user whether a username is free — **runs inside Telegram's own client on the user's own account** in that flow. So the one thing the current walkthrough cannot do, this does for free.

  **What it costs, stated so nobody reads this as a recommendation.** Smoozy would have to **own and run a bot of its own**, permanently, which the project does not do today and which the *Smoozy never sends anything, to anyone, ever* stance would have to be read against. The manager bot needs a setting turned on in the bot-making chat first. And it does not escape the ceiling Telegram publishes on how many bots one account may own — a user at that limit is refused either way.

  **It changes what the user gets, so under Rule 7 it enters at Specs or not at all.** Nothing in the architecture written for the current walkthrough is wasted if it is taken: `Telegram` §6 S1a–S1c answers the names Smoozy offers, and those are the same names either flow would use.

  **Due:** whenever the Creator is next ruling on Telegram, or at the Specs' next opening.

- **Re-submitting a job that failed.** A job's settings are fixed at submission, so trying a failed render again means submitting a new job. The specs treat that as costless and it is not: the user has to find the `.blend` in the file browser again and re-enter every setting. By the time a job fails the user is likely browsing another server and has no realistic recollection of where the file sat. So the next step is the same job with something changed — **but what to change is an open question, not a settled one.** This thread and the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) both used to answer it with "fewer samples or a smaller resolution". On 2026-07-27 an agent argued that this does not follow: since the usual failure is insufficient GPU memory, and GPU memory is taken up by a scene's geometry and textures rather than by its sample count, cutting samples would not address the cause the same sentence names. The Creator agreed. Both places have been corrected to stop asserting it. What *does* help after that failure is now unanswered, and it needs the Blender research already parked below (whether a scene too heavy for the GPU fails cleanly or takes the machine down) before this feature can say anything useful to an artist. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-27) that the old answer was wrong; the replacement is **unresolved**. The rest is settled: the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) states that a finished entry keeps the whole setup the job ran with — file, scene, range, resolution, overrides — precisely so the user does not have to find the file again, and the [Rendering spec](../Specs/Rendering.md) now owns the action, which opens the settings page filled in with what that job ran with so the user can change something before submitting again. So the machinery is in place and only the advice is missing. This is the practical half of the item above on **what a Blender render failure usually is**, which already asks what the message should tell an artist to do next; settle the two together, since the answer to "what do I do now" is largely this. One more thing now waits on the same answer: **what *try it again* from a phone actually sends.** [Telegram](../Specs/Telegram.md) offers it as one tap on a failed job's message, while [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) says submitting again opens the settings page precisely so the user changes something first — and an identical job resent from a tap is the outcome that section exists to prevent. Rendering carries a marker saying the two do not yet fit, rather than claiming they do (2026-07-30). **When:** after the Blender research on how a scene too heavy for the GPU fails, in whichever of the documents the answer belongs to.

  ~~**Due:** when the **Reports** topic's findings land — specifically how a scene too heavy for the graphics card fails.~~ **That is answered, and it is answered three different ways depending on the machine** — see *Telling the user a job spilled* below, narrowed at this close. **So the advice this item says is missing can now be written, and nobody has written it.**
  **The trigger on this item fired long before the close of 2026-08-30 and nobody ever moved it. See *Five Due lines that fired when their topics closed* under **Due now**.**

- **Letting a job that was given up be picked up rather than submitted again.** Raised by the Queue & History spec's round-2 audit on 2026-08-03 and **not agreed** — the consolidator turned it down as a want under Rule 7 and parked it here. A job interrupted twice is given up, and the frames it wrote stay in its own folder. Submitting it again is a new job with a new identity and a new folder, so a heavy job given up at frame 640 of 800 renders the whole range from the start and those 640 frames buy the user nothing. The want is to let the user carry the old job on instead. What it costs is why it was not taken: it reopens three settled rules across two specs — that a job's identity is fixed at submission, that a re-submission is a new job, and that this is precisely what makes submitting again safe, since two jobs can never collide. The half of the finding that *was* taken is in the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md), which now says plainly that submitting again renders the whole range and that the given-up job's frames are there to download or browse. *(From the Queue & History spec round 2, M25 and its rejected half, 2026-08-03.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12, and **the Creator ruled it down as asked and up in a different shape.** Carrying the old job on is not taken: the three rules it would reopen — a job's identity fixed at submission, a re-submission being a new job, and two jobs never colliding — are what make submitting again safe, and they stand. What the Creator ruled instead is that **starting from the last saved frame should be possible, as a new job, marked as one in the queue.** So [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) now carries a second action on a history entry, *carry on from where this stopped*, which opens the settings page with the range starting after the last frame Smoozy can vouch for as finished — a new job with its own identity and its own folder, like any other. It is a separate action from submitting again, which is what keeps that feature's promise to fill the page in with what the job actually ran with. [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) owns the two things it needs: the vouched-frame figure it already kept, and the mark on the new job's entry naming the job it carries on from. It applies to **any** entry that ended short, not only a given-up one, on the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-12.

- **Letting a group of scene-jobs be reordered as one unit.** Raised by the Queue & History spec's round-2 audit on 2026-08-03 and parked rather than taken up — the spec already states that reordering pulls a scene-job out of its group, and treats the fragmentation as accepted rather than solved. The want was to let the user move a whole group at once, the way they can already cancel or stop one. *(From the Queue & History spec round 2, rejected findings, 2026-08-03.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it in.** [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) now offers reordering alongside cancelling and stopping as a group action: the group moves as one block to a chosen position in its machine's line, keeping the order its scene-jobs already have among themselves, and a scene-job of that group already rendering stays where it is. Moving a single scene-job still pulls it out of its group — that is unchanged — but it is now the user's choice rather than the only thing available, and a group scattered that way is gathered back together when it is moved as one.

- **Having Smoozy speak up the moment a render reads the file and finds a reference it has never resolved.** Raised by the scenario "The user adds a texture and renders again", round 1, and dropped there as a want past where Smoozy's job stops — the file is already being read on the machine when the render starts, so the moment costs nothing extra, but Smoozy checking a user's file for them on its own initiative is not something the plan currently does. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 1, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it out.** It stays past the line under Rule 8: Smoozy does not check a user's file on its own initiative, and the missing-assets warning in [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) already covers the moment that matters. Nothing was written into any spec.

- **Offering the first-frame check as a third way past the missing-assets warning.** Raised by the scenario "The user adds a texture and renders again", round 1, and dropped there as a want past the line. [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) already has the first-frame check catch textures that did not load, and the missing-assets warning is the moment Smoozy already suspects it — the want was to offer that check as an option right there instead of only warning and letting the user choose to carry on or not. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 2, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it in.** The missing-assets warning in [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) now offers three ways on rather than two: go and check the assets, submit anyway, or submit with the first-frame check. It adds nothing to the product and only moves where the check is offered — the same two jobs, the same waiting job, the same answer — and it is not offered where the user has already asked for the check.

- **File Browser offering to run the dependency check when a `.blend` is sent up.** Raised by the scenario "The user adds a texture and renders again", round 1 (Gemini 3.6 Flash High), and dropped there as a want past the line — [File Browser](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) treats a `.blend` as no different from any other file until it is chosen for a render, and [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) names this shape of thing outright as nagging. The worry behind it — a user finding out too late that a file's assets were never checked — is answered a different way, by the same round's fix to the missing-assets warning. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 3, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it out.** A `.blend` stays no different from any other file until it is chosen for a render, and offering the check at upload is the shape [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) names outright as nagging. Nothing was written into any spec.

- **Pre-filling a new machine's dependency check with last week's answers.** Raised by the scenario "The user renders the same project on a new machine", round 1 (GLM 5.2), and dropped there as a want past where Smoozy's job stops — the record already keeps which local file resolved each reference, but running the check again on a new machine is the cost of renting a new machine, not a fault in the plan. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 9, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it in, and it turned out to be already promised — nothing needed writing.** [Dependency Upload & Remap](../Specs/Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md) settles it twice over, under *What Smoozy remembers*: a located answer is keyed to the reference as the file records it, with no machine in it, so "the same file checked on a machine it has never been on gets every match the user made by hand"; and "rent a new server, upload again, and the work of locating everything is not repeated". What genuinely is repeated on a new machine is the **sending** — the files themselves must cross the wire again, because Smoozy never moves files between two servers — and that is the cost of renting a machine rather than a gap. Recorded here rather than closed silently: the want was real and the answer was that the spec already had it, which is worth knowing the next time this shape of thing is raised.

- **Showing a job's resolution in pixels beside the percentage.** Raised by the scenario "The user renders the same project on a new machine", round 1 (Opus 5), and dropped there as a want — Render Settings stated the percentage only, and the size in pixels landed on the queue entry, which without a pre-load means after the render has already begun. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 10, 2026-08-08.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it in, with one thing settled at the same time: it is an indicator, not a setting.** [Render Settings](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) now shows the size the percentage works out to, in pixels, beside the box — and states plainly that the figure cannot be typed into, dragged or overridden, that the percentage is the only setting the user changes, and that Smoozy offers no way to ask for a render by its width and height. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-12). It needs the file to have been read, since the scene's full size sits inside the `.blend`, so a page with no pre-load shows the percentage alone and says the size is not known.

- **Pre-filling the range when a job that ended short is submitted again.** Raised by the scenario "The server disk fills up during render", round 1 (Grok 4.5), and **not agreed** — the consolidator turned it down and parked it here. Where a history entry ended short of a clean finish and the last frame it finished is known, the want is for submitting again to open the settings page with the range already set to start after that frame, so the user does not re-render and re-pay for the frames they already have by submitting unchanged. What it would touch is why it was not simply taken: [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) settles that submitting again opens the settings page **filled in with what that job ran with**, and a page carrying a range the job did not run with contradicts the promise the feature is built on — the same section also says plainly that what to change is not Smoozy's to suggest. The worry behind it is answered a different way by the same round: Rendering now states plainly that finishing a part-done range leaves two folders, so nobody discovers that after paying twice. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Must-do 8, 2026-08-09.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it out as asked, and the need behind it was met another way the same day.** Submitting again still opens the settings page filled in with what the job actually ran with, untouched — a page carrying a range the job did not run with would contradict the promise that feature is built on. What the user gets instead is a **second action** on the same entry, *carry on from where this stopped*, asked for by name and saying outright that it has moved the range: see the given-up-job item above, where that ruling is recorded in full. So the range is never quietly different from what the entry says; it differs only where the user asked for a different action.

- **Warning at the moment of submitting that a job will not fit the machine's free disk.** Raised by the scenario "The server disk fills up during render", round 1 (Grok 4.5, and named the same way by Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Terra without being filed as a fault), and dropped there as a want past where Smoozy's job stops — [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) is explicit that Smoozy does not check that the user's file, or their choice of machine, is set up sensibly, and renting a machine too small for a job is the user's own sizing. What this round does instead is show the machine's free space on the entry, so the number is there to be read. *(From that scenario's round 1 Verdict, Rejected 2, 2026-08-09.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it out.** [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) is explicit that Smoozy does not check that the user's file, or their choice of machine, is set up sensibly, and renting a machine too small for a job is the user's own sizing under Rule 8. The free space is shown on the entry to be read. Nothing was written into any spec.

- **Whether a machine's hardware can be read over a connection.** The [Servers spec](../Specs/Servers.md) now shows each machine's GPUs — how many, which, and how much memory each holds — and the machine's own memory, and nothing in this project confirms any of it can be read over an ordinary connection. Label: **unresolved**, cited in that spec's [Sources](../Specs/Sources/Servers.sources.md). Settle at Architecture, together with the two related reads already parked: a GPU's **live** memory use, which the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) raises, and the **free disk space** the [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) shows for a machine's disk. If a machine cannot be read this way, that is a hole in whichever promise leans on it, to be fixed there.

  **Due: done, 2026-08-20, and the three reads closed in three different documents.** The **free disk space** clause is `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 1.4 L11–L13, on Fact 28, which came back answered: it is **a choice of mount rather than a reading**, taken per-path against the folder being browsed, the available figure rather than the free one, never summed or totalled — the plain reading returned 51 filesystems, 48 of them memory-backed, and one of the three that were not was the host's own disk showing space the rental may not use. The **machine's hardware** clause closed with `Servers`, whose §15 fixes the reading's shape and hands it on, and the **card's live memory** clause with `Queue & History`. **Nothing here turned out to be a hole in a promise**, which is what this item was armed against.

- **Uploading, which is the real bottleneck and is treated lightly.** The conversation the Creator asked for was held on 2026-07-26, and every product question it raised is settled — in the [File Browser spec](../Specs/File%20Browser.md) and the [Whole app spec](../Specs/Whole%20app.md). What is recorded below is where each landed, and the one question left for a later stage.

  **Settled there.** A half-sent file was being counted as already present by the already-exists check, which compares by name and place — so *skip existing* would have passed over it and called the upload finished. That hole is closed. And the promise that nothing already sent crosses the wire again now covers **part of a file**, not only whole files, so a large packed `.blend` interrupted near the end is carried on rather than started from nothing. It is conditional: Smoozy carries on only where it can tell the file has not changed, and sends that file again from the beginning where it cannot, so the promise gives way rather than joining two versions of a file together. How that is done, and whether a transfer can be carried on part-way at all, are Architecture's and are labelled unresolved in the spec's [Sources](../Specs/Sources/File%20Browser.sources.md). Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-26).

  **Also settled, in the [Whole app spec](../Specs/Whole%20app.md).** Two limits on how fast Smoozy uses the user's connection, one up and one down. And the question of whether an arrival is checked as intact, which turned out not to need a promise of its own: a file arriving damaged is the same harm as one arriving half-finished, so the existing promise — a file is whole and correct, or plainly not there — was widened to cover both directions and both causes. Whether keeping it needs any work beyond what a transfer already does is Architecture's.

  **Also settled in File Browser:** what lands when the user saves over a file while it is still going up. Smoozy stops that transfer and says the file changed while it was being sent, then offers to send the new version rather than doing it unasked — a save made mid-edit can be a state the user would never render, and the change means nothing already sent can be reused. Judging that a file has changed is the same knowledge the carry-on rule needs to judge that it has not, so one Architecture question serves both.

  **Left for later.** Whether anything is squeezed on the way is invisible to the user — it only makes transfers faster or it does not — so it is **Architecture's**, not a Specs question, and it is the only part of this thread still waiting. Whether the same assets must cross the wire again for the next machine is not open at all: Smoozy never moves files between two servers, so each new machine is uploaded to from the user's own computer. **When:** the squeezing question, at Architecture.

  **Due:** ~~at Architecture, for the one part still open — whether anything is squeezed on the way.~~ **MET 2026-08-31.** It was filed as Row 514 that morning — the sweep that found it also found that this half had been split from its sibling and that only the sibling was carried — and answered the same day at `Preparation/Architecture/Server protocol and transfers.md` **S31**. **Nothing is squeezed, in either direction, at either layer.** The connection is left at the transport's own client default of off, on that transport's own manual's advice that compression *"will only slow down things on fast networks"*, and against material that is mostly compressed before it starts. **The file body is never compressed and that half is a requirement rather than a preference**: a resume checkpoint is a byte offset into the staged file, so a compressed body would make a resumed offset mean something different from a fresh one — the interaction this thread's own *when* was worried about. The whole thread is now closed.


- **Where a frame preview's small copy is made — an Architecture question with a Blender answer.** [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md) leaves how those copies are made to Architecture. One option is worth weighing there before anything else: making them **on the server**, where a Blender is already installed. It answers two problems at once — whether Smoozy can open a heavy image format at all, and what look to show it in, since Blender knows the look the file was rendered under. It costs a little of the machine's time, and it keeps the promise that only a small copy crosses the connection. Raised by an agent and agreed by the Creator on 2026-07-28 as worth recording, not as a decision. **This gained a second reason on 2026-07-29**: the [Telegram spec](../Specs/Telegram.md) has the bot make small copies **on the machine** and send those to a phone, because nothing heavy should cross the connection and Smoozy may not be running to make them. So one of the two ways of doing this is now written into a spec, and whether the machine-side copies and the ones the app holds on the user's computer are the same work done once or two separate things is Architecture's to settle. **When:** at Architecture. *(From the compositor conversation, 2026-07-28.)*

  **Due: done. The answer is one piece of work, not two**, and the option this item said was worth weighing is the one that was taken. `Preparation/Architecture/Job files and outputs.md` 8.4 B22 settles it: **the still copies are produced once on the rented machine and both readers read the same set** — `Queue & History` fetches and holds copies and produces none, and `Telegram` reads the same derivatives. `Preparation/Architecture/Telegram.md` Section 11 keeps that by citation and adds the one case the rule leaves: a derivative legitimately cleared is **re-produced through the same route** rather than through a second one. **The moving picture and the contact sheet are a separate class** and were the part still owed; `Telegram.md` Section 12 closes them, and the contact sheet turns out to compose no image at all — it is a selection of at most ten copies that already exist.

- **Replying to a message from the phone, as a way of answering the job it is about.** Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-12, while watching the Telegram behaviour tests, and **not agreed** — parked under Rule 7 rather than built. Their words: server A sends me a preview and I reply to this message with something. Today the phone offers five things and every one of them is a tap on a message or a request the user types; replying to a particular message is neither.

  **The tests establish that it would work, which is why it is worth parking rather than dismissing.** A reply carries the id and the full text of the message replied to, and a flag saying the bot sent it — so the listening machine can always tell which job the user is answering, even where that job is on a different machine. A tap carries the same routing information by another route, through whatever the sending machine wrote into its own button. Both were run: a reply and a tap on machine ONE's message both arrived at machine TWO and were correctly identified as ONE's. See `Audits/Specs/Telegram/Behaviour tests 2026-08-12/Findings.md`.

  **What is not settled is what a reply would be for**, and that is the whole question. The module's own rule is that the phone offers no action the app does not have, so a reply would have to map onto something Smoozy already does — and the two candidates are the ones already open: what *try it again* should send, which [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md) carries as unresolved, and naming a frame for *send a frame*, which currently has to be typed. A free-text reply that means whatever the user wrote is a different product and is not what was asked for.

  **Due:** at Specs, if the Creator wants it — it would touch Telegram and, if it carries a re-submission, Rendering. Not agreed; nothing is blocked either way.

- **Whether the user can tell Smoozy what a machine costs them per hour.** Raised while settling Telegram (2026-07-29) and **not agreed**, unlike everything else in this section — the Creator asked to think about it and nothing was decided. The case for it: a phone is where somebody weighs whether to let a render carry on, and "$14 so far" makes that decision sharp in a way a progress bar does not. The case against: Smoozy knows no providers and no prices by design, providers bill in their own odd ways, and a confident wrong number about somebody's money is worse than none. The [Telegram spec](../Specs/Telegram.md) takes the cautious route and states it — the pinned message says how long the machine has been up, which is true, and the user multiplies it themselves.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-12. **The Creator ruled it out.** Smoozy knows no providers and no prices by design, providers bill in their own odd ways, and a confident wrong number about somebody's money is worse than none. The cautious route in Telegram stands as the answer. Nothing was written into any spec. If it is ever wanted, it comes back as a whole-app concern rather than Telegram's, since the rate would belong to the server and every module showing elapsed time could use it.

- **What Access owes Architecture.** The `Audits/Specs/Access/Decisions record.md` is written and settles everything the user meets. Two things underneath it are not settled, and both are Architecture's. **How a trial is held against a computer** so that the ordinary tricks — winding the computer's clock back, reinstalling the app — do not earn more time or a second trial. The spec promises the outcome and rests it on the trial being recorded away from the user's own computer; no method is chosen, and it meets the whole-app security concern. Label: **an assumption**, cited in that spec's `Audits/Specs/Access/Decisions record.md`. The spec deliberately claims only that the ordinary tricks fail, not that it cannot be broken at all. And **who handles the sale**: the licence is bought on Smoozy's website through a seller that takes the payment and emails the key, which at the time of writing is **Lemon Squeezy**, and the Creator expects to change it. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-07-29). The spec names no seller on purpose, since what the user gets is the same either way — so this is where the name is written down until Architecture records it properly. *(From writing the Access spec, 2026-07-29, which closed the trial-clock thread parked here on 2026-07-23: the clock now starts when the user asks it to, and an expired trial always leaves open the ways to stop a render and bring finished work home.)*

---

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture.~~ **BOTH HALVES DONE; marked 2026-09-02 at the Architecture stage close.** **How a trial is held against a computer** is `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md` §6. **The record is written by the ordinary check** — there is no separate recording request and no separate moment, so *at the first connection there is* comes true without the desktop having to know which connection is the first. **It is keyed on the computer identity and the store itself refuses a second row**, which was proved rather than assumed: forty simultaneous first-checks for one identity left exactly one row, and the run states what a negative would have looked like. **Winding the clock back is defeated where this item said it had to be** — by the service holding the record and never by anything read on the computer: the desktop's claimed start is recorded and **used only where it is earlier than the service's own recording**, so a wound-back clock can shorten a trial and never lengthen one. **Reinstalling the app is defeated by the identity outliving the installation.** **And who handles the sale** is settled at the same document: Lemon Squeezy still sells and issues the keys, and the component the Creator runs holds the trial record, the slots, the release and the switching off — so changing seller does not move any of that. **The spec's own limit stands unchanged**, and is what makes the promise keepable: somebody with full control of their own computer is past what any app can guarantee. **The facts underneath the first half are now in, answered 2026-08-16** — Facts 11, 12 and 13 in `Preparation/Architecture/Facts.md`, with the evidence in `Preparation/Architecture/The computer itself — its name, its identity, and its clock.md`. They do not choose a method; they bound what any method can claim. In short: **no operating system offers a website-distributed binary a computer identity that its own administrator cannot change**, two computers can carry the same name on all three, and nothing on any of them survives both a reboot and an administrator. Reinstalling the app is defeated easily; winding the clock back has to be defeated by the service holding the record rather than by anything read on the computer. Whoever writes this must not assume otherwise, and the spec's own limit — that somebody with full control of their own computer is past what any app can guarantee — is what makes the promise still keepable.


  Superseded by the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04; recorded 2026-10-26. Paid-layer work is withdrawn. Earlier rulings and closures remain as history.

- **Architecture: render-start setups surviving a restart.** The requirement that the redirect, the GPU availability, the animation rule, and the dependency remap all survive a re-run with Smoozy closed is an Architecture concern. Parked here so it is not missed. *(From the Rendering spec round 1, M28; the remap added 2026-07-28 when the Dependency Upload & Remap spec was written.)*

---

  ~~**Due:** at Architecture.~~ **DONE 2026-08-19; marked 2026-09-02 at the Architecture stage close.** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` **L29** answers it: **everything the launch needs is on the machine before the job exists, and a re-run reads it rather than recomposing it.** The chain holds with nobody connected and Smoozy closed — the immutable submission manifest (the version, the scene, the frame range, the settings payload, the remap reference, the map of where the file was read before submitting, and the job output folder's path), then the remap material in the asset area, then the launch material composed fresh on the machine per attempt from exactly those inputs. **A re-run reads the manifest; it never composes one.** **So all four of the things this item names survive** — the redirect, the cards, the animation rule and the dependency remap are carried by the manifest and the asset area, and **none of them is something Smoozy applies by hand at render time**, which is what would have made a restart lose them. That is Row 15.

- **Architecture: the transfer-priority order.** The File Browser spec puts uploads into the same per-server pool as downloads and invokes auto-download's priority from the Download spec, but never states the order among auto-download, manual download, and upload. This is unresolved for Architecture. *(From the File Browser spec round 1, M13.)*

  **Due: done, 2026-08-20.** `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 6.2 O4–O7 answers Row 189, which is the one `[PENDING]` in that spec naming Architecture. **Five declared classes in one order** — auto-download, manual download, dependency-upload, upload, then the preview fetch `Queue & History` P6 had already declared below every user-asked class. **The top pair was already settled at Specs and is contained rather than re-decided**: [Download](../Specs/Download.md) states *"Auto-download transfers take priority over manual download transfers in the server's transfer queue."*, Creator-stated 2026-07-22, quoted verbatim as Row 207's own promise — so this item's *nothing anywhere states the order* was true of the three-way order and not of that pair, and the architecture declares the rest around it. **The two upload classes sit below both downloads** on the eleven-to-twelve-times-faster direction, with dependency-upload above upload because its rows gate a submission. **The order is a preference at each admission decision and never a gate**, per `Server protocol and transfers` T10: a class that has had work waiting throughout the last N grants without receiving one takes the next, N being the count of declared classes, **with the tie among classes qualifying at the same decision broken by rank**, so a continuously waiting class receives a grant within **at most 2N−1 grants at that server** — nine for the five declared classes — **and the guarantee scales as later documents declare classes in**. *(This entry read "guaranteed a grant in every N+1" until 2026-08-20; the Round 1 close corrected that bound in `File Browser` O5 and in Row 189's answer, and this file was the one place left carrying it.)* `Download`'s Row 207 confirms the settled pair by citation and declares of its own only its two classes' detail and Row 206's ceiling. The moment the order actually bites is the repair — several kinds resuming across several servers at once — and O8 states that the desktop-side admission point holds the same order across servers. **The first draft reversed the settled pair and the before-audit gate caught it; the omission was the runner's**, that half having been named neither in Row 189's constraint paragraph nor in the writing brief, and it was ruled a defect rather than an escalation because nothing prevented declaring the order the spec's way.

- **The next round of Blender Research — the whole question set, in one place.** Assembled with the Creator on 2026-08-06, after the first twelve topics closed and were carried into the specs. Every topic below has its questions drafted blind and ready for a sheet. A researcher-runner opening this file should read this item and the two that follow it, and nothing else in this section.

  **1. What a render reports about what it actually used.** This is the one to do first. Three broken promises share a single root: a broken graphics driver, a missing render engine and a missing add-on each produce a clean render of the wrong thing and report success. If any of it is knowable, three specs get their promises back together; if none of it is, three specs have to say plainly that Smoozy cannot tell.

  1. When a render has finished, what can be learned about which parts of the computer it used?
  2. When a render has finished, what can be learned about which render engine produced it?
  3. When a render has finished, what can be learned about which add-ons were in use while it ran?
  4. What does a render do when it is asked for something the computer or the installed Blender cannot provide, and what does it report?
  5. Where can each of those things be learned from — what a render prints while it works, what it leaves on disk, or what can be asked afterwards?
  6. At what point can each of those things be learned — before a render starts, while it runs, or only once it has finished?

  Question 6 was added last and matters most in practice. The first five all ask about a finished render, and by then the machine has already been paid for — a six-hour render on the processor costs the same whether the mistake is discovered afterwards or not.

  **Decide before this runs, not after:** *nothing* is a real possible answer to all six. What the three specs say in that case is a product decision, and settling it in advance is cheaper than discovering it and reopening them.

  **2. What can be done to reduce what a render demands of the computer.** Raised by the Creator, who named a group of settings that caps texture resolution and does other work on a scene to make a render cheaper. **The feature is deliberately not named in the questions** — Smoozy will outlive the versions we can see, and a question naming a feature dies the day it is renamed.

  1. What settings, if any, change how much a render demands of the computer it runs on?
  2. For each of them, what does it change about what the render produces?
  3. Which of them can be settled at the moment a render is started from outside Blender, and which only by changing the file?
  4. Do any of them belong to a particular render engine rather than to every engine?
  5. Can their values be read out of a file?

  **A warning for whoever writes this sheet.** It overlaps the **Settings** topic, which already answered which of a render's settings can be imposed at the start of a render. The [Method](../Blender%20Research/Method.md) is explicit about the danger — add-ons were kept out of the dependency questions for exactly this reason — so the sheet must say which part is new, or two topics will answer the same thing differently. Expect samples and resolution to come back among the answers; trimming the question to exclude them would leak what is being fished for, and the noise is cheaper than the leak.

  **3. Whether anything a render does can wait for a person.** One question, and nothing anywhere has asked it. On a machine with nobody at it, a render that is waiting looks exactly like a render that is working — same clock, same bill, no output, and nothing to tell them apart. Existing topics cover running with no display and cover failing; none covers simply sitting there.

  1. Can anything a render does wait for a person, and what happens on a computer with nobody at it?

  **4. Add-ons, three more.**

  1. Does installing an add-on disturb a render already running on the same computer?
  2. Once an add-on is installed and switched on, what more can be learned about whether it will work?
  3. Does an add-on set up for one Blender work in another on the same computer, and what decides it?

  The third matters because the user picks which Blender renders each job, and an add-on lives in a folder named for the version — so a version change is already known to leave add-ons behind. Whether one would even work if carried across is unasked.

  **5. Two questions already written and never answered.** **The compositor 8** — how far a picture can be followed through colour work and a merging node. **File output nodes 14** — whether what decides a node writes can change frame to frame. Both are in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md). **Reuse them word for word.** Rewriting a blind question that has been sitting in the register is how a hint gets in.

  **6. Five things that want tests, not questions.** Each is already asked and already marked unanswered in its own comparison, so writing a new question would only duplicate it. What is owed is a hand test: whether rendering one scene pulls only that scene's references (**Dependencies 14**); whether every kind of recorded path is covered by the read-and-write machinery (**Reading a file 5**); whether a reference held only inside a node group, a driver or a custom property appears in the map of what uses what (**Reading a file 7**); whether names inside a file are compared case-sensitively (**Dependencies 8**); and a full disk on Linux, which was written out and not run because the machine was shared.

  **7. Files authored on Windows.** Smoozy never renders on Windows, so how Blender behaves there does not matter. What matters is what a file **made** on Windows looks like when it lands on a Linux render machine — and the existing Dependencies questions already cover that. **This needs no new questions, only material**: a Windows machine's whole job is to save a handful of deliberately awkward files and hand them over, with every render happening on the Linux machine afterwards.

  Worth authoring, in rough order of value: a texture referenced with capitalisation that is wrong but works on Windows anyway; a name using a combining accent, which is the case the existing non-Latin test explicitly could not raise; a texture on a different drive from the `.blend`; a texture on a network share; a texture beside the file and one a folder deeper, both saved relative, to see which slash lands in the file; a name using a character one system allows and the other does not; a linked `.blend` carrying its own references; a font; and an output path pointing somewhere Windows-shaped. At least one of them saved from Blender's own interface rather than a script, which closes a separate gap — every file tested so far was saved by a windowless Blender.

  **And one file made the way an artist would actually make one, with none of that list in mind, as a control.** The list above is the cases somebody thought of, which is precisely how the geometry-node hole happened: the dependency test file held seven kinds because seven kinds were imagined.

  **Where each of these goes, agreed with the Creator on 2026-08-06.** Three new topics, and the rest added to topics that already exist.

  - **What actually ran** — new topic, holding set 1. It cuts across *The machine*, *Settings* and *Add-ons* rather than sitting inside any of them, because the question is not what Blender does but what it can be made to say about what it did.
  - **What a render demands** — new topic, holding set 2. Its sheet must say which part is new against *Settings*, which already answered which settings can be imposed at the start of a render.
  - **Node setups** — new topic, holding the two questions about what a procedural setup makes a file point at and what a linked one records. It is deliberately **not** folded into *Dependencies*, and the reason is the whole point: Dependencies' test file was built to hold seven kinds, none of them a node, so a researcher answering there would reach for the same file and find the same seven things. A separate topic gets a test file built for nodes. **The boundary, stated so the two do not answer each other differently:** a researcher on *Dependencies* does not fold node setups in, and a researcher on *Node setups* does not re-answer what an ordinary reference is.
  - **The machine** gains the question about waiting for a person. It already owns running with no screen and with no display server, and this is the next question in that line.
  - **Dependencies** gains the two write-then-read questions — what use a file makes of the places it records for writing, and what a render does when a cache or bake is absent. Their neighbours are already there, as questions 10 and 12.
  - **Reading a file** gains the machinery question — by what means every recorded location can be listed and rewritten, and what each of those means covers. Its own question 5 is the neighbour, and the new one must be worded so it does not simply repeat it.
  - **Add-ons** gains three.

  Every one of those additions to an existing topic takes a number in the register **and** a row in the *questions added after a version was finished* section of [Coverage](../Blender%20Research/Coverage.md). Six of the seven topics above have already closed for all four versions, so every question added to them is owed by four versions from the moment it is written.

  **Part 7 is done, 2026-08-26 — the material exists.** A Windows 11 desktop machine was rented and all ten cases were authored on it: the wrongly-capitalised reference, the combining accent, a texture on a second drive, one on a network share, two relative paths at different depths, a name Windows takes and Linux will not, a two-deep library chain, a font, a Windows-shaped output path, and the control file made with none of the list in mind. They sit at `/Users/artist/Documents/Windows-authored files/`, each case in its own folder, with `How these were made.md` beside them recording exactly how each was built and what could not be done. **The separate gap is closed too**: one file was saved from Blender's own window rather than by a windowless Blender, which no file this project has ever tested was.

  **Two limits on the material, stated so nobody reads more into it.** The network-share case had both ends on **one machine**, so it shows what such a path looks like and not what crossing a real network does. The second drive was a virtual disk given a drive letter rather than a physical one.

  **One thing the material shows that is not yet a fact about Blender.** Reading the files here, a relative path written on Windows carries a **backslash inside it** — `//sub\tex_b.png` for a texture, `//lib\child.blend` for a library — and a windowed save produced a file about 70 KB larger than a windowless one. **These are this project's own observations of ten files on one machine, not claims about Blender**, and they are fenced here exactly as the Architecture stage's machine observations were on 2026-08-18. **No new question is needed** — this item already established that the existing *Dependencies* questions cover what a Windows-made file does, and that what was owed was material rather than questions. What these observations become is settled when the files are rendered on the Linux machine, which is what they were made for.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-26. Parts 1 to 6 were asked and answered on 2026-08-07; part 7's material was authored on 2026-08-26. **What remains is not this item's**: rendering these files on a Linux machine belongs to the hand tests.

- **What a procedural node setup drags in, and whether a place a file writes to is read back — the research never asked either.** Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-06 and checked the same day against the comparisons and the specs.

  **No test file in the whole Dependencies topic ever held a geometry node.** The test file was built to carry seven kinds — an image, a sound, a font, a volume, an OSL script, an IES profile and a text file — so anything reached through a node setup could not have appeared. What the research says about node setups is documentation-reading with nothing behind it: import nodes are documented in three versions' findings, absent from the fourth's, and tested nowhere, and the comparison marks that a silence rather than a difference. The Creator names caches, bakes, imported meshes and fonts as things a node setup can hold.

  **The second half is worse, because it already cost a promise.** Caches and bakes appear both in the list of what a file points at and in the list of places it writes to, and nothing reconciles them. The [Dependency Upload & Remap spec](../Specs/Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md) had read them as write-only and did not send them, which loses a bake the render needed and yields a picture of a different simulation at full price. That is fixed in the spec — they are sent, on the reasoning that wasting bandwidth is the cheaper mistake — but the fact behind it is still nobody's.

  Five questions, drafted blind and ready for a sheet:

  1. What files can a geometry node setup inside a `.blend` cause that file to point at?
  2. What use does a `.blend` make of the places it records for writing, besides writing to them?
  3. What does a render do when a cache or a bake that a file records is absent?
  4. When a `.blend` links a node setup from another `.blend`, what does the linking file record about the files that setup uses, and how are they reached?
  5. By what means can every location recorded in a `.blend` be listed and rewritten, and what does each of those means cover?

  Three existing questions also want tests rather than rewording, and all three are already in the register: whether every kind of recorded path is covered by the read-and-write machinery (**Reading a file 5**), whether a reference held only inside a node group, a driver or a custom property appears in the map of what uses what (**Reading a file 7**), and whether names inside a file are compared case-sensitively (**Dependencies 8**). Each is marked unanswered in its own comparison.

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-07. Question 1 and question 4 became the *Node setups* topic; questions 2 and 3 became Dependencies 17 and 18; question 5 became Reading a file 9. The three older questions that wanted tests rather than rewording got them. Question 2 was worth protecting: it found that a missing cache or bake changes the picture and is announced nowhere, exit 0.

- **Nothing in the research method asks whether a test could have failed.** Raised on 2026-08-06 by the Creator, on noticing that one question survived several passes of research and of checking and came back settled-as-unsettled each time, for a reason none of those passes named.

  The question was whether rendering one scene of a file pulls only that scene's references. The test watched whether Blender **complained** about another scene's missing file. It never did — and that is not evidence, because "did not complain" and "did not load it" are different things, and no number of repeats tells them apart. In one version the test carried no information at all, since that version's Cycles never names a missing image under any circumstances.

  **The point is what the machinery did with it.** A researcher wrote the test, an audit read it, a citation check re-ran it, and a comparison check ruled on it — and all four passed a test whose only possible outcome was the one it got. Every one of those steps asks whether the answer follows from the evidence. None asks whether the evidence could have come out the other way.

  The fix is probably one line in the method: a test that cannot fail is not a test, and whoever writes one says what a negative result would have looked like. It is cheap, it is mechanical, and it would have caught this. Weigh it against the usual cost — one more thing for every step to hold — before adding it. *(Creator-raised, 2026-08-06.)*

  **Due:** done, 2026-08-06. Added to the Method and to the Hand Tests brief before the round started, and it earned itself at once: four tests in this round could not have come out the other way, and rebuilding one of them reversed its finding. The cost the item asked to be weighed turned out to be small — one line per test entry.

- **What a machine losing power outright leaves behind — the one case nothing has tested.** Raised while carrying the Frames findings into the [Queue & History spec](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md), 2026-08-06.

  Every other way a render ends has been watched, and they all agree: nothing is written for the frame in flight. That agreement is what makes an automatic re-run safe, because a re-run skips the frames already on disk and a half-written frame would be skipped as though it were finished. **The power-cut case was tried on no computer in any version.** It was offered on the Creator's Mac and declined, and it cannot be done to a machine reached over the network — which is every machine Smoozy renders on.

  It matters more than the other silences in the research, because it is precisely the case the re-run was built for: a machine that goes down and comes back. Two things are known and neither closes it — a file existing is not proof the frame finished writing, and a machine that has been restarted leaves an output folder holding more files than there are pictures, the extra ones zero bytes with ordinary names. Whether an abrupt power loss can leave a frame file that is neither empty nor whole is unknown, and if it can, the re-run keeps it.

  It wants a hand test on a machine somebody can pull the plug on, which is not a rented one. Recorded in that spec's sources file as unresolved.

  **Due:** as a hand test, whenever a machine that can be switched off at the wall is available. Nothing schedules it, and the spec states the gap rather than assuming the safe answer.

  **Marked 2026-09-02, at the Architecture stage close: this is the Creator's own act and no ruling reaches it.** It is the same family as verification-round item 1 above, and it moves the same way. **The 2026-08-31 ruling — no further Blender research and no further rented machine — does not touch it**, because what it wants is not a rental: it is free virtualisation software on the Creator's own Mac, so a guest machine can be killed at the hypervisor without touching the Mac's own power. **The honest half-answer is worth knowing before anybody spends the evening:** a virtual machine does not remove a physical drive's write cache, which is half of what this case turns on — so it closes the *machine stops dead* half and leaves the *what was still in the drive* half open. **Nothing schedules it and nothing can**, and the spec states the gap rather than assuming the safe answer, which is the whole protection here.

- **Telling the user a job spilled, and what it cost them.** Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-06 while the research was being carried into the specs, and agreed the same day. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-06).

  The research changed what the graphics-card memory case actually looks like, and the change is the whole reason for this item. A card filling up **is not a failure**. Blender fills the card, takes the rest from the machine's own memory, writes the picture and exits 0 — nothing printed, no error, no warning, the same in all four versions. The only sign is the cost: about ten times longer for twice the data, at the same size and the same sample count. See `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Reports.md`, question 6.

  **Narrowed 2026-08-30 by the cross-sweep at the stage close, and the narrowing matters to what this item asks for.** The paragraph above is a fact about **one ratio of the machine's own memory to the card's** — the runs behind it were 16 GB cards behind 197 GB of system memory, and *Reports* says in as many words that the *out of memory* message was never reached because that machine had so much to spill into. **Two other topics record the same act ending the render.** On a machine with about 49 GB, running the host's memory out is a silent kill in 4.5 and 5.0 and a loud abort in 5.1 and 5.2. On a machine with about 32 GB behind a 10 GB card, *What a render demands 7* had **every run of one rendering method killed by the system with no frame written, in all four versions**, before the card was anywhere near full. **So the same act has at least three endings depending on how much system memory sits behind the card, and nothing anywhere joins the three** — the one a builder reads is the silent expensive success. **Nothing here is settled and no side is chosen**; it is written down because this item's whole premise is the first of the three. The record is `Audits/Blender Research/Cross-sweep/2026-08-30 — Conditions — Claude Opus 5.md`, candidate 1, and `2026-08-30 — Absolutes — Claude Opus 5.md`, candidate 6.

  So on a rented machine the failure an artist knows from their own computer mostly does not arrive as a job to try again. It arrives as a job that finished, looks perfectly normal in history, and cost ten times what it should have — on a machine billed by the hour. Nothing in the specs would tell the user that happened. **On a smaller machine it may instead arrive as a render that died**, which is a different thing to tell the user and a different thing to detect.

  What is wanted is that Smoozy says so. What it can actually be told from is not settled and is the work: Blender prints nothing about it, so the signal is elapsed time against what the job should have taken, or the machine's own reading of the card, or both. Whether either can be read over a connection is already parked with the other hardware reads.

  **Due:** at Specs, for whether the user is told at all and where — it touches Queue & History, which owns what an entry shows, and Design, which owns how it reads. The how-it-is-detected half is Architecture's, with the other hardware reads.

- **Simplify — a group of settings nobody has asked Blender about.** Raised by the Creator on 2026-08-06. Their words: there is a group of settings in Blender called Simplify; it can cap the resolution of textures and do other useful things with the scene on the fly to make it render faster. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-06).

  **The research has nothing on it, and this is a hole in the questions rather than a gap in the answers.** No question in the [Question register](../Blender%20Research/Question%20register.md) mentions Simplify, no comparison covers it, and it appears in the findings only by accident — as a panel name in photographs of the Render Properties tab, listed among its neighbours with nothing established about what it does. So it was never asked, in any of the twelve topics.

  Why it matters here. Every other lever the specs have for making a job cheaper changes what the user gets — fewer samples, a smaller resolution. If Simplify can make a render cheaper by capping what a scene loads rather than by shrinking what it produces, it is a different kind of lever from anything Render Settings currently offers, and it would bear directly on the two threads above: what is worth changing after a failure, and what to do about a job that spilled.

  Two pieces of work, in order. **A research topic first**, since nothing can be decided without it — what Simplify controls, whether it can be settled at the moment a render is started or only by changing the file, whether it differs between versions, and what it costs the picture. Then **the product decision**, which is Render Settings' and probably the Creator's alone: whether Smoozy offers any of it, and whether an artist would want it offered.

  **Due:** the research half was done on 2026-08-07, as the topic *What a render demands* — the feature is named in none of its questions, on purpose. **The product half was taken on 2026-08-10 and it was taken conservatively, so the Creator still has the real call.** Render Settings now says plainly that **none** of Blender's other cost settings is offered, and why, in two groups. The eleven that leave the picture untouched pixel for pixel — thread count, tile size, tiling, the settings governing how a scene is prepared for tracing, keeping prepared data between frames, high-quality normals — are not offered because there is nothing in them for an artist and Smoozy has no basis for choosing a value. **The group the Creator actually asked about, including the texture-resolution cap, is left off *for now* rather than ruled out**: it changes the picture, reaches every engine, can be read out of a file and can be imposed on a render, so it could be offered — and whether an artist wants that trade offered by a render farm rather than made in their own file is a judgment about how artists work and is the Creator's alone. Labelled in Render Settings as Smoozy's decision so it can be pulled the moment the Creator wants any of it.

  **Done, 2026-08-12. The Creator took the real call, and it went both ways at once.** Simplify is **not** on the ordinary settings page — Smoozy is a render farm, the artist prepares the file in advance, and offering to cut their scene down at every submission is holding their hand where they did not ask. It **is** offered on a job that has already failed, under Blender's own name, exposing Max Subdivision, Child Particles and Texture Limit. It lives in [Rendering](../Specs/Rendering.md), and [Render Settings](../Specs/Render%20Settings.md) now points there instead of saying the group waits on a ruling. Blender 5.2's own texture-resolution setting is left out because the other three versions have not got it, and the two culling settings because they drop whole objects out of frame.

  **One thing is promised on thinner evidence than the rest, deliberately, and it is worth knowing where.** Only Simplify's switch and its subdivision cap were watched being imposed as a render starts. The texture cap was written into a file and read back at the value set, never imposed at the start of a render with the result looked at; the child-particle cap was neither. The Creator ruled on 2026-08-12 that all three are promised as they stand rather than held for another test, so if either misbehaves it surfaces at Build.

  ~~**Due:** at **Build**, as one thing to check early — that the texture cap and the child-particle cap really do reach a render when handed to Blender as it starts. It is one run per setting and it would close the only inferred half of a promise the user can see.~~ **FIRED 2026-09-04, at Build's step 6 close, because step 7 is the step that can carry it.** Simplify lives in `Rendering`, and `Rendering` composes the launch material — so **step 7 is the first moment there is anything to hand the two settings to a render with**, and *early at Build* means here. **It is still one run per setting**, and it still closes the only inferred half of a promise the user can see: only Simplify's switch and its subdivision cap were ever watched being imposed as a render starts, the texture cap was written into a file and read back but never imposed, and the child-particle cap was neither. **Due:** step 7, with the render path.

### A locked user's held job sits forever on a machine they are paying for

**Parked from Architecture's Round 1 on `Telegram`, 2026-08-19.** An auditor raised it, did not file it, and the consolidator dropped it as a **want** — past where that document's job stops — so it comes here to be weighed on its own merits rather than as a fault.

**The moment.** A job on a rented machine stops and waits for the user to say *go on* — the first frame is rendered and it wants a yes, or a dependency wants an answer. Before they answer, the licence lock comes down: the machine refuses the two starting-work actions, and *go on* is one of them. **Nothing anywhere expires a held job.** So the job sits, held, on a machine billing by the hour, and neither the phone nor the desk can release it until the lock lifts.

**Why it is a want and not a defect.** `Telegram` states the machine-side refusal and cites `Access` for which actions a lock applies to; the list is `Access`'s and that document is unwritten. Nothing in `Telegram` is wrong, and no promise it makes is broken — the user is told the refusal plainly. What is missing is a decision nobody has been asked to take.

**What it would take.** Two candidates and neither is architecture's to pick. Either the lock's list excludes releasing a job that is already held — on the ground that a lock is meant to stop new work rather than to strand work already paid for — or the product accepts that a locked user's held job waits, and says so where the user can read it. **The first is a change to what `Access` promises and is the Creator's.**

**CLOSED 2026-08-25. The Creator took the first candidate.** A held job's *go on* is off the lock's list: it is on `Preparation/Specs/Access.md`'s open enumeration and `Preparation/Architecture/Access.md` P3 no longer carries an entry for it. **Their ground:** the job was submitted, uploaded and started before the lock came down and is sitting on a machine billing by the hour, so refusing the yes strands work already paid for rather than preventing new work.

**It was ruled the other way first, on 2026-08-20, and that reading is worth keeping.** The second candidate was taken then — the held job waits and the waiting is stated where the user can read it — on the ground that the spec's own open list already carries the held job's ending, so a locked user could always stop it, refuse it, cancel it, and bring home what it produced, and was therefore never stranded. That remains true; the 2026-08-25 ruling is about the cost of the machine that keeps billing while the user decides, which the 2026-08-20 reading weighed and the Creator has now weighed differently.

**What it cost, recorded rather than absorbed:** a locked user can cause a render to begin on a machine, which submitting otherwise forbids. `Access` P8 states it. **Nothing here is owed any more.**
